Twisted correlations of the divisor function via discrete averages of SL 2 ⁡ ( ℝ ) \operatorname{SL}_{2}({\mathbb{R}}) Poincaré series
Lasse Grimmelt, Jori Merikoski

Abstract
. We prove a theorem that allows one to count solutions to determinant equations twisted by a periodic weight with high uniformity in the modulus. It is obtained by using spectral methods of SL 2 ⁡ ( ℝ ) \operatorname{SL}_{2}({\mathbb{R}}) automorphic forms to study Poincaré series over congruence subgroups. By keeping track of interactions between multiple orbits we get advantages over the widely used sums of Kloosterman sums techniques. We showcase this with applications to correlations of the divisor functions twisted by periodic functions and the fourth moment of Dirichlet L L -functions on the critical line.



2020 Mathematics Subject Classification
11N37, 11F72, 11N75 primary, 11M06 secondary

1.
Introduction

The
Kloostermania
techniques introduced by Deshouillers and Iwaniec
[
8
]
, building on the works of Bruggeman
[
3
]
and Kuznetsov
[
19
]
, have produced remarkable applications. This is evidenced by
[
2
,
22
,
27
,
32
]
, among many other works. In this paper we introduce a new approach that allows us to better exploit certain geometric and arithmetic aspects. Inspired by the works of Duke, Friedlander, and Iwaniec
[
11
]
as well as Bruggeman and Motohashi
[
4
]
, our method focuses on the present large symmetry under the action of a congruence subgroup of
\operatorname{SL}_{2}({\mathbb{Z}})
.

For a concrete example, consider the determinant equation

(1.1)

\displaystyle ad-bc=1\quad\text{with divisibility conditions}\quad q_{1}|b,\,\,q_{2}|d,

where
q_{1},q_{2}
are fixed nonzero integers and

a,b,c,d
are integer variables. This can be seen as variant of a correlation sum of the divisor function
\sum_{n}d(n)d(n+1)
. The standard approach for counting the number of solutions up to a given height to (
1.1
) is to apply the Poisson summation formula. This reduces matters to bounding sums of Kloosterman sums at the pair of cusps
(\infty,1/q_{1})
for the Hecke congruence subgroup
\Gamma_{0}(q_{1}q_{2})
. In many situations the black-box results developed by Deshouillers and Iwaniec
[
8
]
are sufficient for this task.

The approach we take here is to more immediately make use of the underlying congruence subgroup symmetry. Arranging the solutions to (
1.1
) into a set of matrices

\displaystyle\mathcal{M}=\bigg\{\matrixquantity(\lx@physics@matrix a & b \\c & d \endlx@physics@matrix)\in\operatorname{SL}_{2}({\mathbb{Z}}):\,q_{1}|b,\,q_{2}|d\bigg\},

this set has an action by the congruence subgroup

(1.2)

\displaystyle\Gamma=\Gamma_{2}(q_{1},q_{2}):=\bigg\{\matrixquantity(\lx@physics@matrix a & b \\c & d\endlx@physics@matrix)\in\operatorname{SL}_{2}({\mathbb{Z}}):\,q_{1}|b,\,\,q_{2}|c\bigg\},

that is, for all
\gamma\in\Gamma
we have
\gamma\mathcal{M}=\mathcal{M}
. This allows us to use spectral methods directly to count the number of solutions to (
1.1
), instead of stepping via Kloosterman sums. The group
\Gamma_{2}(q_{1},q_{2})
is a conjugate of the standard Hecke congruence subgroup
\Gamma_{0}(q_{1}q_{2})=\Gamma_{2}(1,q_{1}q_{2})
.

The direct approach turns out to have an advantage in the case that the action of
\Gamma
on a set
\mathcal{M}
has a large number of orbits. This naturally occurs if one replaces the congruence conditions
q_{1}|b,\,q_{2}|d
by a more general left
\Gamma
-invariant function
\alpha(\smallmatrixquantity(\lx@physics@smallmatrix a &b \\ c& d\endlx@physics@smallmatrix))
. In this case we are able to keep track of the distribution of
\Gamma\backslash\mathcal{M}
inside the ambient space
\Gamma\backslash\operatorname{SL}_{2}({\mathbb{R}})
as well as exploit cancellations in
\alpha
. We even allow
\mathcal{M}\subseteq\operatorname{SL}_{2}({\mathbb{R}})
to have non-integer entries, provided that
\Gamma
acts on
\mathcal{M}
with finitely many orbits.

We now formalise concept of an invariant weight. For functions
f:\operatorname{GL}_{2}({\mathbb{R}})\to{\mathbb{C}}
and
g,h\in\operatorname{GL}_{2}({\mathbb{R}})
define the left translation operator
l_{h}:f(g)\mapsto f(hg)
.

Definition 1

(Automorphic function)
.

For any subgroup
\Gamma
of
\operatorname{SL}_{2}({\mathbb{R}})
and for any left
\Gamma
-invariant subset
\mathcal{M}\subseteq\operatorname{GL}_{2}({\mathbb{R}})
we define the set of
\Gamma
-automorphic functions on
\mathcal{M}
by

\displaystyle\mathcal{A}(\Gamma\backslash\mathcal{M}):=\{\alpha:\mathcal{M}\to{\mathbb{C}}:\,l_{\gamma}\alpha=\alpha\,\,\text{for all}\,\,\gamma\in\Gamma\}.

Further, naturally in the context of an analytic counting problem we require that the variables are weighted with a smooth function.

Definition 2

(Smooth dyadically supported function)
.

For
n,J\in{\mathbb{Z}}_{>0}
,
\delta>0,
and
X_{1},\dots,X_{n}\in{\mathbb{R}}_{>0}
define the space of
J
times differentiable dyadically supported functions.

\displaystyle C_{\delta}^{J}(X_{1},\dots,X_{n}):=\{f\in

\displaystyle C^{J}({\mathbb{R}}^{n}):\,f(x_{1},\dots,x_{n})\,\,\text{supported on}\,

\displaystyle(|x_{1}|,\dots,|x_{n}|)\in[X_{1},2X_{1}]\times\cdots\times[X_{n},2X_{n}],

\displaystyle\|\partial_{x_{1}}^{J_{1}}\cdots\partial_{x_{n}}^{J_{n}}f\|_{\infty}\leq\prod_{i\leq n}(\delta X_{i})^{-J_{i}}\quad\text{for all}\quad 0\leq J_{1}+\cdots+J_{n}\leq J\}.

With this we can state a simplified version of our central result that already captures the core new features. We will later give more technical and stronger versions, see Theorems
7.1
and
10.1
.

Theorem 1.1
.

Let
\mathcal{M}\subseteq\operatorname{SL}_{2}({\mathbb{R}})
,
q_{1},q_{2}\in{\mathbb{Z}}_{>0}
and denote
\Gamma=\Gamma_{2}(q_{1},q_{2})
. Let
\mathrm{d}g
denote integration with respect to the Haar measure of the group
\operatorname{SL}_{2}({\mathbb{R}})
. Assume that
\mathcal{M}
is left

\Gamma
-invariant with finitely many orbits
\Gamma\backslash\mathcal{M}
and let
\alpha\in\mathcal{A}(\Gamma\backslash\mathcal{M})
. Let
A,C,D,\delta>0
with
AD>\delta
and let
f\in C^{7}_{\delta}(A,C,D)
. Denote
F:\operatorname{SL}_{2}({\mathbb{R}})\to{\mathbb{C}},\,F(\smallmatrixquantity(\lx@physics@smallmatrix a & b \\ c& d\endlx@physics@smallmatrix)):=f(a,c,d)
. Then for any
\varepsilon>0
we have

\displaystyle\bigg|\sum_{g\in\mathcal{M}}\alpha(g)F(g)-\frac{1}{|\Gamma\backslash\operatorname{SL}_{2}({\mathbb{R}})|}\sum_{\tau\in\Gamma\backslash\mathcal{M}}\alpha(\tau)\int_{\operatorname{SL}_{2}({\mathbb{R}})}F(g)\,\mathrm{d}g\bigg|\ll_{\varepsilon}\delta^{-O(1)}Z^{\varepsilon}\sqrt{AD\,\mathcal{K}\,\mathcal{R}},

where
Z=\max\{A^{\pm 1},C^{\pm 1},D^{\pm 1}\}
,

\displaystyle\mathcal{K}

\displaystyle=\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}g=\smallmatrixquantity(\lx@physics@smallmatrix a & b \\c & d\endlx@physics@smallmatrix)\in\mathcal{M}^{-1}\mathcal{M}\\
|a|+|b|C/D+|c|D/C+|d|\,\leq 6\end{subarray}}\Bigl|\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}\tau\in\Gamma\backslash\mathcal{M}\\
\tau g\in\mathcal{M}\end{subarray}}\alpha(\tau)\overline{\alpha(\tau g)}\Bigr|,\quad\quad\text{and}

\displaystyle\mathcal{R}

\displaystyle=\left(1+(AD)^{2\theta}\bigg(\frac{C}{Aq_{2}}\bigg)^{2\theta}\right)\left(1+\bigg(\frac{C}{Aq_{2}}\bigg)^{1-2\theta}\right)+\frac{A}{Cq_{1}},\quad\theta=7/64.

The error term in Theorem
1.1
consist of three parts
\sqrt{AD}
,
\mathcal{K}
, and
\mathcal{R}
. The first and the third term are directly controlled by the ranges of the variables
a,c,d
. The term
\sqrt{AD}
corresponds to square-root cancellation in terms of the height.

The term
\mathcal{R}
depends on the vertical skewness
C/A
of the ranges as well as spectral data of automorphic forms for the group
\Gamma_{0}(q_{1}q_{2})
, via the spectral large sieve. In particular, denoting
q=q_{1}q_{2}
, it depends on
\theta_{q}:=\max\{0,\operatorname{Re}(\sqrt{1/4-\lambda_{1}(q)})\}
with
\lambda_{1}(q)
denoting the smallest positive eigenvalue for
\Gamma_{0}(q)
. In the statement we have utilised the bound
\theta_{q}\leq 7/64
of Kim and Sarnak
[
17
]
. Assuming the Selberg eigenvalue conjecture
\theta_{q}=0
the term
\mathcal{R}
would simplify to a more symmetrical

\displaystyle\mathcal{R}\ll 1+\frac{C}{Aq_{2}}+\frac{A}{Cq_{1}}.

The term
\mathcal{K}
is different in that it depends more intricately on the underlying geometry of
\Gamma\backslash\mathcal{M}
and arithmetic of the weight
\alpha
. For this reason, an estimate for
\mathcal{K}
needs to be provided separately for each intended application. This should not be seen as a defect – it is what gives the strength of our method for new applications. In general terms,
\mathcal{K}
depends on the horizontal skewness
C/D
and on how well-spaced the set of orbits
\Gamma\backslash\mathcal{M}
(weighted with
\alpha
) is within the fundamental domain
\Gamma\backslash SL_{2}({\mathbb{R}})
. For
C\asymp D
and
\alpha
bounded we expect that

\displaystyle\mathcal{K}\ll|\Gamma\backslash\mathcal{M}|+\frac{|\Gamma\backslash\mathcal{M}|^{2}}{q_{1}q_{2}},

provided that
\Gamma\backslash\mathcal{M}
does not form clusters in the fundamental domain. Furthermore, even if
C/D
or
D/C
are large, we can hope to exploit oscillations or sparseness in the weight
\alpha
to recover this heuristic upper bound. We will sketch this for a concrete example in Section
1.3
and carry out rigorously in Sections
11
and
12
.

1.1.
Applications

We obtain the following applications, which follow from a more technical version of Theorem
1.1
, see Theorem
10.1
. Theorem
1.1
would suffice for these in the range
|h|\ll 1
. For simplicity we restrict to the case of a square-free modulus
q
and
\gcd(h,q)=1
, in the general case for some applications there may be additional losses depending on
q/\mathrm{rad}(q)
and
\gcd(h,q^{\infty})
.

As a first application we consider a general statement concerning sums over
d(n)d(n+h)t(n)
, where
t(n)
is a periodic function. Define for square-free
q
and for
h,r\in{\mathbb{Z}}
with
\gcd(h,q)=1

\displaystyle\omega(r,h;q)

\displaystyle:=\prod_{p|q}\omega(r,h;p),

\displaystyle\omega(r,h;p)

\displaystyle:=\begin{cases}\frac{p-1}{p(p+1)},\quad&\text{if }r(r+h)\not\equiv 0\,\,(\mathrm{mod}\,{q})\\
\frac{2p-1}{p(p+1)},\quad&\text{if }r(r+h)\equiv 0\,\,(\mathrm{mod}\,{q})\end{cases}

and note that
r\mapsto\omega(r,h;q)
defines a probability measure on
{\mathbb{Z}}/q{\mathbb{Z}}
.

Theorem 1.2

(Divisor correlations with a periodic weight)
.

Let
h,q\in{\mathbb{Z}}
with
q
square-free and
\gcd(h,q)=1
. Let
X,\eta>0
and suppose that
-X/2\leq h\leq X^{1+\eta}
. Let
G:{\mathbb{R}}\to{\mathbb{C}}
be a smooth function supported on
[1,2]
that satisfies
G^{(j)}\ll_{j}X^{j\eta}
for all
j\geq 0
. Let
t:{\mathbb{Z}}/q{\mathbb{Z}}\to{\mathbb{C}}
be a function. Then for some binary quadratic polynomial
P_{h}
, only depending on
h
, we have

\displaystyle\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}n\end{subarray}}G(n/X)d(n)d(n+h)t(n)=

\displaystyle\sum_{r\,\,(\mathrm{mod}\,{q})}t(r)\omega(r,h;q)\int G(u/X)P_{h}(\log u,\log(u+h))\,\mathrm{d}u

\displaystyle\hskip 20.0pt+O\bigg(X^{1/2+O(\eta)}q^{1/2}\|t\|_{2}(|h|^{\theta}+(X/q)^{\theta})\bigg),

where
\|t\|_{2}^{2}=\sum_{r\,\,(\mathrm{mod}\,{q})}|t(r)|^{2}
and
\theta=7/64
denotes the best approximation to the Ramanujan-Peterson and Selberg eigenvalue conjectures.

The polynomial
P_{h}
can be explicitly computed, see
[
23
]
, for instance. We now state three corollaries that are all immediate consequences of Theorem
1.2
. The first corollary concerns divisor correlations in a fixed arithmetic progression. The expected main term is given by

(1.3)

\displaystyle\mathrm{MT}_{G}(X,r,h,q)=\omega(r,h;q)\int G(u/X)P_{h}(\log u,\log(u+h))\,\mathrm{d}u

where
P_{h}
is the quadratic polynomial as in Theorem
1.2
.

Corollary 1.3
.

Assume the requirements of Theorem
1.2
, let
r\in{\mathbb{Z}}
and
\mathrm{MT}_{G}(X,r,h,q)
given by (
1.3
). Then we have

\displaystyle\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}n\equiv r\,\,(\mathrm{mod}\,{q})\end{subarray}}G(n/X)d(n)d(n+h)=\mathrm{MT}_{G}(X,r,h,q)+O\bigg(X^{1/2+O(\eta)}q^{1/2}(|h|^{\theta}+(X/q)^{\theta})\bigg).

As far as we aware, this is the first result of this type that goes beyond the range
q\leq X^{1/6-\varepsilon}
that can be obtained by opening one of the divisor functions and using the fact that
d(n)
is well distributed in arithmetic progressions to moduli at most
X^{2/3-\varepsilon}
. Assuming
\theta=0
we obtain the range
q\leq X^{1/3-\varepsilon}
. Unconditionally, the result is non-trivial if

\displaystyle q

\displaystyle\leq X^{\frac{1-2\theta}{3-2\theta}-\varepsilon},\quad\quad|h|\leq X/q

\displaystyle q

\displaystyle\leq X^{1/3-\varepsilon}|h|^{-2\theta/3}\quad\quad X/q<|h|\leq X^{1+\eta}.

Note that by
\theta\leq 7/64

[
17
]
we have
X^{\frac{1-2\theta}{3-2\theta}}\geq X^{0.281\ldots}
and uniformly in the considered
h
range we have
X^{1/3}|h|^{-2\theta/3}\geq X^{0.261\ldots+O(\eta)}
. In particular, in this range we have

\displaystyle\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}n\leq X\\
n\equiv r\,\,(\mathrm{mod}\,{q})\end{subarray}}d(n)d(n+h)=\omega(r,h;q)\int_{1}^{X}P_{h}(\log u,\log(u+h))\,\mathrm{d}u(1+O(X^{-\varepsilon^{\prime}})).

If we consider an average over congruences to a fixed modulus, we can do better, as the following corollary shows. This is again an immediate consequence of Theorem
1.2
.

Corollary 1.4
.

Assume the requirements of Theorem
1.2
and let
\mathrm{MT}_{G}(X,r,h,q)
given by (
1.3
). Then we have

\displaystyle\sum_{r(q)}\left|\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}n\equiv r\,\,(\mathrm{mod}\,{q})\end{subarray}}G(n/X)d(n)d(n+h)-\mathrm{MT}_{G}(X,r,h,q)\right|\ll X^{1/2+O(\eta)}q\bigg(|h|^{\theta}+(X/q)^{\theta}\bigg).

Assuming
\theta=0
this result is non-trivial as long as
q\leq X^{1/2-\varepsilon}
. Similarly as in the case of a fixed congruence, possible exceptional eigenvalues and larger choices of
h
will reduce the range of
q
. Unconditional we still get a non-trivial result for

\displaystyle q

\displaystyle\leq X^{\frac{1-2\theta}{2-2\theta}-\varepsilon},\quad\quad|h|\leq X/q

\displaystyle q

\displaystyle\leq X^{1/2-\varepsilon}|h|^{-\theta},\quad\quad X/q<|h|\leq X^{1+\eta}.

Here, again by
\theta\leq 7/64

[
17
]
, we have
X^{\frac{1-2\theta}{2-2\theta}}\geq X^{0.438\ldots}
and uniformly in the considered
h
range we have
X\leq X^{1/2}|h|^{-\theta}\geq X^{0.390\ldots+O(\eta)}
.

As final direct consequence of Theorem
1.2
we consider twists by an exponential phase with denominator
q
.

Corollary 1.5
.

Assume the requirements of Theorem
1.2
and let
r\in{\mathbb{Z}}
with
\gcd(r,q)=1
. Then

\displaystyle\sum_{n}G(n/X)d(n)d(n+h)e(nr/q)\ll\frac{d(q)}{q}X+X^{1/2+O(\eta)}q\bigg(|h|^{\theta}+(X/q)^{\theta}\bigg).

In a different direction, we get new results for the fourth moment of Dirichlet
L
-functions along the critical line. We first consider the case of two different characters.

Theorem 1.6
.

Assume that
q_{1},q_{2}
are square-free and let
\chi_{1},\chi_{2}
be different primitive Dirichlet characters to moduli
q_{1},q_{2}
. Let
\eta>0
and
\omega:(0,\infty)\to{\mathbb{C}}

be a smooth compactly supported function with
\omega^{(j)}\ll_{j}T^{j\eta}
for all
j\geq 0
. Then for some quadratic polynomial
P_{\chi_{1},\chi_{2}}
we have

\displaystyle\int_{\mathbb{R}}|L(1/2+it,\chi_{1})|^{2}|L(1/2+it,\chi_{2})|^{2}\omega(t/T)\mathrm{d}t=

\displaystyle\int_{\mathbb{R}}P_{\chi_{1},\chi_{2}}(\log t)\omega(t/T)\mathrm{d}t

\displaystyle+O\bigg(\,(q_{1}q_{2})^{3/4}\,T^{1/2+\theta+O(\eta)}\bigg).

If we instead have a single character, then we can do better than just setting
q_{1}=q_{2}
in the previous statement.

Theorem 1.7
.

Assume that
q
is square-free and let
\chi
be a primitive Dirichlet character to the modulus
q
. Let
\eta>0
and
\omega:(0,\infty)\to{\mathbb{C}}

be a smooth compactly supported function with
\omega^{(j)}\ll_{j}T^{j\eta}
for all
j\geq 0
. Then for some quartic polynomial
P_{\chi}
we have

\displaystyle\int_{\mathbb{R}}|L(1/2+it,\chi)|^{4}\omega(t/T)\mathrm{d}t=

\displaystyle\int_{\mathbb{R}}P_{\chi}(\log t)\omega(t/T)\mathrm{d}t+O\bigg(\,q\,T^{1/2+\theta+O(\eta)}\bigg).

These two results should be compared to
[
30
, Theorem 1.4, Theorem 1.2]
where Topacogullari obtained error terms of respective strength (for square-free moduli)

\displaystyle O((q_{1}+q_{2})^{1/2}(q_{1}q_{2})^{3/4-3\theta/2}T^{1/2+\theta})\quad\text{and}\quad O(q^{2-3\theta}T^{1/2+\theta}).

In particular, in the case of a single character we essentially save a full factor of
q
in the error term. See also the work of Kaneko
[
16
]
who obtained a full Motohashi-type spectral expansion for one character to a prime modulus, but did not improve upon the error term in the setting of
[
30
, Theorem 1.2]
. Our main technical result (Theorem
10.1
) is also applicable to a product of four
L
-functions with four different characters.

1.2.
Proof sketch of Theorem
1.1

As mentioned above, our argument borrows ideas from two main sources, the works of Duke, Friedlander, and Iwaniec
[
11
]
as well as Bruggeman and Motohashi
[
4
]
. More precisely, we start as in
[
4
]
and parametrize a given determinant problem over
\operatorname{SL}_{2}({\mathbb{R}})
with the help of a congruence subgroup. In this way we get a type of Poincaré series that is suitable for spectral expansion. Afterwards, in the spirit of
[
11
]
, we apply the Cauchy-Schwarz inequality and reverse the spectral expansion. The main new idea is to apply this on average over multiple orbits and to keep track of this data throughout the argument.

Consider the set up in Theorem
1.1
with
\alpha\in\mathcal{A}(\Gamma\backslash\mathcal{M})
and
F(\smallmatrixquantity(\lx@physics@smallmatrix a & b \\ c& d\endlx@physics@smallmatrix))=f(a,c,d)
. For simplicity let us assume that
\Gamma=\Gamma_{0}(q)
. The general case is reduced to this by conjugation by
\mathrm{a}[q_{1}]=\smallmatrixquantity(\lx@physics@smallmatrix\sqrt{q_1} & \\ & 1/\sqrt{q_1}\endlx@physics@smallmatrix)
. Using the
\Gamma
-invariance of
\alpha
we can write

\displaystyle\sum_{g\in\mathcal{M}}\alpha(g)F(g)=\sum_{\tau\in\Gamma\backslash\mathcal{M}}\alpha(\tau)P_{f}(\tau),\quad\quad P_{f}(\tau):=\sum_{\gamma\in\Gamma}F(\gamma\tau).

Here
P_{f}(\tau)
is a type of Poincaré series on
\operatorname{SL}_{2}({\mathbb{R}})
, which is smooth and invariant under the left action by
\Gamma
. Hence, applying the spectral expansion on
\Gamma\backslash\operatorname{SL}_{2}({\mathbb{R}})
we get (omitting the continuous spectrum for brevity)

\displaystyle P_{f}(\tau)=\frac{1}{|\Gamma\backslash\operatorname{SL}_{2}({\mathbb{R}})|}\langle P_{f},1\rangle_{\Gamma\backslash\operatorname{SL}_{2}({\mathbb{R}})}+\sum_{\varphi\in\mathcal{B}(q)}\langle P_{f},\varphi\rangle_{\Gamma\backslash\operatorname{SL}_{2}({\mathbb{R}})}\varphi(\tau),

where
\mathcal{B}(q)
denotes an orthonormal basis of automorphic forms on
\Gamma\backslash\operatorname{SL}_{2}({\mathbb{R}})
and the inner product is given by

\displaystyle\langle f_{1},f_{2}\rangle_{\Gamma\backslash\operatorname{SL}_{2}({\mathbb{R}})}:=\int_{\Gamma\backslash\operatorname{SL}_{2}({\mathbb{R}})}f_{1}(g)\overline{f_{2}}(g)\,\mathrm{d}g.

The projection onto the constant function produces the main term in Theorem
1.1
so that it remains to bound

(1.4)

\displaystyle\sum_{\tau\in\Gamma\backslash\mathcal{M}}\alpha(\tau)\sum_{\varphi\in\mathcal{B}(q)}\langle P_{f},\varphi\rangle_{\Gamma\backslash\operatorname{SL}_{2}({\mathbb{R}})}\varphi(\tau)\leq\sum_{\varphi\in\mathcal{B}(q)}|\langle P_{f},\varphi\rangle_{\Gamma\backslash\operatorname{SL}_{2}({\mathbb{R}})}|\bigg|\sum_{\tau\in\Gamma\backslash\mathcal{M}}\alpha(\tau)\varphi(\tau)\bigg|.

Importantly, we here keep the sum over
\tau\in\Gamma\backslash\mathcal{M}
alive in the application of the triangle inequality. In the basis
\mathcal{B}(q)
we sum over roughly
q
many elements and using the triangle inequality destroys any hope of detecting cancellation between these harmonics. By exploiting the averaging over
\tau
we are able to gain back some of this loss.

The inner products
\langle P_{f},\varphi\rangle_{\Gamma\backslash\operatorname{SL}_{2}({\mathbb{R}})}
are computed by unfolding and inserting a Fourier expansion for the automorphic form
\varphi
. This yields a sum roughly of the shape

(1.5)

\displaystyle\sqrt{AD}\sum_{\varphi\in\mathcal{B}(q)}h(\varphi)\bigg|\frac{1}{\sqrt{C/A}}\sum_{n\leq C/A}\varrho_{\varphi}(n)\bigg|\bigg|\sum_{\tau\in\Gamma\backslash\mathcal{M}}\alpha(\tau)\varphi(\tau)\bigg|,

where
\varrho_{\varphi}(n)
denote the Fourier coefficients of
\varphi
and
h(\varphi)
is a non-negative function which decays quickly in the spectral parameter. Note that by
L^{2}
-normalization both
\varrho_{\varphi}(n)
and
\varphi(\tau)
are typically of size
\approx q^{-1/2}
in absolute value.

Plugging this into (
1.4
) and applying the Cauchy-Schwarz inequality we get

\displaystyle\eqref{eq:introspecexp}\leq\sqrt{AD\,\mathcal{K}\,\mathcal{R}},

where

\displaystyle\mathcal{R}=\frac{1}{C/A}\sum_{\varphi\in\mathcal{B}(q)}h(\varphi)\bigg|\sum_{n\leq C/A}\varrho_{\varphi}(n)\bigg|^{2}

and

(1.6)

\displaystyle\mathcal{K}=\sum_{\varphi\in\mathcal{B}(q)}h(\varphi)\bigg|\sum_{\tau\in\Gamma\backslash\mathcal{M}}\alpha(\tau)\varphi(\tau)\bigg|^{2}=\sum_{\tau_{1},\tau_{2}\in\Gamma\backslash\mathcal{M}}\alpha(\tau_{1})\overline{\alpha(\tau_{2})}\sum_{\varphi\in\mathcal{B}(q)}h(\varphi)\varphi(\tau_{1})\overline{\varphi(\tau_{2})}.

The term
\mathcal{R}
may be bounded by the spectral large sieve of Deshouillers and Iwaniec
[
8
]
.

To approach
\mathcal{K}
we identify it as the spectral expansion of an automorphic kernel on
\operatorname{SL}_{2}({\mathbb{R}})
(also known as pre-trace formula). Indeed, for a suitable function
h(\varphi)
this gives the existence of a smooth function
k:\operatorname{SL}_{2}({\mathbb{R}})\to{\mathbb{C}}
supported on a small neighborhood of the identity, such that

\displaystyle\sum_{\varphi\in\mathcal{B}(q)}h(\varphi)\varphi(\tau_{1})\overline{\varphi(\tau_{2})}=\sum_{\gamma\in\Gamma}k(\tau_{2}^{-1}\gamma\tau_{1}).

Thus, denoting
g=\tau_{2}^{-1}\gamma\tau_{1}
we get

\displaystyle\mathcal{K}

\displaystyle=\sum_{\gamma\in\Gamma}\sum_{\tau_{1},\tau_{2}\in\Gamma\backslash\mathcal{M}}\alpha(\tau_{1})\overline{\alpha(\tau_{2})}k(\tau_{2}^{-1}\gamma\tau_{1})

\displaystyle=\sum_{g\in\mathcal{M}^{-1}\mathcal{M}}k(g)\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}\tau_{1},\tau_{2}\in\Gamma\backslash\mathcal{M}\\
\tau_{2}g\tau_{1}^{-1}\in\Gamma\end{subarray}}\alpha(\tau_{1})\overline{\alpha(\tau_{2})}

\displaystyle\leq\sum_{g\in\mathcal{M}^{-1}\mathcal{M}}|k(g)|\bigg|\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}\tau\in\Gamma\backslash\mathcal{M}\\
\tau g\in\mathcal{M}\end{subarray}}\alpha(\tau g)\overline{\alpha(\tau)}\bigg|,

which is almost of the shape as proposed in Theorem
1.1
.

However, we have oversimplified in the above sketch in one crucial aspect. Namely, in the case that
\max\{C/D,D/C\}
is large, the smooth weight
F
lives on a narrow part of the
\mathrm{K}
-component of the Iwasawa decomposition
\operatorname{SL}_{2}({\mathbb{R}})=\mathrm{N}\mathrm{A}\mathrm{K}
. Then the spectrum blows up in terms of the
\mathrm{K}
types and the sum over
\varphi\in\mathcal{B}(q)
actually runs over roughly
q\max\{C/D,D/C\}
many elements. This can be resolved by first rescaling the smooth weight

\displaystyle P_{f}(\tau)=\sum_{\gamma\in\Gamma}F(\gamma\tau)=\sum_{\gamma\in\Gamma}F(\gamma\tau\mathrm{a}[D/C]\mathrm{a}[C/D])=P_{f_{0}}(\tau\mathrm{a}[D/C]),

where

\displaystyle f_{0}(g)=f(g\mathrm{a}[C/D])\in C_{\delta}^{7}(A_{0},C_{0},D_{0})

with

\displaystyle C_{0}=D_{0}=\sqrt{CD},\quad A_{0}D_{0}=AD,\quad\text{and}\quad C_{0}/A_{0}=C/A.

Alternatively, this can be seen as applying the spectral expansion to the original
P_{f}
after the change of basis
\varphi(\tau)\mapsto\varphi(\tau\mathrm{a}[D/C])
.

Then by the above argument we need to bound

(1.7)

\displaystyle\begin{split}\sqrt{AD}&\sum_{\varphi\in\mathcal{B}(q)}h(\varphi)\bigg|\frac{1}{\sqrt{C/A}}\sum_{n\leq C/A}\varrho_{\varphi}(n)\bigg|\bigg|\sum_{\tau\in\Gamma\backslash\mathcal{M}}\alpha(\tau)\varphi(\tau\mathrm{a}[D/C])\bigg|\end{split}

and we arrive at

\displaystyle\mathcal{K}

\displaystyle=\sum_{\gamma\in\Gamma}\sum_{\tau_{1},\tau_{2}\in\Gamma\backslash\mathcal{M}}\alpha(\tau_{1})\overline{\alpha(\tau_{2})}k(\mathrm{a}[D/C]^{-1}\tau_{2}^{-1}\gamma\tau_{1}\mathrm{a}[D/C])

\displaystyle\leq\sum_{g\in\mathcal{M}^{-1}\mathcal{M}}|k(\mathrm{a}[C/D]g\mathrm{a}[D/C])|\bigg|\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}\tau\in\Gamma\backslash\mathcal{M}\\
\tau g\in\mathcal{M}\end{subarray}}\alpha(\tau g)\overline{\alpha(\tau)}\bigg|.

Here we can show that for
g=\smallmatrixquantity(\lx@physics@smallmatrix a & b \\ c& d\endlx@physics@smallmatrix)
the weight
|k(\mathrm{a}[C/D]g\mathrm{a}[D/C])|
vanishes outside

\displaystyle|a|+|b|C/D+|c|D/C+|d|\leq 6.

This gives the shape of
\mathcal{K}
in Theorem
1.1
.

1.3.
Sketch of applications

To illustrate how the method may be applied, we sketch the proof of Corollary
1.3
in the case
h=1
,
\gcd(r(r+1),q)=1
, using Theorem
1.1
and assuming
\theta=0
.

We have the following blueprint for applying our main theorem.

(1)

Identify the largest symmetry by a congruence subgroup
\Gamma
and the orbits
\Gamma\backslash\mathcal{M}
.

(2)

Handle very skewed ranges trivially.

(3)

Compute an upper bound for
\mathcal{K}
.

(4)

Apply theorem.

Consider the case of Corollary
1.3
with
h=1
. We write

\displaystyle d(n)=\sum_{n=bc}1,\quad\quad\quad d(n+1)=\sum_{n=ad}1

and insert a smooth dyadic partition for
a,c,d
via
f\in C^{7}_{\delta}(A,C,D)
for some
A,C,D\ll X
with
AD\asymp X
and
\delta\asymp\eta
. Denote also
B:=X/C
. This reduces matter to evaluating

\displaystyle\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}ad-bc=1\\
bc\equiv r\,\,(\mathrm{mod}\,{q})\end{subarray}}f(a,c,d).

Here we take

\displaystyle\mathcal{M}=\operatorname{SL}_{2}({\mathbb{Z}}),\quad\alpha(\smallmatrixquantity(\lx@physics@smallmatrix a & b \\ c& d\endlx@physics@smallmatrix))=\mathbf{1}_{bc\equiv r\,\,(\mathrm{mod}\,{q})}.

We then observe that
\alpha
is invariant under the action of
\Gamma=\Gamma_{2}(q,q)
, since for any
\smallmatrixquantity(\lx@physics@smallmatrix a_0 & b_0 q \\ c_0 q & d_0\endlx@physics@smallmatrix)\in\Gamma_{2}(q,q)
we have that

\displaystyle\matrixquantity(\lx@physics@matrix a_0 & b_0 q \\ c_0 q & d_0\endlx@physics@matrix)\matrixquantity(\lx@physics@matrix a & b \\ c& d\endlx@physics@matrix)=\matrixquantity(\lx@physics@matrix\ast& a_0 b + q b_0 d \\ d_0 c + q c_0 a & \ast\endlx@physics@matrix)

maps

\displaystyle bc\mapsto(a_{0}b+qb_{0}d)(d_{0}c+qc_{0}a)\equiv a_{0}d_{0}bc\equiv bc\,\,(\mathrm{mod}\,{q}).

Note that
\Gamma
is in some sense too sparse a group. The original problem lives on a set of density roughly
1/q
whereas
\Gamma
has density roughly
1/q^{2}
in
\operatorname{SL}_{2}({\mathbb{Z}})
. Consequently
\alpha
has density roughly
1/q
on the set of orbits
\Gamma\backslash\operatorname{SL}_{2}({\mathbb{Z}})
.

By symmetry (swapping
ad\leftrightarrow bc
,
a\leftrightarrow d
,
b\leftrightarrow c
) we may assume that

\displaystyle C\leq D\leq A\leq B.

The very skewed ranges where

\displaystyle\max\{A,D\}\geq qC

may be counted trivially (Poisson summation). In the complementary range we have

(1.8)

\displaystyle 1\leq\frac{A}{C},\frac{D}{C}\leq q.

To apply Theorem
1.1
we need an upper bound for

\displaystyle\mathcal{K}=\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}g=\smallmatrixquantity(\lx@physics@smallmatrix a & b \\c & d\endlx@physics@smallmatrix)\in\operatorname{SL}_{2}({\mathbb{Z}})\\
|a|+|b|C/D+|c|D/C+|d|\,\leq 6\end{subarray}}\Bigl|\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}\tau\in\Gamma\backslash\operatorname{SL}_{2}({\mathbb{Z}})\end{subarray}}\alpha(\tau)\overline{\alpha(\tau g)}\Bigr|.

The contribution from
g=I
is bounded by

\displaystyle\sum_{\tau\in\Gamma\backslash\operatorname{SL}_{2}({\mathbb{Z}})}|\alpha(\tau)|^{2}\ll q

and we can never hope to get a better bound. The contribution from
ad\neq 1
satisfies a similar bound
\ll q
by Cauchy-Schwarz and using the divisor bound for
c|ad-1
. Finally, since
1\leq D/C\leq q
and since for generic
g\in\operatorname{SL}_{2}({\mathbb{Z}})
the function
\tau\mapsto\alpha(\tau)\overline{\alpha(\tau g)}
has density
1/q\cdot 1/q=1/q^{2}
, we have

\displaystyle\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}g=\smallmatrixquantity(\lx@physics@smallmatrix\pm 1 & b \\0 & \pm 1\endlx@physics@smallmatrix)\in\operatorname{SL}_{2}({\mathbb{Z}})\\
0<|b|\,\leq 6D/C\end{subarray}}\Bigl|\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}\tau\in\Gamma\backslash\operatorname{SL}_{2}({\mathbb{Z}})\end{subarray}}\alpha(\tau)\overline{\alpha(\tau g)}\Bigr|\ll D/C\ll q,

so that
\mathcal{K}\ll q
. In fact, it is easy to check that for
\gcd(r(r+1),q)=1
we have

\displaystyle|\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}\tau\in\Gamma\backslash\operatorname{SL}_{2}({\mathbb{Z}})\end{subarray}}\alpha(\tau)\overline{\alpha(\tau g)}|\ll\gcd(b,q),\quad\quad g=\matrixquantity(\lx@physics@matrix\pm 1 & b \\0 & \pm 1\endlx@physics@matrix).

Using
1<A/C\leq q
and our assumption that
\theta=0
, we get

\displaystyle\mathcal{R}\ll 1+\frac{C}{Aq}+\frac{A}{Cq}\ll 1.

Thus, applying Theorem
1.1
we get a main term of size roughly
X/q
and an error term that is bounded by

\displaystyle\sqrt{AD\,\mathcal{K}\,\mathcal{R}}\ll X^{1/2}q^{1/2}.

This is dominated by the main term as long as
q
is a bit smaller than
X^{1/3}
.

The proof of the more general Theorem
1.2
is a bit more involved. One has to first decompose
t(n)
into functions
t^{\flat}(n;q_{0}),q_{0}|q
, which are
balanced
in the sense that they have mean value 0 over any coset of a non-trivial subgroup of
{\mathbb{Z}}/q_{0}{\mathbb{Z}}
. Applying the method separately for each
t^{\flat}(n;q_{0})
essentially gives us that
\mathcal{K}\ll q\|t\|_{2}^{2}
. Without this decomposition we would only be able to get
\mathcal{K}\ll q\|t\|_{1}^{2}
, potentially losing a factor of
q
for general
t(n)
. The key property that is leveraged here is that the weight
\alpha(\smallmatrixquantity(\lx@physics@smallmatrix a & b \\ c &d\endlx@physics@smallmatrix))=t(ad)
is constant along certain one dimensional linear fibers in the two dimensional space
\Gamma_{2}(q,q)\backslash\operatorname{SL}_{2}({\mathbb{Z}})
.

For Theorem
1.6
we apply the approximate functional equation, use the group
\Gamma_{2}(q_{1},q_{2})
, and check that

\displaystyle\alpha(\smallmatrixquantity(\lx@physics@smallmatrix a &b \\ c& d\endlx@physics@smallmatrix))=\chi_{1}(a)\overline{\chi_{1}}(b)\overline{\chi_{2}}(c)\chi_{2}(d)

is left-invariant. In fact, here we average also over the determinant

\displaystyle\sum_{h\sim H}\sum_{ad-bc=h}\alpha(\smallmatrixquantity(\lx@physics@smallmatrix a &b \\ c& d\endlx@physics@smallmatrix))f(a,c,d),\quad H\ll\frac{AD}{T},\,T\ll AD,BC\ll T\sqrt{q_{1}q_{2}}.

This and a good dependency on the size of
h
in other applications is facilitated by the more technical Theorem
10.1
. In this case a strong bound for
\mathcal{K}
is a consequence of an elementary character sum bound, which captures the oscillations in
\alpha
from the characters.

1.4.
Comparison to the literature

Consider the determinant equation

\displaystyle\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}ad-bc=1\\
q|c\end{subarray}}f(a,c,d)

with variables weighted by a smooth
f(a,c,d)
supported around
(A,C,D)
. Applying Poisson summation to the variables
a
and
d
one obtains a main term and an error term roughly of the form

\displaystyle\frac{AD}{C^{2}}\sum_{m\leq C/D}\sum_{n\leq C/A}\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}c\equiv 0\,\,(\mathrm{mod}\,{q})\end{subarray}}F(c/C)S(m,n;c),\quad S(m,n;c):=\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}a,d\,\,(\mathrm{mod}\,{c})\\
ad\equiv 1\,\,(\mathrm{mod}\,{c})\end{subarray}}e_{c}(ma+nd).

By Kuznetsov’s formula for
\Gamma_{0}(q)
, this is bounded by a sum of the form (omitting the continuous spectrum)

\displaystyle\frac{AD}{C}\sum_{\varphi\in\mathcal{B}(q)}h(\varphi)\bigg|\sum_{n\leq C/A}\varrho_{\varphi}(n)\bigg|\bigg|\sum_{m\leq C/D}\varrho_{\varphi}(m)\bigg|.

This can essentially be recovered from (
1.7
), since for
\tau=I
we morally have by the Fourier expansion (in the regular spectrum)

\displaystyle\varphi(\mathrm{a}[D/C])\approx\sqrt{D/C}\sum_{m\leq C/D}\varrho_{\varphi}(m).

A more general form with Kloosterman sums at cusps may also be recovered when
\tau
is a scaling matrix of a cusp. Therefore, for a fixed orbit
\tau
our argument produces exactly the same results as applying
[
8
]
. It should be noted that our method is simpler to apply in the sense that we never actually need to consider cusps and scaling matrices.

In similar spirit, if after spectral expansion of the Poincaré series we followed further the strategy in the work of Bruggeman and Motohashi
[
4
]
, we would need to employ what they call the
Kirillov scheme
. This could be done if the orbits were identified suitably with scaling matrices and would yield the identical result as the application of Kuznetsov’s formula we just considered. Indeed, this essentially amounts to retracing the proof of Kuznetsov’s formula as presented in the book by Cogdell and Piatetski-Shapiro
[
6
]
.

It should be mentioned that Musicantov and Zehavi
[
25
]
also consider a version of
[
11
]
on
\operatorname{SL}_{2}({\mathbb{R}})
, but the setting is otherwise similar to
[
11
]
.

1.5.
Further developments

Our underlying principle of making direct use of the group symmetry of the considered problem appears to be quite general. We intend to work on extending the method for other groups besides congruence subgroups on
\operatorname{SL}_{2}({\mathbb{Z}})
.

In a joint project with Duker Lichtman we will apply the results of this paper to the distribution of primes in arithmetic progressions to large moduli. For instance, extending
[
2
]
, we will consider averages of primes in simultaneous arithmetic progressions

\displaystyle\sum_{v\sim V}\eta_{v}\max_{\gcd(b,v)=1}\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}q\sim Q\\
\gcd(q,v)=1\end{subarray}}\lambda_{q}\bigg(\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}n\leq x\\
n\equiv a\,\,(\mathrm{mod}\,{q})\\
n\equiv b\,\,(\mathrm{mod}\,{v})\end{subarray}}\Lambda(n)-\frac{x}{\varphi(qv)}\bigg),

where
\lambda_{q}
is a factorable weight. This type of problem is motivated by the Maynard-Tao sieve with potential applications to bounded gaps between primes
[
20
,
28
]
.

Our results are uniform in determinants up to
|h|\leq X^{1+\eta}
with a small
\eta>0
. For practical applications this is of the same strength as the recent work of Assing, Blomer, and Li
[
1
]
. The sums of Kloosterman sums techniques allow one to handle even larger determinants, for instance, see Meurman
[
21
]
. While we feel that better uniformity in
h
should be achievable with more work, this appears to be a delicate problem. Similarly, it should be possible to extend the method to handle more oscillatory weight functions
f
.

In Theorem
1.2
we restrict to
\gcd(h,q)=1
to obtain uniformity in
|h|
. For general periodic weights there seems to be an obstruction for
h
which have a large common factor with
q
. Since in (
10.5
) we require that we can swap the order of the Hecke orbits and
T=\Gamma\backslash\operatorname{SL}_{2}({\mathbb{Z}})
, and the part
\gcd(h,q^{\infty})
of the Hecke orbits needs to be handled inside
\mathcal{K}
. This gives a worse dependency on
\gcd(h,q^{\infty})
. It is not clear if this problem can be overcome.

1.6.
Notations and structure of the paper

The structure of the paper is as follows. In the next two sections we summarise the necessary background material. The content is standard, but we have not found it gathered in the precise composition required for us. In Section
2
we discuss coordinate systems, differential operators and integration for
G=\operatorname{SL}_{2}({\mathbb{R}})
. In Section
3
we import the spectral expansion of
L^{2}(\Gamma\backslash G,\chi)
in terms of automorphic forms, consider their Fourier expansions, and recall Hecke operators.

Our next task is to prove technical results that are required for the proofs of the main theorems. In Section
4
we prove decay properties of certain inner products. Together with the Fourier expansion, this is part of the rigorous treatment of (
1.5
). The following two sections consider the automorphic kernel that is essential in our treatment of the
\mathcal{K}
term (see (
1.6
)). In Section
5
we give a proof of the spectral expansion of the kernel. In Section
6
we apply this to give a mean value theorem for automorphic forms, which is what we ultimately use to bound the
\mathcal{K}
part.

At this point we have gathered all the background tools to state and prove the most technical version of our main result, Theorem
7.1
. This is done in Section
7
. Combining Theorem
7.1
with the spectral large sieve that we import in Section
8
, we prove Theorem
1.1
in Section
9
.

In Section
10.1
we state and prove Theorem
10.1
which is the main technical result. Like Theorem
1.1
it follows from Theorem
7.1
and the spectral large-sieve, but it is much stronger with respect to the shift
h
.

Finally, in the last two sections, we prove the applications stated in the introduction. In section
11
we consider correlations of the divisor function with a periodic twist and show Theorem
1.2
. Section
12
deals with the
L
-functions applications, Theorems
1.7
and
1.6
.

Dependency graph of results:

We are grateful to James Maynard and Jared Duker Lichtman for helpful discussions. This project has
received funding from the European Research Council (ERC) under the European Union’s Horizon 2020 research and innovation programme (grant agreement No 851318).

2.
Geometry of the Lie group
G=\operatorname{SL}_{2}({\mathbb{R}})

In this section and the following section we provide an overview of the background material that goes into Theorems
7.1
and
10.1
. We also refer the readers to the book of Bump
[
5
]
and the excellent survey of Motohashi
[
24
]
.

2.1.
Coordinates

We start by introducing two coordinate systems for the Lie group

\displaystyle G:=\operatorname{SL}_{2}({\mathbb{R}})=\bigg\{\matrixquantity(\lx@physics@matrix a&b \\ c& d\endlx@physics@matrix):a,b,c,d\in{\mathbb{R}},ad-bc=1\bigg\}

following mostly the notation in
[
24
]
. The relevant subgroups for us are

\displaystyle\mathrm{N}:=

\displaystyle\bigg\{\mathrm{n}[x]=\matrixquantity(\lx@physics@matrix 1&x \\ & 1\endlx@physics@matrix):x\in{\mathbb{R}}\bigg\}

\displaystyle\mathrm{A}:=

\displaystyle\bigg\{\mathrm{a}[y]=\matrixquantity(\lx@physics@matrix\sqrt{y }& \\ & 1/\sqrt{y}\endlx@physics@matrix):y\in(0,\infty)\bigg\}

\displaystyle\mathrm{K}:=

\displaystyle\bigg\{\mathrm{k}[\theta]=\matrixquantity(\lx@physics@matrix\cos\theta& \sin\theta\\ -\sin\theta& \cos\theta\endlx@physics@matrix):\theta\in{\mathbb{R}}/2\pi{\mathbb{Z}}\bigg\}

We will refer to the groups
\mathrm{N},\mathrm{A},\mathrm{K}
and their elements
\mathrm{n},\mathrm{a},\mathrm{k}
without further notice and these symbols are reserved for this purpose.

We then have the
Iwasawa decomposition

\displaystyle G=\mathrm{N}\mathrm{A}\mathrm{K},

where the representation
g=\smallmatrixquantity(\lx@physics@smallmatrix a& b \\ c & d\endlx@physics@smallmatrix)=\mathrm{n}[x]\mathrm{a}[y]\mathrm{k}[\theta]
is uniquely defined for
\theta\in[0,2\pi)
via

(2.1)

\displaystyle x=\frac{ac+bd}{c^{2}+d^{2}},\quad y=\frac{1}{c^{2}+d^{2}},\quad\theta=\arctan\bigg(-\frac{c}{d}\bigg)

and

(2.2)

\displaystyle\mathrm{n}[x]\mathrm{a}[y]\mathrm{k}[\theta]=\matrixquantity(\lx@physics@matrix \sqrt{y} \cos\theta- \frac{x}{\sqrt{y}} \sin\theta& \sqrt{y} \sin\theta+ \frac{x}{\sqrt{y}} \cos\theta\\ - \frac{1}{\sqrt{y}} \sin\theta&\frac{1}{\sqrt{y}} \cos\theta\endlx@physics@matrix).

The first two Iwasawa coordinates
(x,y)
correspond to the upper half-plane
\mathbb{H}:=\{x+iy:y>0\}
, which is isomorphic to the set of cosets
G/\mathrm{K}
, in the sense that the group action on
\mathbb{H}
via
\smallmatrixquantity(\lx@physics@smallmatrix a & b \\ c&d\endlx@physics@smallmatrix):z\mapsto\frac{az+b}{cz+d}
is the matrix multiplication from the left on
G/\mathrm{K}
.

We also have the
Cartan decomposition

\displaystyle G=\mathrm{K}\mathrm{A}\mathrm{K},

where the representation
g=\mathrm{k}[\varphi]\mathrm{a}[e^{-\varrho}]\mathrm{k}[\vartheta]
is unique provided that
\varrho>0
and
\varphi\in[0,\pi)
. We have

(2.3)

\displaystyle\mathrm{k}[\varphi]\mathrm{a}[e^{\varrho}]\mathrm{k}[\vartheta]=\mathrm{k}[\varphi+\pi/2]\mathrm{a}[e^{-\varrho}]\mathrm{k}[\vartheta-\pi/2]=\mathrm{k}[\varphi+\pi]\mathrm{a}[e^{\varrho}]\mathrm{k}[\vartheta-\pi].

The Cartan coordinates are best described by the isomorphism of groups via conjugation by the Cayley matrix
\mathcal{C}=\smallmatrixquantity(\lx@physics@smallmatrix 1 & -i \\ 1 & i\endlx@physics@smallmatrix)
, which diagonalizes
\mathrm{K}
,

\displaystyle\operatorname{SL}_{2}({\mathbb{R}})

\displaystyle\cong\operatorname{SU}_{1,1}({\mathbb{R}}):=\bigg\{\matrixquantity(\lx@physics@matrix \alpha& \beta\\ \overline{\beta} & \overline{\alpha}\endlx@physics@matrix):\alpha,\beta\in{\mathbb{C}},\,|\alpha|^{2}-|\beta|^{2}=1\bigg\},

\displaystyle g

\displaystyle\mapsto\mathcal{C}g\mathcal{C}^{-1}.

From this we can compute for
\smallmatrixquantity(\lx@physics@smallmatrix a& b \\ c& d\endlx@physics@smallmatrix)=\mathrm{n}[x]\mathrm{a}[e^{t}]\mathrm{k}[\theta]=\mathrm{k}[\varphi]\mathrm{a}[e^{-\varrho}]\mathrm{k}[\vartheta]

(2.4)

\displaystyle\begin{cases}\alpha=\frac{1}{2}(a+d+i(b-c))=e^{i\theta}(\cosh(t/2)+ie^{-t/2}x/2)=e^{i(\varphi+\vartheta)}\cosh(\varrho/2),\\
\beta=\frac{1}{2}(a-d-i(b+c))=e^{-i\theta}(\sinh(t/2)-ie^{-t/2}x/2)=e^{i(\varphi-\vartheta)}\sinh(-\varrho/2).\end{cases}

The first two Cartan coordinates
(\varphi,e^{-\varrho})
correspond to the polar coordinates of the Poincaré disk model of the upper half-plane

\displaystyle\mathbb{H}\cong\{|z|<1\}:z\mapsto\frac{z-i}{z+i}.

Using (
2.3
) and (
2.4
) we compute that for

\displaystyle\mathrm{a}[e^{t}]\mathrm{n}[x]

\displaystyle=\mathrm{k}[\varphi]\mathrm{a}[e^{-\varrho}]\mathrm{k}[\vartheta]

we have

(2.5)

\displaystyle\begin{split}\cosh\varrho=&\cosh t+e^{t}x^{2}/2,\\
e^{i(\varphi+\vartheta)}=&\frac{\cosh(t/2)}{\cosh(\varrho/2)}=\frac{\cosh(t/2)+ie^{t/2}x/2}{|\cosh(t/2)+ie^{t/2}x/2|},\\
e^{i(\varphi-\vartheta)}=&\frac{\sinh(t/2)}{\sinh(\varrho/2)}=\frac{\sinh(t/2)-ie^{t/2}x/2}{|\sinh(t/2)-ie^{t/2}x/2|}.\end{split}

Denoting

\displaystyle u(z,w):=\frac{|z-w|^{2}}{4\operatorname{Im}(z)\operatorname{Im}(w)},\quad z,w\in\mathbb{H},

we have
\cosh(\varrho)=2u(gi,i)+1
and for
g=\smallmatrixquantity(\lx@physics@smallmatrix a & b \\ c& d\endlx@physics@smallmatrix)\in G

(2.6)

\displaystyle u(gi,i)=\tfrac{1}{4}(a^{2}+b^{2}+c^{2}+d^{2}-2).

2.2.
Differential operators

Following the notation in
[
24
]
,
we introduce matrices

\displaystyle\mathrm{X}_{1}:=\matrixquantity(\lx@physics@matrix\,\,&1 \\ \,\, &\endlx@physics@matrix),\quad\mathrm{X}_{2}:=\matrixquantity(\lx@physics@matrix 1& \\ &-1\endlx@physics@matrix),\quad\mathrm{X}_{3}:=\matrixquantity(\lx@physics@matrix & 1 \\ -1 & \endlx@physics@matrix),

which are the infinitesimal generators for the subgroups

\displaystyle\mathrm{N}=\{\operatorname{exp}(t\mathrm{X}_{1}):t\in{\mathbb{R}}\},\,\mathrm{A}=\{\operatorname{exp}(t\mathrm{X}_{2}):t\in{\mathbb{R}}\},\,\mathrm{K}=\{\operatorname{exp}(t\mathrm{X}_{3}):t\in{\mathbb{R}}\}.

We then define the right Lie differentials

\displaystyle\mathrm{x_{j}}f(g):=\partial_{t}f(g\,\operatorname{exp}(t\mathrm{X}_{j}))|_{t=0}.

By construction these operators are left-invariant under the action of the group
G
on itself. That is, denoting

\displaystyle l_{h}:f(g)\mapsto f(hg),\quad r_{h}:f(g)\mapsto f(gh),

we have
\mathrm{x}_{j}l_{h}=l_{h}\mathrm{x}_{j}
for
j\in\{1,2,3\}
.

We construct the
raising and lowering operators

\displaystyle\mathrm{e}^{+}:=

\displaystyle 2\mathrm{x}_{1}+\mathrm{x}_{2}-i\mathrm{x}_{3},

\displaystyle\mathrm{e}^{-}:=

\displaystyle-2i\mathrm{x}_{1}+\mathrm{x}_{2}+i\mathrm{x}_{3}.

In the Iwasawa coordinates
\mathrm{n}[x]\mathrm{a}[y]\mathrm{k}[\theta]
we have

\displaystyle\mathrm{e}^{+}

\displaystyle=e^{2i\theta}(2iy\partial_{x}+2y\partial_{y}-i\partial_{\theta}),

\displaystyle\mathrm{e}^{-}

\displaystyle=e^{-2i\theta}(-2iy\partial_{x}+2y\partial_{y}+i\partial_{\theta}),

\displaystyle\mathrm{x}_{3}

\displaystyle=\partial_{\theta}.

The
Casimir operator
is then defined as

(2.7)

\displaystyle\Omega:=-\frac{1}{4}\mathrm{e}^{+}\mathrm{e}^{-}+\frac{1}{4}\mathrm{x}_{3}^{2}-\frac{1}{2}i\mathrm{x}_{3}.

The Casimir element is not only left-invariant but also right-invariant under the action of the group
G
, that is,

(2.8)

\displaystyle\Omega l_{h}=l_{h}\Omega,\quad\Omega r_{h}=r_{h}\Omega.

In the Iwasawa coordinates
\mathrm{n}[x]\mathrm{a}[y]\mathrm{k}[\theta]
the Casimir operator
\Omega
is given by

(2.9)

\displaystyle\Omega=-y^{2}(\partial_{x}^{2}+\partial_{y}^{2})+y\partial_{x}\partial_{\theta},

where the first part
-y^{2}(\partial_{x}^{2}+\partial_{y}^{2})
is the Laplace operator on the upper half-plane
\mathbb{H}
.
In the Cartan coordinates
\mathrm{k}[\varphi]\mathrm{a}[e^{-\varrho}]\mathrm{k}[\vartheta]
, denoting
\cosh\varrho=2u+1,
we have

(2.10)

\displaystyle\begin{split}\Omega=&-\partial_{\varrho}^{2}-\frac{1}{\tanh\varrho}\partial_{\varrho}-\frac{1}{4\sinh^{2}\varrho}\partial_{\varphi}^{2}+\frac{1}{2\sinh\varrho\tanh\varrho}\partial_{\varphi}\partial_{\vartheta}-\frac{1}{4\sinh^{2}\varrho}\partial_{\vartheta}^{2}\\
=&-u(u+1)\partial_{u}^{2}-(2u+1)\partial_{u}-\frac{1}{16u(u+1)}\partial_{\varphi}^{2}+\frac{2u+1}{4u(u+1)}\partial_{\varphi}\partial_{\vartheta}-\frac{1}{16u(u+1)}\partial_{\vartheta}^{2}.\end{split}

From this we see that
\Omega
commutes with taking inverses: If

\displaystyle\iota:f(g)\mapsto f(g^{-1}),

then

(2.11)

\displaystyle\Omega\iota=\iota\Omega

since

\displaystyle(\mathrm{k}[\varphi]\mathrm{a}[e^{-\varrho}]\mathrm{k}[\vartheta])^{-1}=\mathrm{k}[-\vartheta]\mathrm{a}[e^{\varrho}]\mathrm{k}[-\varphi]=\mathrm{k}[\pi/2-\vartheta]\mathrm{a}[e^{-\varrho}]\mathrm{k}[-\pi/2-\varphi].

Let
\ell\in{\mathbb{Z}}
and
\nu\in{\mathbb{C}}
. The functions (defined using the Iwasawa coordinates)

(2.12)

\displaystyle\phi_{\ell}(g,\nu):=y^{\nu+1/2}e^{i\ell\theta}

are eigenfunctions of the Casimir operator
\Omega
on
G

\displaystyle\Omega\phi_{\ell}(g,\nu)=\bigg(\frac{1}{4}-\nu^{2}\bigg)\phi_{\ell}(g,\nu).

Furthermore, the functions
\phi_{\ell}(g,\nu)
are of
right-type

\ell
, by which we mean that

\displaystyle\phi_{\ell}(g\mathrm{k}[\theta],\nu)=e^{i\ell\theta}\phi_{\ell}(g,\nu).

The notion of functions with
left-type

\ell
is defined similarly.

2.3.
Integration

The invariant Haar measure
\mathrm{d}g
on
G
is defined in terms of the Iwasawa and Cartan coordinates via (note the swap between
\mathrm{a}
and
\mathrm{n}
)

(2.13)

\displaystyle\begin{split}\mathrm{d}\mathrm{n}[x]=\mathrm{d}x,\quad&\mathrm{d}\mathrm{a}[y]=\frac{\mathrm{d}y}{y},\quad\mathrm{d}\mathrm{k}[\theta]=\frac{\mathrm{d}\theta}{2\pi},\\
\int_{G}f(g)\mathrm{d}g=&\int_{\mathrm{A}\times\mathrm{N}\times\mathrm{K}}f(\mathrm{a}\mathrm{n}\mathrm{k})\mathrm{d}\mathrm{a}\mathrm{d}\mathrm{n}\mathrm{d}\mathrm{k}=\int_{{\mathbb{R}}\times{\mathbb{R}}_{>0}\times{\mathbb{R}}/2\pi{\mathbb{Z}}}f(\mathrm{n}[x]\mathrm{a}[y]\mathrm{k}[\theta])\frac{\mathrm{d}x\mathrm{d}y\mathrm{d}\theta}{2\pi y^{2}}\\
=&\int_{\mathrm{K}\times\mathrm{K}}\int_{{\mathbb{R}}_{>0}}f(\mathrm{k}_{1}\mathrm{a}[e^{-\varrho}]\mathrm{k}_{2})\sinh(\varrho)\mathrm{d}\varrho\,\mathrm{d}\mathrm{k}_{1}\mathrm{d}\mathrm{k}_{2}\end{split}

Then

(2.14)

\displaystyle\int_{\Gamma_{0}(q)\backslash G}\mathrm{d}g=[\operatorname{SL}_{2}({\mathbb{Z}}):\Gamma_{0}(q)]\int_{\operatorname{SL}_{2}({\mathbb{Z}})\backslash\operatorname{SL}_{2}({\mathbb{R}})}\mathrm{d}g=\frac{\pi}{6}q\prod_{p|q}(1+p^{-1}).\,

In terms of the matrix coordinates we have (by using (
2.1
) to compute the Jacobian determinant)

\displaystyle\mathrm{d}g=\frac{\mathrm{d}x\mathrm{d}y\mathrm{d}\theta}{2\pi y^{2}}=\frac{1}{\pi}\frac{\mathrm{d}a\mathrm{d}c\mathrm{d}d}{c}.

Thus, using
\zeta(2)=\pi^{2}/6
we have for any integrable
F:G\to{\mathbb{C}}

(2.15)

\displaystyle\frac{1}{|\Gamma_{0}(q)\backslash G|}\int_{G}F(g)\mathrm{d}g=\frac{1}{\zeta(2)\,q\,\prod_{p|q}(1+p^{-1})}\int_{{\mathbb{R}}^{3}}F(\smallmatrixquantity(\lx@physics@smallmatrix a & \ast\\ c & d\endlx@physics@smallmatrix))\frac{\mathrm{d}a\mathrm{d}c\mathrm{d}d}{c}.

The inner product on
L^{2}(G)
is defined by

\displaystyle\langle f_{1},f_{2}\rangle_{G}:=\int_{G}f_{1}(g)\overline{f_{2}(g)}\mathrm{d}g.

Then we have

(2.16)

\displaystyle\langle\mathrm{e}^{\pm}f_{1},f_{2}\rangle_{G}=-\langle f_{1},\mathrm{e}^{\mp}f_{2}\rangle_{G}

and

(2.17)

\displaystyle\langle\Omega f_{1},f_{2}\rangle_{G}=\langle f_{1},\Omega f_{2}\rangle_{G},

that is,
\Omega
is a symmetric operator.

3.
Spectral theory of
L^{2}(\Gamma\backslash G,\chi)

Fix
q\in{\mathbb{Z}}_{>0}
and denote
\Gamma=\Gamma_{0}(q)
. While we consider the more general groups
\Gamma_{2}(q_{1},q_{2})
, these are isomorphic to
\Gamma_{0}(q_{1}q_{2})
by conjugation with
\mathrm{a}[q_{1}]
. Let
\chi
be a Dirichlet character to the modulus
q
. This defines a character on
\Gamma
via

\displaystyle\chi(\smallmatrixquantity(\lx@physics@smallmatrix a &b \\ c & d\endlx@physics@smallmatrix)):=\chi(d)=\overline{\chi}(a).

We let
\kappa\in\{0,1\}
denote the parity of
\chi
, that is,
\chi(-1)=(-1)^{\kappa}.

The group
\Gamma
acts on
G
by matrix multiplication from the left. We denote

\displaystyle\langle f_{1},f_{2}\rangle_{\Gamma\backslash G}:=\int_{\Gamma\backslash G}f_{1}(g)\overline{f_{2}(g)}\mathrm{d}g

and

\displaystyle L^{2}(\Gamma\backslash G,\chi):=\{f:G\to{\mathbb{C}}:f(\gamma g)=\chi(\gamma)f(g),\quad\langle f,f\rangle_{\Gamma\backslash G}<\infty\}.

In this section we consider the decomposition of this space into an orthogonal basis of automorphic forms and the Fourier expansion of these basis elements. The automorphic forms described below of course depend on
q
and
\chi
but it is customary to omit this dependency in the notation.

3.1.
Spectral expansion

We denote by
\mathfrak{C}(q,\chi)
a set of inequivalent representatives for the singular cusps of
\Gamma\setminus G
for the character
\chi
. The notion of singular cusps and scaling matrices coincide (with the identification
G/\mathrm{K}\cong\mathbb{H}
) with the upper half-plane setting ( see
[
9
, Section 4.1]
, for instance).
Let
\phi_{\ell}(g,\nu)
be as in (
2.12
) and suppose that
\ell\equiv\kappa\,\,(\mathrm{mod}\,{2})
. For
\mathfrak{c}\in\mathfrak{C}(q,\chi)
, we define the Eisenstein series for
{\mathbb{R}}(\nu)>1/2
by

\displaystyle E_{\mathfrak{c}}^{(\ell)}(g,\nu)=\sum_{\gamma\in\Gamma_{\mathfrak{c}}\setminus\Gamma}\overline{\chi}(\gamma)\phi_{\ell}(\sigma_{\mathfrak{c}}^{-1}\gamma g,\nu),

where
\Gamma_{\mathfrak{c}}
is the stabilising group and
\sigma_{\mathfrak{c}}
a scaling matrix of the cusp
\mathfrak{c}
. The Eisenstein series are extended to
\nu\in{\mathbb{C}}
by meromorphic continuation, and have only one simple pole at
\nu=1/2
for
\Gamma=\Gamma_{0}(q)

[
8
, Lemma 3.7]
. For general Fuchsian groups
\Gamma
it is possible that there are other poles on
0<\nu<1/2
, which would appear as terms in the spectral decomposition
[
14
, Chapter 7]
. The residue at
\nu=1/2
is

\displaystyle\mathrm{Res}_{\nu=1/2}E^{(0)}_{\mathfrak{c}}(z,\nu)=\frac{3}{\pi q\prod_{p|q}(1+p^{-1})}.

Proposition 3.1
.

(Spectral expansion of
L^{2}(\Gamma\backslash G,\chi)
)
.
Let
q\in{\mathbb{Z}}_{>0}
,
\chi
be a Dirichlet character to the modulus
q
of parity
\kappa\in\{0,1\}
, and let
\Gamma=\Gamma_{0}(q)
. There exists a countable set
\mathcal{B}(q,\chi)
and for
V\in\mathcal{B}(q,\chi),\ell\in{\mathbb{Z}},\ell\equiv\kappa\,\,(\mathrm{mod}\,{2})
, complex numbers
\nu_{V}
, and smooth functions
\varphi^{(\ell)}_{V}\in L^{2}(\Gamma\backslash G,\chi)
of right-type
\ell
with

(3.1)

\displaystyle\Omega\varphi^{(\ell)}_{V}=\bigg(\frac{1}{4}-\nu_{V}^{2}\bigg)\varphi^{(\ell)}_{V}

such that the following holds. For any smooth and bounded
f\in L^{2}(\Gamma\backslash G,\chi)
with
\Omega f
bounded we have

\displaystyle f(g)=

\displaystyle\mathbf{1}_{\chi\,\mathrm{principal}}\frac{1}{|\Gamma\backslash G|}\langle f,1\rangle_{\Gamma\backslash G}+\sum_{V\in\mathcal{B}(q,\chi)}\sum_{\ell\equiv\kappa\,\,(\mathrm{mod}\,{2})}\langle f,\varphi_{V}^{(\ell)}\rangle_{\Gamma\backslash G}\varphi_{V}^{(\ell)}(g)

\displaystyle+\sum_{\mathfrak{c}\in\mathfrak{C}(q,\chi)}\sum_{\ell\equiv\kappa\,\,(\mathrm{mod}\,{2})}\frac{1}{4\pi i}\int_{(0)}\langle f,E^{(\ell)}_{\mathfrak{c}}(*,\nu)\rangle_{\Gamma\backslash G}E^{(\ell)}_{\mathfrak{c}}(g,\nu)\mathrm{d}\nu.

Note that while the Eisenstein series are not square-integrable, for
f\in L^{2}(\Gamma\backslash G,\chi)
the inner product
\langle f,E^{(\ell)}_{\mathfrak{c}}(*,\nu)\rangle_{\Gamma\backslash G}
exists and is finite.
Proposition
3.1
follows from
[
10
, Section 4]
, by identifying for a fixed
\ell
the basis elements
u_{j}
of weight
k_{\text{DFI}}=\ell
in
[
10
, (4.50))]
via

\displaystyle\varphi_{V}^{(\ell)}(\mathrm{n}[x]\mathrm{a}[y]\mathrm{k}[\theta])=u_{j}(x+iy)e^{i\ell\theta},

and similarly for the Eisenstein series.

We now describe the functions
\varphi_{V}^{(\ell)}
in the discrete spectrum. The objects
V
appearing are the irreducible subspaces of
L^{2}(\Gamma\backslash G,\chi)
, and the Casimir operator is constant on each
V
, that is, there is some
\nu_{V}
such that for all smooth
f\in V
we have
\Omega f=(\frac{1}{4}-\nu_{V}^{2})f
. Each
V
splits into subspaces according to the right-action by
\mathrm{K}

\displaystyle V=\bigoplus_{\ell\equiv\kappa\,\,(\mathrm{mod}\,{2})}V^{(\ell)},

where
V^{(\ell)}
is a one or zero dimensional subspace consisting of functions of right-type
\ell
. The raising and lowering operators define maps
\mathrm{e}^{\pm}:V^{(\ell)}\to V^{(\ell\pm 2)}
. We pick generators
\varphi^{(\ell)}_{V}
for each
V^{(\ell)}
such that

\displaystyle\langle\varphi^{(\ell)}_{V},\varphi^{(\ell)}_{V}\rangle_{\Gamma\backslash G}=1

to get an orthonormal basis.

The cuspidal spectrum
\mathcal{B}(q,\chi)
splits into three parts

(1)

Regular spectrum:
\nu_{V}\in i{\mathbb{R}}

(2)

Exceptional spectrum:
\nu_{V}\in(0,1/2)

(3)

Discrete series:
\nu_{V}=\frac{k-1}{2},\quad k>0,\quad k\equiv\kappa\,\,(\mathrm{mod}\,{2})
.

In the first two cases all of the
V^{(\ell)}
are non-trivial. The Selberg eigenvalue conjecture states that the exceptional spectrum is empty. The best bound towards this is by Kim-Sarnak
[
17
]
, which states that for congruence subgroups we have
\nu_{V}\in(0,7/64)
in the possible exceptional spectrum. We let
\theta_{q}
denote
\max_{V}\{\operatorname{Re}(\nu_{V})<1/2\}
.

For the discrete series
\nu_{V}=k-1/2
we call
k
the
weight
and we have either

\displaystyle V=\bigoplus_{\begin{subarray}{c}\ell\geq k\\
\ell\equiv\kappa\,\,(\mathrm{mod}\,{2})\end{subarray}}V^{(\ell)}\quad\text{or}\quad V=\bigoplus_{\begin{subarray}{c}\ell\leq-k\\
\ell\equiv\kappa\,\,(\mathrm{mod}\,{2})\end{subarray}}V^{(\ell)}.

In either case the edge function
\varphi_{V}^{(\pm k)}
is annihilated by the lowering/raising operator, that is,
\mathrm{e}^{\mp}\varphi_{V}^{(\pm k)}=0
. For classical holomorphic modular forms
u(x+iy)
of weight
k
we have a correspondence

\displaystyle\varphi_{V}^{(k)}(\mathrm{n}[x]\mathrm{n}[y]\mathrm{k}[\theta])=y^{k/2}u(x+iy)e^{ik\theta}.

3.2.
Fourier expansion of automorphic forms

To compute the projections
\langle f,\varphi_{V}^{(\ell)}\rangle_{\Gamma\backslash G}
we use the Fourier expansion of the basis elements
\varphi_{V}^{(\ell)}
. To state the Fourier expansion, we use the Jacquet operator
\mathcal{A}^{\pm}
. For a function
\phi
on
G
it is defined as

\displaystyle\mathcal{A}^{\pm}\phi(g)=\int_{-\infty}^{\infty}e(\mp\xi)\phi(\mathrm{w}\mathrm{n}[\xi]g)\mathrm{d}\xi,

where

\displaystyle\mathrm{w}=\mathrm{k}[\pi/2]=\matrixquantity(\lx@physics@matrix 0 & 1 \\ -1 & 0\endlx@physics@matrix)

is the Weyl element of
G
.

We will use the Jacquet operator only for the choice
\phi(g)=\phi_{\ell}(g,\nu)
as given in (
2.12
). In that case we can express it in terms of Whittaker functions (see
[
24
, eq. (16.1)]
)

(3.2)

\displaystyle\mathcal{A}^{\pm}\phi_{\ell}(g,\nu)=(-1)^{\ell/2}\pi^{\nu+1/2}e^{i\ell\theta}e(\pm x)\frac{W_{\pm\ell/2,\nu}(4\pi y)}{\Gamma(\nu\pm\ell/2+\frac{1}{2})},

where in Iwasawa coordinates
g=\mathrm{n}[x]\mathrm{a}[y]\mathrm{k}[\theta]
(the choice of the sign of
(-1)^{\ell/2}
for odd
\ell
will not be important for us). We record here the following integral expression (see
[
24
, eq. (16.2)]
) which will be useful later

(3.3)

\displaystyle\mathcal{A}^{\pm}\phi_{\ell}(g,\nu)=e^{i\ell\theta}e(\pm x)y^{1/2-\nu}\int_{-\infty}^{\infty}\frac{e(-y\xi)}{(\xi^{2}+1)^{\nu+1/2}}\left(\frac{\xi-i}{\xi+i}\right)^{\pm\ell/2}\mathrm{d}\xi,

where for odd
\ell
we choose the branch cut
{\mathbb{R}}_{>0}
for
(\frac{\xi-i}{\xi+i})^{\pm\ell/2}
.
We also note that

(3.4)

\displaystyle\begin{split}\Omega\mathcal{A}^{\pm}\phi_{\ell}(g,\nu)=(1/4-\nu^{2})\mathcal{A}^{\pm}\phi_{\ell}(g,\nu),\quad\partial_{x}^{2}\mathcal{A}^{\pm}\phi_{\ell}(g,\nu)=-4\pi^{2}\mathcal{A}^{\pm}\phi_{\ell}(g,\nu),\\
\partial_{\theta}^{2}\mathcal{A}^{\pm}\phi_{\ell}(g,\nu)=-\ell^{2}\mathcal{A}^{\pm}\phi_{\ell}(g,\nu).\end{split}

We gather the statements of
[
24
, (17.3), (17.5), (18.2), (21.11), (24.3)]
in the following, noting that for
\nu_{V}\in i{\mathbb{R}}
we have
\left|\pi^{-2\nu_{V}}\frac{\Gamma(|\ell|/2+\nu_{V}+1/2)}{\Gamma(|\ell|/2-\nu_{V}+1/2)}\right|^{1/2}=1
.

Proposition 3.2
.

We have the Fourier expansions

\displaystyle\varphi_{V}^{(\ell)}(g)=\left|\pi^{-2\nu_{V}}\frac{\Gamma(|\ell|/2+\nu_{V}+1/2)}{\Gamma(|\ell|/2-\nu_{V}+1/2)}\right|^{1/2}\sum_{n\neq 0}\frac{\varrho_{V}(n)}{\sqrt{|n|}}\mathcal{A}^{\operatorname{sgn}(n)}\phi_{\ell}(\mathrm{a}[|n|]g,\nu_{V})

and

\displaystyle E_{\mathfrak{c},\ell}(g,\nu)=

\displaystyle 1_{\mathfrak{c}=\infty}\phi_{\ell}(g,\nu)+\varrho_{\mathfrak{c,\nu}}^{(\ell)}(0)\phi_{\ell}(g,-\nu)

\displaystyle+\sum_{n\neq 0}\frac{\varrho_{\mathfrak{c},\nu}(n)}{\sqrt{|n|}}\mathcal{A}^{\operatorname{sgn}(n)}\phi_{\ell}(\mathrm{a}[|n|]g,\nu_{V}),

where the coefficients
\varrho_{V}(n)
and
\varrho_{\mathfrak{c},\nu}(n)
do not depend on
\ell
.

Of course the Fourier coefficients
\varrho_{V}(n)
and
\varrho_{\mathfrak{c},\nu}(n)
depend on both
q
and
\chi
. As mentioned, this is suppressed in the notation. The Fourier coefficients can be written in terms of the upper half plane setup as in
[
8
]
for
\kappa=0
as follows (the supersript D-I refers to the notation in Deshouillers-Iwaniec)

\displaystyle\varrho_{V}(n)

\displaystyle=\frac{\Gamma(\nu_{V}+1/2)}{2\pi^{\nu_{V}+1/2}}\rho^{\text{D-I}}_{j}(n)\quad\text{with}\quad\nu_{V}=i\kappa^{\text{D-I}}_{j},\quad\text{if }\nu_{V}\not\in\mathbb{N}-1/2;

\displaystyle\varrho_{V}(n)

\displaystyle=\frac{(-1)^{k/2}\Gamma(|k|)^{1/2}}{2^{k}\pi^{(|k|+1)/2}n^{(k-1)/2}}\psi^{\text{D-I}}_{j,k}(n),\quad\text{ if }\nu_{V}=(k-1)/2,n>0\text{ and }V\text{ holomorphic};

\displaystyle\varrho_{V}(n)

\displaystyle=\overline{\varrho_{\overline{V}}(-n)},\quad\text{ if }\nu_{V}=k-1/2,n<0\text{ and }V\text{ anti-holomorphic};

\displaystyle\varrho_{\mathfrak{c},\nu}(n)

\displaystyle=|n|^{\nu}\varphi^{\text{D-I}}_{\mathfrak{c}\infty n}(1/2+\nu),\quad\text{ if }n\neq 0.

3.3.
Hecke operators

The Hecke operator on
L^{2}(\Gamma_{0}(q)\backslash G,\chi)
is defined by

(3.5)

\displaystyle\mathcal{T}_{h}f(g):=\frac{1}{\sqrt{h}}\sum_{ad=h}\chi(a)\sum_{b\,\,(\mathrm{mod}\,{d})}f\bigg(\frac{1}{\sqrt{h}}\matrixquantity(\lx@physics@matrix a & b \\ & d\endlx@physics@matrix)g\bigg).

It satisfies the multiplicativity relation

\displaystyle\mathcal{T}_{m}\mathcal{T}_{n}=\sum_{d|\gcd(m,n)}\chi(d)\mathcal{T}_{mn/d^{2}}.

Then for
\gcd(h,q)=1
the Hecke operators
\mathcal{T}_{h}
commute with each other and
\Omega
, and furthermore are normal (since
\langle\mathcal{T}_{h}f,g\rangle=\langle f,\overline{\chi}(h)\mathcal{T}_{h}g\rangle
), so that we can choose a common orthonormal basis. Then

(3.6)

\displaystyle\mathcal{T}_{h}\varphi_{V}^{(\ell)}(\tau)=\lambda_{V}(h)\varphi_{V}^{(\ell)}(\tau)

for the Hecke eigenvalues
\lambda_{V}(h)
. Similarly, for the Eisenstein series we have for
\gcd(h,q)=1

[
10
, (6.16)]

(3.7)

\displaystyle\mathcal{T}_{h}E_{\mathfrak{c}}^{(\ell)}(\tau,\nu)=\lambda_{\mathfrak{c},\nu}(h)E_{\mathfrak{c}}^{(\ell)}(\tau,\nu)

with
\lambda_{\mathfrak{c},\nu}(h)
given explicitly in
[
10
, (6.17)]
. For
\gcd(h,q)=1
the Hecke eigenvalues satisfy the multiplicativity relation

\displaystyle\lambda_{V}(h)\varrho_{V}(n)=\sum_{d|\gcd(h,n)}\chi(d)\varrho_{V}(hn/d^{2}),

and similarly for the Eisenstein series.

We have
|\lambda_{\mathfrak{c},\nu}(h)|\leq d(h)\ll_{\varepsilon}|h|^{\varepsilon}.
The Ramanujan-Petersson conjecture states that also in the cuspidal spectrum

\displaystyle|\lambda_{V}(h)|\ll_{\varepsilon}|h|^{\varepsilon}.

For the discrete series, this was proved by Deligne
[
7
]
. In general the best bound towards this is
\ll_{\varepsilon}|h|^{\vartheta_{q}+\varepsilon}
with
\vartheta_{q}\leq 7/64
by Kim and Sarnak
[
17
]
.

3.4.
Useful heuristics

The automorphic forms
\varphi_{V}^{(\ell)}
are
L^{2}
-normalized and since the volume of the fundamental domain satisfies
|\Gamma_{0}(q)\backslash G|=q^{1+o(1)}
, we expect that for typical
g\in G
and
n\in{\mathbb{Z}}\setminus\{0\}

\displaystyle|\varphi_{V}^{(\ell)}(g)|=q^{-1/2+o(1)},\quad\quad|\varrho_{V}(n)|=q^{-1/2+o(1)}.

With the help of Selberg’s trace formula one can show that the eigenvalues
\nu_{V}
in the cuspidal spectrum satisfy

\displaystyle|\{\nu_{V}\in i{\mathbb{R}}:|\nu_{V}|\leq K\}|=q^{1+o(1)}K^{2},

that is, there are roughly
q^{1+o(1)}K
spectral parameters in
i[K,K+1]
. In our applications
K
will be small, so that morally the number of harmonics used is
q^{1+o(1)}
. We are unable to detect cancellation between the harmonics and thus always lose at least a factor of
\sqrt{q}
compared to heuristically optimal bounds in the error terms.

4.
Decay of spectrum and Fourier expansion

After applying the spectral expansion of Proposition
3.1
we will need to bound inner products such as
\langle P,\varphi_{V}^{(\ell)}\rangle_{\Gamma\backslash G}
, where
P
is a kind of Poincaré series associated to some smooth function
F
on the ambient space
G
. By unfolding and inserting the Fourier expansion (Proposition
3.2
) we encounter then sums of the form

\displaystyle\sum_{V,\ell}\sum_{n}\frac{\varrho_{V}(n)}{\sqrt{|n|}}\langle F,\mathcal{A}^{\operatorname{sgn}(n)}\phi_{\ell}(\mathrm{a}[|n|]\cdot\,,\nu_{V})\rangle_{G}.

The main goal of this section is to prove the following bound, which simultaneously achieves two things – decay in the parameters
n,\nu,\ell
as well as summation by parts to decouple the smooth weight from the variable
n
. This morally speaking allows us to truncate the above sum at
|\nu_{V}|\ll\delta^{-1},|\ell|\ll\delta^{-1},|n|\ll(\delta X)^{-1}
.

Proposition 4.1
.

Let
\delta\in(0,1)
and
X,Y>0
,
\kappa\in\{0,1\}
. Let
a_{V,\ell},b_{\mathfrak{c},\nu,\ell,}:{\mathbb{Z}}_{>0}\to{\mathbb{C}}
and let
F:G\to{\mathbb{C}}
,
F(g)=F(\mathrm{n}[x]\mathrm{a}[y]\mathrm{k}[\theta])
, be
(2J+1)
times differentiable in all three variables, supported on
(x,y)\in[-X,X]\times[Y,2Y]
, satisfying for all
J_{1},J_{2},J_{3}\geq 0
with
J_{1}+J_{2}+J_{3}\leq 2J+1

(4.1)

\displaystyle\partial_{x}^{J_{1}}\partial_{y}^{J_{2}}\partial_{\theta}^{J_{3}}F\ll(\delta X)^{-J_{1}}(\delta Y)^{-J_{2}}(\delta)^{-J_{3}}.

Define the decay function

(4.2)

\displaystyle D_{J}(t,\nu,\ell):=\min_{J_{0}\in\{0,1,\dots,J\}}\frac{(1+\log t)^{2}(1+Y/X)^{2J_{0}}}{\delta^{2}(1+|\delta\nu|+|\delta tX|+|\delta\ell|)^{2J_{0}-1}}

Then for
\sigma\in\{\pm\}
we have for any
J\geq 0

(4.3)

\displaystyle\begin{split}\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}V\in\mathcal{B}(q,\chi)\\
\operatorname{Re}(\nu_{V})<1/2\end{subarray}}&\sum_{\ell\equiv\kappa\,\,(\mathrm{mod}\,{2})}\sum_{n>0}\langle F,\mathcal{A}^{\sigma}\phi_{\ell}(\mathrm{a}[n]\cdot,\nu_{V})\rangle_{G}\,\frac{a_{V,\ell}(n)}{\sqrt{n}}\\
\ll&\max_{t>0}\frac{X}{Y^{1/2}}\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}V\in\mathcal{B}(q,\chi)\\
\operatorname{Re}(\nu_{V})<1/2\end{subarray}}\sum_{\ell\equiv\kappa\,\,(\mathrm{mod}\,{2})}(Yt)^{-\operatorname{Re}(\nu_{V})}D_{J}(t,\nu_{V},\ell)\bigg|\sum_{0<n\leq t}a_{V,\ell}(n)\bigg|,\end{split}

(4.4)

\displaystyle\begin{split}\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}V\in\mathcal{B}(q,\chi)\\
\nu_{V}=(k-1)/2\end{subarray}}\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}\ell\equiv\kappa\,\,(\mathrm{mod}\,{2})\\
\sigma\ell\geq k\end{subarray}}&\left|\pi^{-2\nu_{V}}\frac{\Gamma(|\ell|/2+\nu_{V}+1/2)}{\Gamma(|\ell|/2-\nu_{V}+1/2)}\right|^{1/2}\sum_{n>0}\langle F,\mathcal{A}^{\sigma}\phi_{\ell}(\mathrm{a}[n]\cdot,\nu_{V})\rangle_{G}\,\frac{a_{V,\ell}(n)}{\sqrt{n}}\\
&\ll\max_{t>0}\frac{X}{Y^{1/2}}\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}V\in\mathcal{B}(q,\chi)\\
\nu_{V}=(k-1)/2\end{subarray}}\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}\ell\equiv\kappa\,\,(\mathrm{mod}\,{2})\\
\sigma\ell\geq k\end{subarray}}D_{J}(t,\nu_{V},\ell)\bigg|\sum_{0<n\leq t}a_{V,\ell}(n)\bigg|,\end{split}

and

(4.5)

\displaystyle\begin{split}\sum_{\mathfrak{c}\in\mathfrak{C}(q,\chi)}&\sum_{\ell\equiv\kappa\,\,(\mathrm{mod}\,{2})}\int_{(0)}\sum_{n>0}\langle F,\mathcal{A}^{\sigma}\phi_{\ell}(\mathrm{a}[n]\cdot,\nu)\rangle_{G}\,\frac{b_{\mathfrak{c},\nu,\ell}(n)}{\sqrt{n}}\mathrm{d}\nu\\
&\ll\max_{t>0}\frac{X}{Y^{1/2}}\sum_{\mathfrak{c}\in\mathfrak{C}(q,\chi)}\int_{(0)}\sum_{\ell\equiv\kappa\,\,(\mathrm{mod}\,{2})}D_{J}(t,\nu,\ell)\bigg|\sum_{0<n\leq t}b_{\mathfrak{c},\nu,\ell}(n)\bigg||\mathrm{d}\nu|.\end{split}

After using the Fourier series of Proposition
3.2
, Proposition
4.1
gives us a tool to handle all parts of the spectral expansion except the
n=0
terms of the Eisenstein series. This is covered by the following.

Proposition 4.2
.

Suppose that the assumptions of Proposition
4.1
hold and let
D_{J}(t,\nu,\ell)
be defined by (
4.2
). We have for any complex coefficients
b_{\mathfrak{c},\nu,\ell}

\displaystyle\sum_{\mathfrak{c}\in\mathfrak{C}(q,\chi)}\sum_{\ell\equiv\kappa\,\,(\mathrm{mod}\,{2})}\int_{(0)}\langle F,\phi_{\ell}(\cdot,\nu)\rangle b_{\mathfrak{c},\nu,\ell}\ll\frac{X}{Y^{1/2}}\sum_{\mathfrak{c}\in\mathfrak{C}(q,\chi)}\int_{(0)}\sum_{\ell\equiv\kappa\,\,(\mathrm{mod}\,{2})}D_{J}(1,\nu,\ell)|b_{\mathfrak{c},\nu,\ell}||\mathrm{d}\nu|.

To show Proposition
4.1
we use the following lemma that is an extension of
[
24
, eqs. (15.4), (15.5)]
.

Lemma 4.3
.

Let
g=\mathrm{n}[x]\mathrm{a}[y]\mathrm{k}[\theta]
.
We have the following bounds.

(1)

For
\operatorname{Re}(\nu)\geq 0
and
y\in(0,1)
we have

\displaystyle\mathcal{A}^{\sigma}\phi_{\ell}(g,\nu)\ll_{\varepsilon}\,(|\ell|+|\nu|+1)y^{1/2-\operatorname{Re}(\nu)}(1+|\log y|).

(2)

For
\operatorname{Re}(\nu)\geq 0
and
y>0
we have

\displaystyle\mathcal{A}^{\sigma}\phi_{\ell}(g,\nu)\ll(|\ell|+|\nu|+1)y^{-1/2-\operatorname{Re}(\nu)}e^{-y/(|\ell|+|\nu|+1)}

(3)

For
\nu=(k-1)/2
,
\sigma\ell\geq k
,
\ell\equiv k\,\,(\mathrm{mod}\,{2})
we have

\displaystyle\pi^{-2\nu_{V}}\frac{\Gamma(|\ell|/2+k/2)}{\Gamma(|\ell|/2-k/2+1)}\int_{0}^{\infty}|\mathcal{A}^{\sigma}\phi_{\ell}(\mathrm{a}[y],\nu)|^{2}\frac{\mathrm{d}y}{y^{2}}\ll\,1.

Proof.

Statements (1) and (2) are contained in
[
24
, eq. (15.5)]
in the case
\ell\equiv 0\,\,\,(\mathrm{mod}\,{2})
. The proofs apply also for
\ell\equiv 1\,\,\,(\mathrm{mod}\,{2})
.

To prove (3), for
\nu=(k-1)/2
and
\sigma\ell\geq k
we have

\displaystyle\bigg(\pi^{-2\nu_{V}}\frac{\Gamma(|\ell|/2+k/2)}{\Gamma(|\ell|/2-k/2+1)}

\displaystyle\bigg)^{1/2}\mathcal{A}^{\sigma}\phi_{\ell}(\mathrm{a}[y],\nu)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle(-1)^{k/2}\pi^{1/2}\frac{\Gamma(|\ell|/2-k/2+1)^{1/2}}{\Gamma(|\ell|/2+k/2)^{1/2}}e^{-2\pi y}(4\pi y)^{k/2}L^{(k-1)}_{(|\ell|-k)/2}(4\pi y),

where
L^{(\alpha)}_{n}
is the Laguerre polynomial
[
29
, Chapter V (5.1.5)]
defined by

\displaystyle L^{(\alpha)}_{n}(x):=\frac{x^{-\alpha}e^{x}}{n!}\partial_{x}^{n}\bigg(e^{-x}x^{n+\alpha}\bigg).

This can be seen from shifting the contour to
\operatorname{Im}(\xi)=-\infty
in (
3.3
) and calculating the residue of the pole of order
(|\ell|-k)/2
at
\xi=-i
. The Laguerre polynomials satisfy the orthogonality relation
[
29
, Chapter V, (5.1.1)]

\displaystyle\int_{0}^{\infty}x^{\alpha}e^{-x}L^{(\alpha)}_{n}(x)L^{(\alpha)}_{m}(x)\mathrm{d}x=\mathbf{1}_{m=n}\frac{\Gamma(n+\alpha+1)}{\Gamma(n+1)}.

We also have the recurrence relation
[
29
, Chapter V, (5.1.13)]

\displaystyle L_{n}^{(\alpha)}(x)=\sum_{i=0}^{n}L_{i}^{(\alpha-1)}(x).

Thus, we get by expanding the square and by orthogonality

\displaystyle\int_{0}^{\infty}x^{\alpha-1}e^{-x}L^{(\alpha)}_{n}(x)^{2}\mathrm{d}x=\sum_{i=0}^{n}\frac{\Gamma(i+\alpha)}{\Gamma(i+1)}\leq(n+1)\frac{\Gamma(n+\alpha)}{\Gamma(n+1)},

so that in particular

\displaystyle\int_{0}^{\infty}(4\pi y)^{k-2}e^{-4\pi y}L^{(k-1)}_{(|\ell|-k)/2}(4\pi y)^{2}\mathrm{d}y\ll(|\ell|/2-k/2+1)\frac{\Gamma(|\ell|/2+k/2-1)}{\Gamma(|\ell|/2-k/2+1)}.

Thus, we have

\displaystyle\pi^{-2\nu_{V}}\frac{\Gamma(|\ell|/2+k/2)}{\Gamma(|\ell|/2-k/2+1)}

\displaystyle\int_{0}^{\infty}|\mathcal{A}^{\sigma}\phi_{\ell}(\mathrm{a}[y],\nu)|^{2}\frac{\mathrm{d}y}{y^{2}}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle 4^{2}\pi^{3}\frac{\Gamma(|\ell|/2-k/2+1)}{\Gamma(|\ell|/2+k/2)}\int_{0}^{\infty}(4\pi y)^{k-2}e^{-4\pi y}L^{(k-1)}_{(|\ell|-k)/2}(4\pi y)^{2}\mathrm{d}y

\displaystyle\ll

\displaystyle\,(|\ell|/2-k/2+1)\frac{\Gamma(|\ell|/2-k/2+1)}{\Gamma(|\ell|/2+k/2)}\frac{\Gamma(|\ell|/2+k/2-1)}{\Gamma(|\ell|/2-k/2+1)}

\displaystyle\ll

\displaystyle\,1.

∎

4.1.
Proof of Propositions
4.1
and
4.2

Let us first consider (
4.3
).
Let

\displaystyle\psi_{\nu,\ell}(t):=t^{-1/2}\langle F,\mathcal{A}^{\sigma}\phi_{\ell}(\mathrm{a}[t]\cdot,\nu)\rangle_{G}

so that the left-hand side of (
4.3
) is

\displaystyle\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}V\in\mathcal{B}(q,\chi)\\
\operatorname{Re}(\nu_{V})<1/2\end{subarray}}\sum_{\ell\equiv\kappa\,\,(\mathrm{mod}\,{2})}\sum_{n>0}\psi_{\nu_{V},\ell}(n)a_{V,\ell}(n).

We define the second order differential operator

\displaystyle\Xi:=1+\delta^{2}(1/4-\Omega)+\bigg(\frac{\delta X}{2\pi i}\partial_{x}\bigg)^{2}+\bigg(\frac{\delta}{i}\partial_{\theta}\bigg)^{2}

and observe that by (
3.4
) and by left-invariance of
\Omega
for any
J\geq 0

(4.6)

\displaystyle\Xi^{J}\mathcal{A}^{\sigma}\phi_{\ell}(\mathrm{a}[t]g,\nu)=\Xi^{J}l_{\mathrm{a}[t]}\mathcal{A}^{\sigma}\phi_{\ell}(g,\nu)=(1+(\delta\nu)^{2}+(\delta tX)^{2}+(\delta\ell)^{2})^{J}l_{\mathrm{a}[t]}\mathcal{A}^{\sigma}\phi_{\ell}(g,\nu)

and that by (
4.1
)

(4.7)

\displaystyle\begin{split}\Xi^{J}F\ll_{J}(1+Y/X)^{2J}\quad\quad\text{and}\\
\partial_{t}l_{\mathrm{a}[t^{-1}]}\Xi^{J}F\ll_{J}(\delta t^{2})^{-1}(1+Y/X)^{2J}.\end{split}

By (
4.6
) we get for any
J\geq 0

\displaystyle\psi_{\nu,\ell}^{\prime}(t)

\displaystyle=(t^{-1/2}\partial_{t}-\tfrac{1}{2}t^{-3/2})\langle F,l_{\mathrm{a}[t]}\mathcal{A}^{\sigma}\phi_{\ell}(\cdot,\nu)\rangle_{G}

\displaystyle=\frac{(t^{-1/2}\partial_{t}-\tfrac{1}{2}t^{-3/2})}{(1+(\delta\nu)^{2}+(\delta tX)^{2}+(\delta\ell)^{2})^{J}}\langle F,\Xi^{J}l_{\mathrm{a}[t]}\mathcal{A}^{\sigma}\phi_{\ell}(\cdot,\nu)\rangle_{G}

\displaystyle=\frac{(t^{-1/2}\partial_{t}-\tfrac{1}{2}t^{-3/2})}{(1+(\delta\nu)^{2}+(\delta tX)^{2}+(\delta\ell)^{2})^{J}}\langle l_{\mathrm{a}[-t]}\overline{\Xi^{J}}F,\mathcal{A}^{\sigma}\phi_{\ell}(\cdot,\nu)\rangle_{G},

where we used the symmetry of
\Omega
(
2.17
) as well as the Iwasawa decomposition to apply integration by parts for
\partial_{x}
and
\partial_{\theta}
, and the invariance of the measure
\mathrm{d}g
. Using (
4.7
) we get

\displaystyle\psi_{\nu,\ell}^{\prime}(t)\ll\frac{(\delta^{-1}t^{-5/2}+t^{-3/2})(1+Y/X)^{2J}}{(1+|\delta\nu|+|\delta tX|+|\delta\ell|)^{2J}}\int_{G}\mathbf{1}_{\begin{subarray}{c}x\ll Xt\\
y\asymp Yt\end{subarray}}\left|\mathcal{A}^{\sigma}\phi_{\ell}(\mathrm{n}[x]\mathrm{a}[y],\nu)\right|\mathrm{d}g.

This gives us by (1) and (2) of Lemma
4.3

(4.8)

\displaystyle\begin{split}\psi_{\nu,\ell}^{\prime}(t)&\ll\frac{(\delta^{-1}t^{-5/2}+t^{-3/2})(1+Y/X)^{2J}(1+|\nu|+|\ell|)}{(1+|\delta\nu|+|\delta tX|+|\delta\ell|)^{2J}}Xt\int_{\begin{subarray}{c}y\asymp Yt\end{subarray}}y^{-3/2-\operatorname{Re}(\nu)}\mathrm{d}y\\
&\ll\frac{(\delta^{-1}t^{-2}+t^{-1})(1+Y/X)^{2J}(1+|\nu|+|\ell|)}{(1+|\delta\nu|+|\delta tX|+|\delta\ell|)^{2J}}\frac{X}{Y^{1/2}}(Yt)^{-\operatorname{Re}(\nu)}.\end{split}

Defining

\displaystyle R_{V,\ell}(t):=\sum_{0<n\leq t}a_{V,\ell}(n),

we bound the left-hand side of (
4.3
) with Abel summation and (
4.8
)

\displaystyle\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}V\in B(q,\chi)\\
\operatorname{Re}(\nu_{V})<1/2\end{subarray}}\sum_{\ell\equiv\kappa\,\,(\mathrm{mod}\,{2})}\sum_{n>0}\psi_{\nu_{V},\ell}(n)a_{V,\ell}(n)=-\int_{1/2}^{\infty}\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}V\in\mathcal{B}(q,\chi)\\
\operatorname{Re}(\nu_{V})<1/2\end{subarray}}\sum_{\ell\equiv\kappa\,\,(\mathrm{mod}\,{2})}\psi^{\prime}_{\nu_{V},\ell}(t)R_{V,\ell}(t)\mathrm{d}t

\displaystyle\leq\int_{1/2}^{\infty}\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}V\in\mathcal{B}(q,\chi)\\
\operatorname{Re}(\nu_{V})<1/2\end{subarray}}\sum_{\ell\equiv\kappa\,\,(\mathrm{mod}\,{2})}|\psi^{\prime}_{\nu_{V},\ell}(t)||R_{V,\ell}(t)|\mathrm{d}t

\displaystyle\ll\int_{1/2}^{\infty}\frac{X}{Y^{1/2}}\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}V\in\mathcal{B}(q,\chi)\\
\operatorname{Re}(\nu_{V})<1/2\end{subarray}}\sum_{\ell\equiv\kappa\,\,(\mathrm{mod}\,{2})}\frac{(\delta^{-1}t^{-2}+t^{-1})(1+Y/X)^{2J}(1+|\nu|+|\ell|)}{(1+|\delta\nu|+|\delta tX|+|\delta\ell|)^{2J}}(Yt)^{-\operatorname{Re}(\nu)}|R_{V,\ell}(t)|\mathrm{d}t

\displaystyle\ll\int_{1/2}^{\infty}\frac{X}{Y^{1/2}}\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}V\in\mathcal{B}(q,\chi)\\
\operatorname{Re}(\nu_{V})<1/2\end{subarray}}\sum_{\ell\equiv\kappa\,\,(\mathrm{mod}\,{2})}\frac{(1+\log t)^{2}(1+Y/X)^{2J}}{\delta^{2}(1+|\delta\nu|+|\delta tX|+|\delta\ell|)^{2J-1}}(Yt)^{-\operatorname{Re}(\nu)}|R_{V,\ell}(t)|\frac{\mathrm{d}t}{t(1+\log t)^{2}}

The claim (
4.3
) now follows by allowing the parameter
J
to depend on
t
, taking the minimum over them, and then taking the maximum over
t
since
\int_{1/2}^{\infty}\frac{\mathrm{d}t}{t(1+\log t)^{2}}<\infty
. The proof of (
4.5
) follows by the same argument.

For the discrete series (
4.4
) with
\nu_{V}=(k-1)/2
,
\sigma\ell>k
, to get the analogue of (
4.8
) we have to bound

\displaystyle\left|\pi^{-2\nu_{V}}\frac{\Gamma(|\ell|/2+\nu_{V}+1/2)}{\Gamma(|\ell|/2-\nu_{V}+1/2)}\right|^{1/2}\int_{y\sim Yt}\left|\mathcal{A}^{\sigma}\phi_{\ell}(\mathrm{a}[y],\nu)\right|\frac{\mathrm{d}y}{y^{2}}.

By Cauchy-Schwarz and (3) of Lemma
4.3
this is bounded by

\displaystyle\ll

\displaystyle\bigg(\int_{y\sim Yt}\frac{\mathrm{d}y}{y^{2}}\bigg)^{1/2}\bigg(\left|\pi^{-2\nu_{V}}\frac{\Gamma(|\ell|/2+\nu_{V}+1/2)}{\Gamma(|\ell|/2-\nu_{V}+1/2)}\right|\left|\int_{0}^{\infty}\mathcal{A}^{\sigma}\phi_{\ell}(\mathrm{a}[y],\nu)\right|^{2}\frac{\mathrm{d}y}{y^{2}}\bigg)^{1/2}\ll

\displaystyle(Yt)^{-1/2}.

The rest of the argument is exactly the same.

Proposition
4.2
follows by a similar argument but is technically much easier. We use the point-wise upper bound
|\phi_{\ell}(g,\nu)|\leq y^{1/2}
instead of Lemma
4.3
.

5.
Spectral expansion of an automorphic kernel on
G

Let
k:G\to{\mathbb{C}}
be a smooth function with compact support. For
\ell_{1}\equiv\ell_{2}\,\,(\mathrm{mod}\,{2})
we define the projections

(5.1)

\displaystyle k_{\ell_{1},\ell_{2}}(g):=\int_{\mathrm{K}}\int_{\mathrm{K}}k(\mathrm{k_{1}}g\mathrm{k_{2}})e^{-i\ell_{1}\theta(\mathrm{k_{1}})-i\ell_{2}\theta(\mathrm{k_{2}})}\mathrm{d}\mathrm{k}_{1}\mathrm{d}\mathrm{k}_{2}.

Then

\displaystyle k_{\ell_{1},\ell_{2}}(\mathrm{k}[\theta_{1}]g\mathrm{k}[\theta_{2}])=e^{i\ell_{1}\theta_{1}+i\ell_{2}\theta_{2}}k_{\ell_{1},\ell_{2}}(g)

and we say that any function with this property is of type
(\ell_{1},\ell_{2})
, or left-type
\ell_{1}
and right-type
\ell_{2}
. By the Cartan decomposition
g=\mathrm{k}[\varphi]\mathrm{a}[e^{-\varrho}]\mathrm{k}[\vartheta]
a function of fixed type is uniquely determined by its values on
\mathrm{A}
. We have a Fourier series decomposition into functions of fixed type

\displaystyle k(g)=\sum_{\ell_{1},\ell_{2}\in{\mathbb{Z}}}k_{\ell_{1},\ell_{2}}(g)=\sum_{\ell_{1},\ell_{2}\in{\mathbb{Z}}}k_{\ell_{1},\ell_{2}}(\mathrm{a}[e^{-\varrho}])e^{i\ell_{1}\varphi+i\ell_{2}\vartheta}.

Given
k
as above, we define the invariant integral operator with kernel
k_{\ell_{1},\ell_{2}}
as

\displaystyle L_{\ell_{1},\ell_{2}}f(g):=\int_{G}k_{\ell_{1},\ell_{2}}(g^{-1}h)f(h)\mathrm{d}h.

The main goal of this section is to prove the following proposition, which gives a spectral expansion of an automorphic kernel (also known as the pre-trace formula). For our purpose it is enough to consider the case
\ell_{1}=\ell_{2}
. In terms of the upper half-plane language this can be found in Hejhal’s books
[
12
,
13
]
. As these comprehensive books are not the easiest-to-use sources, we opt to provide a self-contained proof, generalising the argument in
[
14
, Chapter 1.8]
.

Proposition 5.1
.

Let
k:G\to{\mathbb{C}}
be smooth and compactly supported. Define

\displaystyle K^{(\ell)}(\tau_{1},\tau_{2}):=\sum_{\gamma\in\Gamma}\overline{\chi}(\gamma)k_{\ell,\ell}(\tau_{2}^{-1}\gamma\tau_{1}).

Then for
\ell\equiv\kappa\,\,(\mathrm{mod}\,{2})

\displaystyle K^{(\ell)}(\tau_{1},\tau_{2})=

\displaystyle\frac{\mathbf{1}_{\chi\,\mathrm{principal}}\mathbf{1}_{\ell=0}}{|\Gamma\backslash G|}\int_{G}k(g)\mathrm{d}g

\displaystyle+\sum_{V\in\mathcal{B}(q,\chi)}\Phi_{\ell,\ell}(k;\nu_{V})\varphi_{V}^{(\ell)}(\tau_{1})\overline{\varphi_{V}^{(\ell)}(\tau_{2})}

\displaystyle+\sum_{\mathfrak{c}\in\mathfrak{C}(q,\chi)}\frac{1}{4\pi i}\int_{(0)}\Phi_{\ell,\ell}(k;\nu)E^{(\ell)}_{\mathfrak{c}}(\tau_{1},\nu)\overline{E^{(\ell)}_{\mathfrak{c}}(\tau_{2},\nu)}\mathrm{d}\nu,

where

(5.2)

\displaystyle\Phi_{\ell,\ell}(k;\nu):=

\displaystyle\int_{G}k_{\ell,\ell}(h)\overline{\phi_{\ell}(h,\nu)}\mathrm{d}h.

Proposition
5.1
is a corollary of Proposition
3.1
and the following.

Proposition 5.2
.

Let
\nu\in{\mathbb{C}}
,
k\in C_{0}^{\infty}(G)
, and let
f_{\nu}^{(\ell)}
be an eigenfunction of
\Omega
with eigenvalue
1/4-\nu^{2}
and right-type
\ell
. Then we have

\displaystyle L_{\ell,\ell}\overline{f_{\nu}^{(\ell)}(g)}=\Phi_{\ell,\ell}(k;\nu)\overline{f_{\nu}^{(\ell)}(g)}.

Proof of Proposition
5.1
assuming Proposition
5.2
.

By Proposition
3.1
applied to
\tau_{1}
we have

\displaystyle K^{(\ell)}(\tau_{1},\tau_{2})=

\displaystyle\frac{\mathbf{1}_{\chi\,\mathrm{principal}}}{|\Gamma\backslash G|}\langle K^{(\ell)}(\cdot,\tau_{2}),1\rangle_{\Gamma\backslash G}+\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}V\in\mathcal{B}(q,\chi)\\
j\equiv\kappa\,\,(\mathrm{mod}\,{2})\end{subarray}}\langle K^{(\ell)}(\cdot,\tau_{2}),\varphi_{V}^{(j)}\rangle\varphi_{V}^{(j)}(\tau_{1})

(5.3)

\displaystyle+\sum_{\mathfrak{c}\in\mathfrak{C}(q,\chi)}\sum_{j\equiv\kappa\,\,(\mathrm{mod}\,{2})}\frac{1}{4\pi i}\int_{(0)}\langle K^{(\ell)}(\cdot,\tau_{2}),E^{(j)}_{\mathfrak{c}}(\cdot,\nu)\rangle E^{(j)}_{\mathfrak{c}}(g,\nu)\mathrm{d}\nu.

By unfolding the first term matches the first term in Proposition
6.1
. We use unfolding to compute

\displaystyle\langle K^{(\ell)}(\cdot,\tau_{2}),\varphi_{V}^{(j)}\rangle=

\displaystyle\int_{\Gamma\backslash G}K^{(\ell)}(g,\tau_{2})\overline{\varphi_{V}^{(j)}(g)}\mathrm{d}g=\sum_{\gamma\in\Gamma}\int_{\Gamma\backslash G}\overline{\chi}(\gamma)k_{\ell,\ell}(\tau_{2}^{-1}\gamma g)\overline{\varphi_{V}^{(j)}(g)}\mathrm{d}g

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\sum_{\gamma\in\Gamma}\int_{\gamma(\Gamma\backslash G)}k_{\ell,\ell}(\tau_{2}^{-1}g)\overline{\varphi_{V}^{(j)}(g)}\mathrm{d}g=\int_{G}k_{\ell,\ell}(\tau_{2}^{-1}g)\overline{\varphi_{V}^{(j)}(g)}\mathrm{d}g.

With the Haar measure decomposition
\mathrm{d}g=\mathrm{d}\mathrm{a}\mathrm{d}\mathrm{n}\mathrm{d}\mathrm{k}
we get

\displaystyle\int_{G}k_{\ell,\ell}(\tau_{2}^{-1}g)\overline{\varphi_{V}^{(j)}(g)}\mathrm{d}g=

\displaystyle\int_{\mathrm{N}}\int_{\mathrm{A}}\int_{\mathrm{K}}k_{\ell,\ell}(\tau_{2}^{-1}\mathrm{a}\mathrm{n}\mathrm{k})\overline{\varphi_{V}^{(j)}(\mathrm{a}\mathrm{n}\mathrm{k})}\mathrm{d}\mathrm{k}\mathrm{d}\mathrm{a}\mathrm{d}\mathrm{n}

\displaystyle=
