\displaystyle\int_{\mathrm{N}}\int_{\mathrm{A}}k_{\ell,\ell}(\tau_{2}^{-1}\mathrm{a}\mathrm{n})\overline{\varphi_{V}^{(j)}(\mathrm{a}\mathrm{n})}\int_{\mathrm{K}}e^{i(\ell-j)\theta(\mathrm{k})}\mathrm{d}\mathrm{k}\mathrm{d}\mathrm{a}\mathrm{d}\mathrm{n}.

The innermost integral is
1_{\ell=j}
. We recall that the
\varphi_{V}^{(l)}
are eigenfunctions by (
3.1
) and apply Proposition
5.2
to get

\displaystyle\langle K^{(\ell)}(\cdot,\tau_{2}),\varphi_{V}^{(\ell)}\rangle

\displaystyle=\int_{G}k_{\ell,\ell}(\tau_{2}^{-1}g)\overline{\varphi_{V}^{(\ell)}(g)}\mathrm{d}g

\displaystyle=L_{\ell,\ell}\overline{\varphi_{V}^{(\ell)}(\tau_{2})}

(5.4)

\displaystyle=\Phi_{\ell,\ell}(k;\nu_{V})\overline{\varphi_{V}^{(\ell)}(\tau_{2})}.

Similar computation holds for the Eisenstein series contribution. Combining (
5.3
) and (
5.4
) Proposition
5.1
follows.
∎

5.1.
Lemmas

We now gather some lemmas for the proof of Proposition
5.2
. As mentioned, our argument is similar to
[
14
, Section 1.8]
.
Recall that
k_{\ell,\ell}(g)
is of left and right type
\ell
and

\displaystyle L_{\ell,\ell}f(g):=\int_{G}k_{\ell,\ell}(g^{-1}h)f(h)\mathrm{d}h=\int_{G}k_{\ell,\ell}(g,h)f(h)\mathrm{d}h,

where we denote

\displaystyle k_{\ell,\ell}(g,h):=k_{\ell,\ell}(g^{-1}h).

Using the Cartan coordinates
g=\mathrm{k}[\varphi]\mathrm{a}[e^{-\varrho}]\mathrm{k}[\vartheta]
and denoting for
u=u(g)

\displaystyle\cosh(\varrho)=2u+1,\quad p_{\ell}(u):=k_{\ell,\ell}(\mathrm{a}[e^{-\varrho}])

we have

(5.5)

\displaystyle k_{\ell,\ell}(g)=e^{i\ell(\varphi+\vartheta)}p_{\ell}(u)=:H_{\ell}(g)p_{\ell}(u).

From (
2.10
) we immediately get the following (compare to
[
24
, eq. (33.7)]
).

Lemma 5.3
.

Denoting

\displaystyle\mathcal{D}_{u,\ell}:=u(u+1)\partial_{u}^{2}+(2u+1)\partial_{u}+\frac{\ell^{2}}{4(u+1)},

we have

\displaystyle\Omega k_{\ell,\ell}(g)=H_{\ell}(g)\mathcal{D}_{u,\ell}\,p_{\ell}(u).

Lemma 5.4
.

We have

\displaystyle\Omega_{h}k_{\ell,\ell}(g,h)=\Omega_{g}k_{\ell,\ell}(g,h)

Proof.

Recall (
2.8
) and (
2.11
), that is, that
\Omega
commutes with translations
r_{h},l_{h}
and the inversion
\iota
. Thus,

\displaystyle\Omega_{h}k(g^{-1}h)=\Omega_{h}l_{g^{-1}}k(h)=l_{g^{-1}}\Omega_{h}k(h)=(\Omega k)(g^{-1}h)

and

\displaystyle\Omega_{g}k(g^{-1}h)=\Omega_{g}r_{h}\iota k(g)=r_{h}\iota\Omega_{g}k(g)=(\Omega k)(g^{-1}h).

∎

From this we get the following.

Lemma 5.5
.

The operators
\Omega
and
L_{\ell,\ell}
commute.

Proof.

By Lemma
5.4
and (
2.17
) we have

\displaystyle\Omega L_{\ell,\ell}f(g)

\displaystyle=\int\Omega_{g}k_{\ell,\ell}(g,h)f(h)\mathrm{d}h=\int\Omega_{h}k_{\ell,\ell}(g,h)f(h)\mathrm{d}h

\displaystyle=\int k_{\ell,\ell}(g,h)\Omega_{h}f(h)\mathrm{d}h=L_{\ell,\ell}\Omega f(g).

∎

Given
h\in G
we define

\displaystyle\mathcal{R}_{h,\ell}f(g):=\int_{0}^{2\pi}e^{i\ell\theta}f(h\mathrm{k}[\theta]h^{-1}g)\frac{\mathrm{d}\theta}{2\pi}

and state some simple facts for this operator.

Lemma 5.6
.

If
f
is of right-type
-\ell
, then

\displaystyle\mathcal{R}_{g,\ell}f(g)=f(g).

Furthermore, denoting

(5.6)

\displaystyle F(g_{1},g_{2}):=\frac{1}{f(g_{2})}\int_{0}^{2\pi}e^{i\ell\theta}f(g_{2}k[\theta]g_{1})\frac{\mathrm{d}\theta}{2\pi},

we have

(5.7)

\displaystyle F(h^{-1}g,h)=\mathcal{R}_{h,\ell}f(g)f(h)^{-1}

and
F(g_{1},g_{2})
has type
(-\ell,-\ell)
in
g_{1}
and right-type
0
in
g_{2}
.

Proof.

Since
f
has right-type
-\ell
we get

\displaystyle\mathcal{R}_{g,\ell}f(g)=\int_{0}^{2\pi}e^{i\ell\theta}f(g\mathrm{k}[\theta]g^{-1}g)\frac{\mathrm{d}\theta}{2\pi}=\int_{0}^{2\pi}f(g)\frac{\mathrm{d}\theta}{2\pi}=f(g),

which proves the first claim. For the remaining part of the lemma, we observe that (
5.7
) is immediate from the definitions and the type claim follows from

\displaystyle F(\mathrm{k}[\theta_{1}]g_{1}\mathrm{k}[\theta_{2}],g_{2}\mathrm{k}[\theta_{3}])=

\displaystyle\frac{1}{f(g_{2}\mathrm{k}[\theta_{3}])}\int_{0}^{2\pi}e^{i\ell\theta}f(g_{2}k[\theta+\theta_{3}+\theta_{1}]g_{1}\mathrm{k}[\theta_{2}])\frac{\mathrm{d}\theta}{2\pi}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle e^{-i\ell(\theta_{1}+\theta_{2})}\frac{1}{f(g_{2})}\int_{0}^{2\pi}e^{i\ell\theta}f(g_{2}k[\theta]g_{1})\frac{\mathrm{d}\theta}{2\pi}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle e^{-i\ell(\theta_{1}+\theta_{2})}F(g_{1},g_{2}).

∎

We next show that
L_{\ell,\ell}
and
\mathcal{R}_{h,\ell}
commute.

Lemma 5.7
.

We have

\displaystyle L_{\ell,\ell}\mathcal{R}_{h,\ell}=\mathcal{R}_{h,\ell}L_{\ell,\ell}.

In particular, we have

\displaystyle L_{\ell,\ell}(\mathcal{R}_{g,\ell}f)(g)=L_{\ell,\ell}f(g).

Proof.

We have by invariance of the measure
\mathrm{d}h^{\prime}
and using
k(g_{1},g_{3}^{-1}g_{2})=k(g_{3}g_{1},g_{2})

\displaystyle L_{\ell,\ell}\mathcal{R}_{h,\ell}f(g)

\displaystyle=\int_{G}k_{\ell,\ell}(g,h^{\prime})\mathcal{R}_{h,\ell}f(h^{\prime})\mathrm{d}h^{\prime}

\displaystyle=\int_{G}k_{\ell,\ell}(g,h^{\prime})\int_{0}^{2\pi}e^{i\ell\theta}f(h\mathrm{k}[\theta]h^{-1}h^{\prime})\frac{\mathrm{d}\theta}{2\pi}\mathrm{d}h^{\prime}

\displaystyle=\int_{0}^{2\pi}e^{i\ell\theta}\int_{G}k_{\ell,\ell}(g,h^{\prime})f(h\mathrm{k}[\theta]h^{-1}h^{\prime})\mathrm{d}h^{\prime}\frac{\mathrm{d}\theta}{2\pi}

\displaystyle=\int_{0}^{2\pi}e^{i\ell\theta}\int_{G}k_{\ell,\ell}(g,(h\mathrm{k}[\theta]h^{-1})^{-1}h^{\prime})f(h^{\prime})\mathrm{d}h^{\prime}\frac{\mathrm{d}\theta}{2\pi}

\displaystyle=\int_{0}^{2\pi}e^{i\ell\theta}\int_{G}k_{\ell,\ell}(h\mathrm{k}[\theta]h^{-1}g,h^{\prime})f(h^{\prime})\mathrm{d}h^{\prime}\frac{\mathrm{d}\theta}{2\pi}

\displaystyle=\mathcal{R}_{h,\ell}L_{\ell,\ell}f(g).

∎

Recall that

\displaystyle\phi_{\ell}(g,\nu)=y^{\nu+1/2}e^{i\ell\theta}.

Lemma 5.8
.

Let
\ell\in{\mathbb{Z}}
,
\lambda\in{\mathbb{C}}
. There is a unique smooth function
U_{\lambda,\ell}:G\to{\mathbb{C}}
which is of type
(\ell,\ell)
satisfying

\displaystyle U_{\lambda,\ell}(1)=1,\quad\Omega U_{\lambda,\ell}=\lambda U_{\lambda,\ell}.

Denoting
\lambda=1/4-\nu^{2}
, we have

(5.8)

\displaystyle U_{\lambda,\ell}(g)=\int_{0}^{2\pi}\phi_{\ell}(\mathrm{k}[-\theta]g\mathrm{k}[\theta],\nu)\frac{\mathrm{d}\theta}{2\pi}=\int_{0}^{2\pi}\phi_{\ell}(\mathrm{k}[-\theta]g\mathrm{k}[\theta],-\nu)\frac{\mathrm{d}\theta}{2\pi}.

Proof.

Recall that
H_{\ell}(g)=e^{i\ell(\varphi+\vartheta)}
and that, just as as in (
5.5
), we have for any
U_{\lambda,\ell}
of type
(\ell,\ell)
for some
P_{\lambda,\ell}
that

\displaystyle U_{\lambda,\ell}(g)=H_{\ell}(g)P_{\lambda,\ell}(u(g)).

By Lemma
5.3
we see that
\Omega U_{\lambda,\ell}=\lambda U_{\lambda,\ell}
is equivalent to

\displaystyle(u(u+1)\partial_{u}^{2}+(2u+1)\partial_{u}+\lambda+\frac{\ell^{2}}{4(u+1)})P_{\lambda,\ell}(u)=0.

Multiplying this with
u/(u+1)
(since
u>0
) we get

\displaystyle(u^{2}\partial_{u}^{2}+u\frac{(2u+1)}{u+1}\partial_{u}+\frac{u\lambda}{u+1}+\frac{u\ell^{2}}{4(u+1)^{2}})P_{\lambda,\ell}(u)=0.

Following the notation in
[
31
, Section 10.3]
), this second order differential equation is of the form

\displaystyle\bigg((z-c)^{2}\partial_{z}+(z-c)P(z)\partial_{z}+Q(z)\bigg)U(z)=0

where
c=0,P(z)=\frac{2z+1}{z+1},Q(z)=\frac{z\lambda}{z+1}+\frac{z\ell^{2}}{4(z+1)^{2}}
. We then consider the indicial equation

\displaystyle\alpha^{2}+(p_{0}-1)\alpha+q_{0}=0

with

\displaystyle p_{0}

\displaystyle=P(0)=1

\displaystyle q_{0}

\displaystyle=Q(0)=0.

In this case, the indicial equation has only the solution
\alpha=0
and as such there exist one solution for the differential equation which is analytic at
u=0
and one with a logarithmic singularity
[
31
, Section 10.32]
. By
U_{\lambda,\ell}(I)=1
and the fact that for
g=I
we have
u=0
, the function
U_{\lambda,\ell}
is uniquely determined to originate from the analytic solution. The claim (
5.8
) is then clear since the integrals on the right-hand side define a type
(\ell,\ell)
eigenfunction with eigenvalue
\lambda
.
∎

Lemma 5.9
.

Let
f
be an eigenfunction of
\Omega
with eigenvalue
\lambda
with right-type
-\ell
. Then we have

\displaystyle\mathcal{R}_{h,\ell}f(g)=U_{\lambda,-\ell}(h^{-1}g)f(h),

where
U_{\lambda,-\ell}
is as in Lemma
5.8
.

Proof.

By Lemma
5.6
we have

\displaystyle\frac{\mathcal{R}_{h,\ell}f(g)}{f(h)}=F(h^{-1}g,h)

with

(5.9)

\displaystyle F(1,h)=\frac{\mathcal{R}_{h,\ell}f(h)}{f(h)}=1

and with
F
having left and right type
-\ell
in the first coordinate. Recall the definition of
F(g_{1},g_{2})
in (
5.6
) and observe that

\displaystyle\Omega_{g_{1}}F(g_{1},g_{2})=\lambda F(g_{1},g_{2})

by the fact that the casimir element commutes with left group action. Thus, by (
5.9
) and Lemma
5.8
we have for any
g_{2}

\displaystyle F(g_{1},g_{2})=U_{\lambda,-\ell}(g_{1})

and so

\displaystyle\mathcal{R}_{h,\ell}f(g)=F(h^{-1}g,h)f(h)=U_{\lambda,-\ell}(h^{-1}g)f(h).

∎

Lemma 5.10
.

Let
f\in L^{2}(G)
be an eigenfunction of
\Omega
with eigenvalue
\lambda
with right-type
-\ell
. Then
f
is also an eigenfunction of
L_{\ell,\ell}
and we have

\displaystyle(L_{\ell,\ell}f)(g)=\Lambda(k,\lambda,\ell)f(g),

where
\Lambda
does not depend on
f
. Furthermore, for
\lambda=1/4-\nu^{2}
we have

(5.10)

\displaystyle\Lambda(k,\lambda,\ell)=\int_{G}k_{\ell,\ell}(h)\overline{\phi_{\ell}(h,\nu)}\mathrm{d}h.

Proof.

By Lemmas
5.7
and
5.9
we have

\displaystyle\Lambda

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{f(g)}(L_{\ell,\ell}f)(g)=\frac{1}{f(g)}(\mathcal{R}_{g,\ell}L_{\ell,\ell}f)(g)=\frac{1}{f(g)}(L_{\ell,\ell}\mathcal{R}_{g,\ell}f)(g)

\displaystyle=\int_{G}k_{\ell,\ell}(g^{-1}h)\frac{\mathcal{R}_{g,\ell}f(h)}{f(g)}\mathrm{d}h

\displaystyle=\int_{G}k_{\ell,\ell}(g^{-1}h)U_{\lambda,-\ell}(g^{-1}h)\mathrm{d}h.

This is independent of
f
and
g
by using the change of variables
h\mapsto gh
and thus depends only on
k,\lambda,\ell
. The last claim follows by applying this with
f(g)=\overline{\phi_{\ell}(g,\nu)}
and evaluating at
g=I
.
∎

5.2.
Proof of Proposition
5.2

Proof of Proposition
5.2
.

The function
\overline{f_{V,\ell}}(g)
is an eigenfunction with eigenvalue
1/4-\nu^{2}
and right-type
-\ell
. Therefore, by Lemma
5.10
and by (
5.10
) we get

\displaystyle L_{k;\ell,\ell}\overline{f_{V,\ell}}(g)=\Phi_{\ell,\ell}(k;\nu)\overline{f_{V,\ell}}(g).

∎

6.
A mean value theorem for automorphic forms on
\Gamma\backslash G

The main goal in this section is to prove the following proposition. It is the essential ingredient to obtain the
\mathcal{K}
terms in Theorems
1.1
and
7.1
.

Proposition 6.1
.

Let
K,L\geq 1.
There exists a function
k:G\to[0,1]
satisfying

\displaystyle k(\smallmatrixquantity(\lx@physics@smallmatrix a& b \\ c & d\endlx@physics@smallmatrix))\leq\mathbf{1}_{|a|+|b|+|c|+|d|\leq 6}

such that the following holds. Let
T\subseteq G
be a finite set and let
\alpha:T\to{\mathbb{C}}
be a function. Then

\displaystyle\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}V\in\mathcal{B}(q,\chi)\\
|\nu_{V}|\leq K\end{subarray}}\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}|\ell|\leq L\\
\ell\equiv\kappa\,\,(\mathrm{mod}\,{2})\end{subarray}}\bigg|\sum_{\tau\in T}\alpha(\tau)\varphi_{V}^{(\ell)}(\tau)\bigg|^{2}

\displaystyle+\sum_{\mathfrak{c}\in\mathfrak{C}(q,\chi)}\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}|\ell|\leq L\\
\ell\equiv\kappa\,\,(\mathrm{mod}\,{2})\end{subarray}}\int_{-iK}^{iK}\bigg|\sum_{\tau\in T}\alpha(\tau)E_{\mathfrak{c},\ell}(\tau,\nu)\bigg|^{2}|\mathrm{d}\nu|

\displaystyle\ll K^{2}L\sum_{\tau_{1},\tau_{2}\in T}\alpha(\tau_{1})\overline{\alpha(\tau_{2})}\sum_{\gamma\in\Gamma}\overline{\chi}(\gamma)k(\tau_{2}^{-1}\gamma\tau_{1}).

Remark 1
.

The above is a non-commutative analogue of the well-known bound

\displaystyle\sum_{|k|\leq K}\bigg|\sum_{m}\alpha_{m}e(kx_{m})\bigg|^{2}\ll K\sum_{m_{1},m_{2}}|\alpha_{m_{2}}\alpha_{m_{1}}|F(x_{m_{1}}-x_{m_{2}}),

where
F
is a bounded function supported on
\|x_{m_{1}}-x_{m_{2}}\|_{{\mathbb{R}}/{\mathbb{Z}}}<1/K
. In both of these the right-hand side measures the distribution of the points (
\tau
or
x_{m}
) inside a fundamental domain (
\Gamma\backslash G
or
{\mathbb{R}}/{\mathbb{Z}}
). Indeed this analogy is not too far fetched, as the proof below actually gives a stronger decay condition for
k
for large
K,L
. We do not pursue this as it is inconsequential for our applications. For instance, the upper bound
k(\smallmatrixquantity(\lx@physics@smallmatrix a& b \\ c & d\endlx@physics@smallmatrix))\leq\mathbf{1}_{|a|+|b|+|c|+|d|\leq 6}
may be strengthened to
k(\smallmatrixquantity(\lx@physics@smallmatrix a& b \\ c & d\endlx@physics@smallmatrix))\leq\mathbf{1}_{|a|^{2}+|b|^{2}+|c|^{2}+|d|^{2}\leq 2+1/K}
.

To prove Proposition
6.1
we need some standard lemmas. We define the
Abel transform
of a function
k:G\to{\mathbb{C}}
by

\displaystyle\mathcal{A}k:\mathrm{A}\to{\mathbb{C}}:\quad\mathcal{A}k(\mathrm{a}):=y^{1/2}\int_{{\mathbb{N}}}k(\mathrm{a}\mathrm{n})\mathrm{d}\mathrm{n},\quad\mathrm{a}=\mathrm{a}[y]

and the
Mellin transform
of a function
f:\mathrm{A}\to{\mathbb{C}}
for
s\in{\mathbb{C}}

\displaystyle\mathcal{M}f(s):=\int_{\mathrm{A}}f(\mathrm{a})y^{s}\mathrm{d}\mathrm{a}=\int_{0}^{\infty}f(\mathrm{a}[y])y^{s}\frac{\mathrm{d}y}{y}=\int_{\mathbb{R}}f(\mathrm{a}[e^{t}])e^{ts}\mathrm{d}t.

Recall the definition of the projection
k_{\ell,\ell}
given in (
5.1
).

Lemma 6.2
.

Let
\Phi_{\ell,\ell}(k;\nu)
be as in (
5.2
). Then we have

\displaystyle\Phi_{\ell,\ell}(k;\nu)=\mathcal{M}\mathcal{A}k_{\ell,\ell}(\overline{\nu}).

Proof.

Recall
\phi_{\ell}(\mathrm{a}[y]\mathrm{n}[x]\mathrm{k}[\theta])=\phi_{\ell}(\mathrm{n}[xy]\mathrm{a}[y]\mathrm{k}[\theta])=y^{1/2+\nu}e^{i\ell\theta}
so that by (
2.13
)

\displaystyle\Phi_{\ell,\ell}(k;\nu)=

\displaystyle\int_{G}k_{\ell,\ell}(h)\overline{\phi_{\ell}(h,\nu)}\mathrm{d}h=\int_{A}\int_{N}\int_{K}k_{\ell,\ell}(\mathrm{a}\mathrm{n}\mathrm{k})y^{1/2+\overline{\nu}}e^{-i\ell\theta}\mathrm{d}\mathrm{k}\mathrm{d}\mathrm{n}\mathrm{d}\mathrm{a}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\int_{A}y^{1/2}\int_{N}k_{\ell,\ell}(\mathrm{a}\mathrm{n})\mathrm{d}\mathrm{n}y^{\overline{\nu}}\mathrm{d}\mathrm{a}=\int_{A}\mathcal{A}k_{\ell,\ell}(\mathrm{a})y^{\overline{\nu}}\mathrm{d}\mathrm{a}=\mathcal{M}\mathcal{A}k_{\ell,\ell}(\overline{\nu}).

∎

For
f_{1},f_{2}\in L^{2}(G)
define the convolution

\displaystyle f_{1}\ast f_{2}(h):=\int_{G}f_{1}(g^{-1}h)f_{2}(g)dg=\int_{G}f_{1}(g^{-1})f_{2}(hg)\mathrm{d}g.

For functions
f_{1},f_{2}\in L^{2}(\mathrm{A})
we define the convolution via

\displaystyle f_{1}\ast_{\mathrm{A}}f_{2}(\mathrm{a}):=\int_{\mathrm{A}}f_{1}(\mathrm{a}_{0}^{-1}\mathrm{a})f_{2}(\mathrm{a}_{0})\mathrm{d}\mathrm{a}_{0}.

These convolutions interact with the Abel transform in the following way.

Lemma 6.3
.

Let
f_{1},f_{2}:G\to{\mathbb{C}}
and suppose that the left type of
f_{1}
is the same as the right type of
f_{2}
. Then

\displaystyle\mathcal{A}(f_{1}\ast f_{2})=\mathcal{A}f_{1}\ast_{\mathrm{A}}\mathcal{A}f_{2}.

Proof.

We have for
\mathrm{a}=\mathrm{a}[y]

\displaystyle y^{-1/2}\mathcal{A}(f_{1}\ast f_{2})(\mathrm{a})=

\displaystyle\int_{\mathrm{N}}(f_{1}\ast f_{2})(\mathrm{a}\mathrm{n})\mathrm{d}\mathrm{n}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\int_{\mathrm{N}}\int_{G}f_{1}(g^{-1}\mathrm{a}\mathrm{n})f_{2}(g)\mathrm{d}g\mathrm{d}\mathrm{n}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\int_{\mathrm{N}}\int_{G}f_{1}(g^{-1}\mathrm{n})f_{2}(\mathrm{a}g)\mathrm{d}g\mathrm{d}\mathrm{n}.

Using the Iwasawa decomposition
g=\mathrm{a}_{0}\mathrm{n}_{0}\mathrm{k}_{0}
and the type assumption we get (denoting
\mathrm{a}_{0}=\mathrm{a}[y_{0}]
)

\displaystyle y^{-1/2}\mathcal{A}(f_{1}\ast f_{2})(\mathrm{a})

\displaystyle=\int_{\mathrm{N}}\int_{\mathrm{A}}\int_{\mathrm{N}}\int_{\mathrm{K}}f_{1}(\mathrm{k}_{0}^{-1}\mathrm{n_{0}}^{-1}\mathrm{a_{0}}^{-1}\mathrm{n})f_{2}(\mathrm{a}\mathrm{a_{0}}\mathrm{n_{0}}\mathrm{k}_{0})\mathrm{d}\mathrm{k}_{0}\mathrm{d}\mathrm{n}_{0}\mathrm{d}\mathrm{a_{0}}\mathrm{d}\mathrm{n}

\displaystyle=\int_{\mathrm{N}}\int_{\mathrm{A}}\int_{\mathrm{N}}f_{1}(\mathrm{n_{0}}^{-1}\mathrm{a_{0}}^{-1}\mathrm{n})f_{2}(\mathrm{a}\mathrm{a_{0}}\mathrm{n_{0}})\mathrm{d}\mathrm{n}_{0}\mathrm{d}\mathrm{a_{0}}\mathrm{d}\mathrm{n}

\displaystyle=\int_{\mathrm{N}}\int_{\mathrm{A}}\int_{\mathrm{N}}y_{0}^{-1}f_{1}(\mathrm{n_{0}}^{-1}\mathrm{n}\mathrm{a_{0}}^{-1})f_{2}(\mathrm{a}\mathrm{a_{0}}\mathrm{n_{0}})\mathrm{d}\mathrm{n}_{0}\mathrm{d}\mathrm{a_{0}}\mathrm{d}\mathrm{n}

\displaystyle=\int_{\mathrm{N}}\int_{\mathrm{A}}\int_{\mathrm{N}}y_{0}^{-1}f_{1}(\mathrm{n}\mathrm{a_{0}}^{-1})f_{2}(\mathrm{a}\mathrm{a_{0}}\mathrm{n_{0}})\mathrm{d}\mathrm{n}_{0}\mathrm{d}\mathrm{a_{0}}\mathrm{d}\mathrm{n}

\displaystyle=\int_{\mathrm{N}}\int_{\mathrm{A}}\int_{\mathrm{N}}f_{1}(\mathrm{a_{0}}^{-1}\mathrm{n})f_{2}(\mathrm{a}\mathrm{a_{0}}\mathrm{n_{0}})\mathrm{d}\mathrm{n}_{0}\mathrm{d}\mathrm{a_{0}}\mathrm{d}\mathrm{n}

\displaystyle=\int_{\mathrm{N}}\int_{\mathrm{A}}\int_{\mathrm{N}}f_{1}(\mathrm{a_{0}}^{-1}\mathrm{a}\mathrm{n})f_{2}(\mathrm{a_{0}}\mathrm{n_{0}})\mathrm{d}\mathrm{n}_{0}\mathrm{d}\mathrm{a_{0}}\mathrm{d}\mathrm{n}.

Thus, we have

\displaystyle\mathcal{A}(f_{1}\ast f_{2})(\mathrm{a})=

\displaystyle\int_{\mathrm{A}}y^{1/2}\int_{\mathrm{N}}f_{1}(\mathrm{a_{0}}^{-1}\mathrm{a}\mathrm{n})\mathrm{d}\mathrm{n}\int_{\mathrm{N}}f_{2}(\mathrm{a_{0}}\mathrm{n_{0}})\mathrm{d}\mathrm{n}_{0}\mathrm{d}\mathrm{a_{0}}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\int_{\mathrm{A}}\bigg((y/y_{0})^{1/2}\int_{\mathrm{N}}f_{1}(\mathrm{a_{0}}^{-1}\mathrm{a}\mathrm{n})\mathrm{d}\mathrm{n}\bigg)\bigg(y_{0}^{1/2}\int_{\mathrm{N}}f_{2}(\mathrm{a_{0}}\mathrm{n_{0}})\mathrm{d}\mathrm{n}_{0}\bigg)\mathrm{d}\mathrm{a_{0}}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\mathcal{A}f_{1}\ast_{\mathrm{A}}\mathcal{A}f_{2}(\mathrm{a}).

∎

The above lemmas allow us to construct a non-negative
\Phi
-function via self-convolution of a kernel.

Lemma 6.4
.

Let
k_{0}\in L^{2}(G)
be a real valued function with
k_{0}(\mathrm{k}g\mathrm{k}^{-1})=k_{0}(g)
and
k_{0}(g^{-1})=k_{0}(g)
. Then for
k=k_{0}\ast k_{0}
we have for
\nu\in{\mathbb{R}}\cup i{\mathbb{R}}

\displaystyle\Phi_{\ell,\ell}(k,\nu)=|\Phi_{\ell,\ell}(k_{0},\nu)|^{2}.

Proof.

By
k_{0}(\mathrm{k}g\mathrm{k}^{-1})=k_{0}(g)
we have

\displaystyle k_{0}(g)=\sum_{\ell\in{\mathbb{Z}}}k_{0,\ell,\ell}(g),\quad k(g)=\sum_{\ell\in{\mathbb{Z}}}k_{0,\ell,\ell}\ast k_{0,\ell,\ell}(g),

since for
\ell\neq\ell^{\prime}
we have by Cartan decomposition
k_{0,\ell^{\prime},\ell^{\prime}}\ast k_{0,\ell,\ell}\equiv 0
. Hence, we have
k_{\ell,\ell}=k_{0,\ell,\ell}\ast k_{0,\ell,\ell}
and by Lemmas
6.2
and
6.3
we get

\displaystyle\Phi_{\ell,\ell}(k,\nu)=\mathcal{M}\mathcal{A}(k_{0,\ell,\ell}\ast k_{0,\ell,\ell})(\overline{\nu})=\mathcal{M}(\mathcal{A}k_{0,\ell,\ell}\ast_{\mathrm{A}}\mathcal{A}k_{0,\ell,\ell}))(\overline{\nu})=(\mathcal{M}\mathcal{A}k_{0,\ell,\ell}(\overline{\nu}))^{2},

where
\mathcal{M}\mathcal{A}k_{0,\ell,\ell}(\overline{\nu})=\mathcal{M}\mathcal{A}k_{0,\ell,\ell}(\nu)
is real valued by the assumption that
k_{0}(g^{-1})=k_{0}(g)
.
∎

Next we have a simple statement relating sizes of Iwasawa coordinates and Cartan coordinates near the identity.

Lemma 6.5
.

Let
K>1
and

\displaystyle\mathrm{a}[e^{t}]\mathrm{n}[x]=\mathrm{k}[\varphi]\mathrm{a}[e^{-\varrho}]\mathrm{k}[\vartheta].

If
|\varrho|\leq 1/K
, then
|t|\ll 1/K
and
|x|\ll 1/K
. Similarly, if
|t|\leq 1/K
and
|x|\leq 1/K
, then
|\varrho|\ll 1/K

Proof.

By (
2.5
) we have for
y=e^{t}

\displaystyle 1\leq\cosh t\leq\cosh t+e^{t}x^{2}/2=\cosh\varrho\leq 1+O(1/K^{2}).

By
\cosh t=1+t^{2}/2+O(t^{4})
this implies that
|t|\ll 1/K
and
|x|\ll 1/K
. The other direction follows similarly.
∎

Proof of Proposition
6.1
.

We construct a smooth compactly supported function
k:G\to{\mathbb{C}}
such that
\Phi_{\ell,\ell}(k,\nu)
takes non-negative real values and

\displaystyle\Phi_{\ell,\ell}(k,\nu)\geq\mathbf{1}_{|\nu|\leq K}\mathbf{1}_{|\ell|\leq L}.

By Lemma
6.4
it suffices to choose
k=k_{0}*k_{0}
such that
\Phi_{\ell,\ell}(k_{0},\nu)\geq 1
for
|\nu|\leq K,|\ell|\leq L
.
We let
C>0
denote a large constant and choose

\displaystyle k_{0}(g):=C^{4}K^{2}Lf(\varrho)F(\varphi+\vartheta),\quad g=\mathrm{k}[\varphi]\mathrm{a}[e^{-\varrho}]\mathrm{k}[\vartheta],

where
f:{\mathbb{R}}\to[0,1]
is a smooth even function with

\displaystyle f(x)

\displaystyle=1,\,x\in[-(2CK)^{-1},(2CK)^{-1}],\quad\mathrm{supp}(f)\subseteq[-(CK)^{-1},(CK)^{-1}],

and
F:{\mathbb{R}}\to[0,1]
is a
2\pi
-periodic even function with

\displaystyle F(x)

\displaystyle=1,\,x\in[-(2CL)^{-1},(2CL)^{-1}],

\displaystyle F(x+\pi)

\displaystyle=(-1)^{\kappa}F(x),

\displaystyle\mathrm{supp}(F)

\displaystyle\subseteq[-(CL)^{-1},(CL)^{-1}]\cup\bigg(\pi+[-(CL)^{-1},(CL)^{-1}]\bigg).

Then

\displaystyle k_{0,\ell,\ell}(g)=C^{4}K^{2}L\widehat{F}(\ell)e^{i\ell(\varphi+\vartheta)}f(\varrho),\quad\quad\widehat{F}(\ell)=\frac{1}{2\pi}\int_{-\pi}^{\pi}F(\theta)e^{-i\ell\theta}\mathrm{d}\theta.

By Taylor approximation we have for
|\ell|\leq L
with
\ell\equiv\kappa\,\,(\mathrm{mod}\,{2})

\displaystyle\widehat{F}(\ell)

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{2\pi}\int_{-\pi}^{\pi}F(\theta)e^{-i\ell\theta}\mathrm{d}\theta=\frac{1}{\pi}\int_{-(CL)^{-1}}^{(CL)^{-1}}F(\theta)(1+O(L\theta))\mathrm{d}\theta

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{\pi}\int_{-(CL)^{-1}}^{(CL)^{-1}}F(\theta)(1+O(1/C))\mathrm{d}\theta\geq\frac{1}{\pi}\int_{-(2CL)^{-1}}^{(2CL)^{-1}}1\mathrm{d}\theta+O(C^{-2}L^{-1})\geq(4CL)^{-1}

if
C
is sufficiently large. Furthermore,
\widehat{F}(\ell)=0
for
\ell\not\equiv\kappa\,\,(\mathrm{mod}\,{2})
.

Similarly, for
|\varrho|\leq(CK)^{-1}
with
\mathrm{a}[e^{t}]\mathrm{n}[x]=\mathrm{k}_{1}\mathrm{a}[e^{-\varrho}]\mathrm{k}_{2}
,
y=e^{t}
, we have by Lemma
6.5
that for sufficiently large
C

\displaystyle\max\{|t|,|x|\}\leq(C^{3/4}K)^{-1}.

Therefore, for
|\nu|\leq K
we get by Taylor approximation

\displaystyle\int_{A}\int_{N}f(\varrho)y^{1/2+\nu}\mathrm{d}\mathrm{n}\mathrm{d}\mathrm{a}

\displaystyle=\int_{{\mathbb{R}}}\int_{\mathbb{R}}f(\varrho)e^{t(1/2+\nu)}\mathrm{d}x\mathrm{d}t

\displaystyle=\int_{-(C^{3/4}K)^{-1}}^{(C^{3/4}K)^{-1}}\int_{-(C^{3/4}K)^{-1}}^{(C^{3/4}K)^{-1}}f(\varrho)e^{t(1/2+\nu)}\mathrm{d}x\mathrm{d}t

\displaystyle=\int_{-(C^{3/4}K)^{-1}}^{(C^{3/4}K)^{-1}}\int_{-(C^{3/4}K)^{-1}}^{(C^{3/4}K)^{-1}}f(\varrho)(1+O(C^{-3/4}))\mathrm{d}x\mathrm{d}t

\displaystyle\geq\int_{-(C^{3/4}K)^{-1}}^{(C^{3/4}K)^{-1}}\int_{-(C^{3/4}K)^{-1}}^{(C^{3/4}K)^{-1}}\mathbf{1}_{|\varrho|\leq(2CK)^{-1}}\mathrm{d}x\mathrm{d}t+O(C^{-9/4}K^{-2})

\displaystyle\gg(CK)^{-2},

where the last bound holds for
C
sufficiently large. Combining the above, we have for
|\ell|\leq L,|\nu|\leq K
that

\displaystyle\Phi_{\ell,\ell}(k_{0},\nu)=C^{4}K^{2}L\widehat{F}(l)\int_{A}\int_{N}f(\varrho)y^{1/2+\nu}\mathrm{d}\mathrm{n}\mathrm{d}\mathrm{a}\geq C^{1/2}\geq 1,

if we, once more, assume that
C
is sufficiently large. By positivity and Proposition
5.1
we get

\displaystyle\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}V\in\mathcal{B}(q,\chi)\\
|\nu_{V}|\leq K\end{subarray}}\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}|\ell|\leq L\\
\ell\equiv\kappa\,\,(\mathrm{mod}\,{2})\end{subarray}}\bigg|\sum_{\tau\in T}\alpha(\tau)\varphi_{V}^{(\ell)}(\tau)\bigg|^{2}+\sum_{\mathfrak{c}\in\mathfrak{C}(q,\chi)}\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}|\ell|\leq L\\
\ell\equiv\kappa\,\,(\mathrm{mod}\,{2})\end{subarray}}\frac{1}{4\pi}\int_{-iK}^{iK}\bigg|\sum_{\tau\in T}\alpha(\tau)E_{\mathfrak{c},\ell}(\tau,\nu)\bigg|^{2}|\mathrm{d}\nu|

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle\,\frac{\mathbf{1}_{\chi\,\mathrm{principal}}\mathbf{1}_{\ell=0}}{|\Gamma\backslash G|}\int_{G}k(g)\mathrm{d}g+\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}V\in\mathcal{B}(q,\chi)\end{subarray}}\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}\ell\equiv\kappa\,\,(\mathrm{mod}\,{2})\end{subarray}}\Phi_{\ell,\ell}(k,\nu)\bigg|\sum_{\tau\in T}\alpha(\tau)\varphi_{V}^{(\ell)}(\tau)\bigg|^{2}

\displaystyle\hskip 80.0pt+\sum_{\mathfrak{c}\in\mathfrak{C}(q,\chi)}\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}\ell\equiv\kappa\,\,(\mathrm{mod}\,{2})\end{subarray}}\frac{1}{4\pi i}\int_{(0)}\Phi_{\ell,\ell}(k,\nu)\bigg|\sum_{\tau\in T}\alpha(\tau)E_{\mathfrak{c},\ell}(\tau,\nu)\bigg|^{2}\mathrm{d}\nu

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\sum_{\tau_{1},\tau_{2}\in T}\alpha(\tau_{1})\overline{\alpha(\tau_{2})}\sum_{\gamma\in\Gamma}\overline{\chi}(\gamma)k(\tau_{2}^{-1}\gamma\tau_{1}).

It remains to show that

\displaystyle k(g)\ll\mathbf{1}_{|a|+|b|+|c|+|d|\leq 6}.

Denoting
g=\mathrm{k}[\varphi]\mathrm{a}[e^{-\varrho}]\mathrm{k}[\vartheta],h=\mathrm{k}[\varphi_{0}]\mathrm{a}[e^{-\varrho_{0}}]\mathrm{k}[\vartheta_{0}],

k_{1}(g)=f(\varrho)F(\varphi+\vartheta)
, we have

\displaystyle k(g)

\displaystyle=(k_{0}\ast k_{0})(g)=C^{8}K^{4}L^{2}\int_{G}k_{1}(h^{-1}g)k_{1}(h)\mathrm{d}h

\displaystyle\leq C^{8}K^{4}L^{2}\int_{G}f(h^{-1}g)k_{1}(h)\mathrm{d}h

\displaystyle\ll C^{8}K^{4}L^{2}\int_{K\times K}\int_{{\mathbb{R}}_{>0}}f(\varrho^{\prime})F(\varphi_{0}+\vartheta_{0})f(\varrho_{0})\sinh(\varrho_0)\mathrm{d}\varrho_{0}\mathrm{d}\mathrm{k}[\varphi_{0}]\mathrm{d}\mathrm{k}[\vartheta_{0}]

\displaystyle\ll C^{7}K^{4}L\int_{K}\int_{{\mathbb{R}}_{>0}}f(\varrho^{\prime})f(\varrho_{0})\sinh(\varrho_0)\mathrm{d}\varrho_{0}\mathrm{d}\mathrm{k}[\varphi_{0}],

where for some
\mathrm{k}_{1},\mathrm{k}_{1}^{\prime},\mathrm{k}_{2}^{\prime}
we have

\displaystyle\mathrm{k}_{1}^{\prime}\mathrm{a}[e^{-\varrho}]\mathrm{k}_{2}^{\prime}=\mathrm{a}[e^{-\varrho_{0}}]\mathrm{k}_{1}\mathrm{a}[e^{-\varrho^{\prime}}].

Here
\varrho_{0},\varrho^{\prime}
are supported on
[-(CK)^{-1},(CK)^{-1}]
and

\displaystyle\cosh(\varrho)

\displaystyle=1+2u(\mathrm{a}[e^{-\varrho_{0}}]\mathrm{k}_{1}\mathrm{a}[e^{-\varrho^{\prime}}]i,i)=1+2u(\mathrm{k}_{1}\mathrm{a}[e^{-\varrho^{\prime}}]i,\mathrm{a}[e^{\varrho_{0}}]i)

\displaystyle=1+2u(\mathrm{k}_{1}e^{-\varrho^{\prime}}i,e^{\varrho_{0}}i)=1+2u(i+O((CK)^{-1}),i+O((CK)^{-1}))

\displaystyle=1+O((CK)^{-2}),

which implies that
k(g)
is supported on
|\varrho|\ll(CK)^{-1}
.
Therefore, using
\sinh(\varrho_0)\ll|\varrho_{0}|
we get

\displaystyle k(g)\ll C^{5}K^{2}L\mathbf{1}_{|\varrho|\ll(CK)^{-1}}.

Taking
C
sufficiently large this is by (
2.6
) supported on
a^{2}+b^{2}+c^{2}+d^{2}\leq 2+1/100
which implies
|a|+|b|+|c|+|d|\leq 6
since
4\cdot\sqrt{2+1/100}<6
.
∎

7.
Discrete average of Poincaré series on
\Gamma\backslash G

The goal in this section is to combine the previous sections to prove the following technical result, which is a precursor of Theorems
1.1
and
10.1
. Recall that

\displaystyle\Gamma_{2}(q_{1},q_{2})=\bigg\{\matrixquantity(\lx@physics@matrix a & b \\ c &d\endlx@physics@matrix)\in\operatorname{SL}_{2}({\mathbb{Z}}):q_{1}|b,\,q_{2}|c\bigg\}=\mathrm{a}[q_{1}]\Gamma_{0}(q_{1}q_{2})\mathrm{a}[q_{1}]^{-1}.

For this group we define the Hecke operator by

(7.1)

\displaystyle\mathcal{T}_{h}f(g):=\frac{1}{\sqrt{h}}\sum_{ad=h}\chi(a)\sum_{b\,\,(\mathrm{mod}\,{d})}f\bigg(\frac{1}{\sqrt{h}}\matrixquantity(\lx@physics@matrix a & b q_1 \\ & d\endlx@physics@matrix)g\bigg),

which by conjugation by
\mathrm{a}[q_{1}]
corresponds to the one for
\Gamma_{0}(q_{1}q_{2})
defined in (
3.5
). For
V\in\mathcal{B}(q,\chi)
,
t>0
, and coefficients
\beta_{h}
we denote

(7.2)

\displaystyle R_{V}(t,\beta):=\sum_{\sigma\in\{\pm\}}\sum_{\gcd(h,q)=1}\beta_{h}\lambda_{V}(h)\sum_{0<|n|\leq t}\varrho_{V}(\sigma n)

and similarly for
\nu\in{\mathbb{C}}
and
\mathfrak{c}\in\mathfrak{C}(q,\chi)
define

(7.3)

\displaystyle R_{\mathfrak{c},\nu}(t,\beta):=\sum_{\sigma\in\{\pm\}}\sum_{\gcd(h,q)=1}\beta_{h}\lambda_{\mathfrak{c},\nu}(h)\sum_{0<|n|\leq t}\varrho_{\mathfrak{c},\nu}(\sigma n).

Theorem 7.1
.

Let
\Gamma=\Gamma_{2}(q_{1},q_{2})
for some
q_{1},q_{2}\geq 1
,
q=q_{1}q_{2}
, and let
T\subseteq G=\operatorname{SL}_{2}({\mathbb{R}})
be a finite set. Let
\alpha:T\to{\mathbb{C}}
and
\beta:{\mathbb{Z}}_{>0}\to{\mathbb{C}}
. Let
\chi
be a Dirichlet character to the modulus
q
and denote the corresponding group character on
\Gamma_{2}(q_{1},q_{2})
by
\overline{\chi}(\smallmatrixquantity(\lx@physics@smallmatrix a & b \\ c & d\endlx@physics@smallmatrix)):=\overline{\chi}(d)
.

Let
A,C,D>0
,
\delta\in(0,1)
with
AD>\delta
. Let
J\geq 3
,
f\in C_{\delta}^{2J+1}(A,C,D)
and let
F:G\to{\mathbb{C}}
denote
F(\smallmatrixquantity(\lx@physics@smallmatrix a&b \\ c& d\endlx@physics@smallmatrix))=f(a,c,d)
.
Denote

\displaystyle P_{f}(\tau):=\sum_{\gamma\in\Gamma}\overline{\chi}(\gamma)F(\gamma\tau),

\displaystyle S_{f,\alpha,\beta,\chi}(\Gamma,T):=\sum_{\tau\in T}\sum_{\gcd(h,q)=1}\alpha(\tau)\beta_{h}\mathcal{T}_{h}P_{f}(\tau),

and

(7.4)

\displaystyle M_{f,\alpha,\beta,\chi}(\Gamma,T)

\displaystyle:=\mathbf{1}_{\chi\,\mathrm{principal}}\bigg(\sum_{\gcd(h,q)=1}\frac{\beta_{h}\sigma_{1}(h)}{\sqrt{h}}\bigg)\bigg(\sum_{\tau\in T}\alpha(\tau)\bigg)\frac{1}{|\Gamma\backslash G|}\int_{G}F(g)\mathrm{d}g.

Let

\displaystyle Z:=\max\{A^{\pm 1},C^{\pm 1},D^{\pm 1},\delta^{-1}\}.

Define the decay function

\displaystyle E_{J}(t,\nu):=\frac{1}{(1+|t|+|\nu|)^{2J-1}}.

Then for
L:=C/D
,
N:=q_{1}\frac{CD}{AD+1},
and for any
\varepsilon>0
we have

(7.5)

\displaystyle|S_{f,\alpha,\beta,\chi}(\Gamma,T)-M_{f,\alpha,\beta,\chi}(\Gamma,T)|\ll_{\varepsilon,J}Z^{\varepsilon}\delta^{-O(1)}(AD)^{1/2}\mathcal{K}_{\alpha,\chi}(\Gamma,T,L)^{1/2}\mathcal{R}_{\beta,\chi}(q_{1},q_{2},N)^{1/2},

where

\displaystyle\mathcal{R}_{\beta,\chi}(q_{1},q_{2},N):=

\displaystyle\max_{t>0}\frac{1}{N}\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}V\in\mathcal{B}(q,\chi)\end{subarray}}E_{J}(t/N,\nu_{V})\bigg(1+\mathbf{1}_{\operatorname{Re}(\nu_{V})<1/2}\bigg(\frac{CDq_{1}}{t}\bigg)^{2\operatorname{Re}(\nu_{V})}\bigg)|R_{V}(t,\beta)|^{2}

\displaystyle+\max_{t>0}\frac{1}{N}\sum_{\mathfrak{c}\in\mathfrak{C}(q,\chi)}\int_{(0)}E_{J}(t/N,\nu)|R_{\mathfrak{c},\nu}(t,\beta)|^{2}|\mathrm{d}\nu|\,+\,\|\beta\|_{1}^{2}N^{-1}

with the two
R
terms given by (
7.2
) and (
7.3
).
Further

\displaystyle\mathcal{K}_{\alpha,\chi}(\Gamma,T,L):=\sum_{\tau_{1},\tau_{2}\in T}\alpha(\tau_{1})\overline{\alpha(\tau_{2})}\sum_{\gamma\in\Gamma}\overline{\chi}(\gamma)k_{L}(\tau_{2}^{-1}\gamma\tau_{1})

for some
k_{L}:G\to[0,1]
which satisfies

\displaystyle k_{L}(\smallmatrixquantity(\lx@physics@smallmatrix a& b \\ c & d\endlx@physics@smallmatrix))\leq\mathbf{1}_{|a|+|b|L+|c|/L+|d|\leq 6}.

Remark 2
.

Here we have assumed that
(h,q)=1
. The case
(h,q)>1
can be sometimes be reduced to the coprime case. For instance, if
(h,q)=r
, then

\displaystyle\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}ad-bc=h\\
c\equiv 0\,\,(\mathrm{mod}\,{q})\end{subarray}}f(a,c,d)=\sum_{r_{1}r_{2}=r}\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}ad-bc=h/r\\
c\equiv 0\,(q/r)\end{subarray}}f(ar_{1},cr,dr_{2}).

Another option is to factorize
h=kh^{\prime}
with
k=(h,q^{\infty})
and incorporate for
k
the Hecke orbits into the set of orbits
T
, as is done in the proof of Theorem
10.1
.

Note that the ranges
A,C,D
can be smaller than
1
, since in practice they correspond to the normalized variables
a/\sqrt{h},c/\sqrt{h},d/\sqrt{h}
for the determinant equation
ad-bc=h
. It is even possible that
AD<1
, in the case that
h
is bigger than the original range for
ad
.

We begin the proof of Theorem
7.1
by first reducing to the case
\Gamma=\Gamma_{0}(q)
and
L=1
.

7.1.
Reduction to the case
\Gamma=\Gamma_{0}(q)

Recall that

(7.6)

\displaystyle\Gamma_{2}(q_{1},q_{2})=\mathrm{a}[q_{1}]\Gamma_{0}(q_{1}q_{2})\mathrm{a}[1/q_{1}]

Thus, denoting

\displaystyle T_{0}:=\mathrm{a}[1/q_{1}]T,\quad f_{0}(a,c,d)=F_{0}(g):=F(\mathrm{a}[q_{1}]g)=f(a\sqrt{q_{1}},c/\sqrt{q_{1}},d/\sqrt{q_{1}}),\quad\alpha_{0}(\tau)=\alpha(\mathrm{a}[q_{1}]\tau),

\displaystyle A_{0}=\frac{A}{\sqrt{q_{1}}},\quad C_{0}=C\sqrt{q_{1}},\quad D_{0}=D\sqrt{q_{1}},\quad L_{0}=L=C/D,\quad N_{0}=\frac{C_{0}D_{0}}{A_{0}D_{0}+1}=\frac{CDq_{1}}{AD+1}=N,

we have

\displaystyle P_{f}(\tau):=\sum_{\gamma\in\Gamma_{2}(q_{1},q_{2})}\overline{\chi}(\gamma)F(\gamma\tau)=\sum_{\gamma\in\Gamma_{0}(q_{1}q_{2})}\overline{\chi}(\gamma)F(\mathrm{a}[q_{1}]\gamma\mathrm{a}[1/q_{1}]\tau)=P_{f_{0}}(\tau_{0}),\quad\tau_{0}\in T_{0},

where
f_{0}
satisfies the assumptions of Theorem
7.1
with
(A,C,D)
replaced by
(A_{0},C_{0},D_{0})

Thus, the case
\Gamma=\Gamma_{2}(q_{1},q_{2})
follows from the case
\Gamma=\Gamma_{0}(q_{1}q_{2})
, after observing that by (
7.6
)

\displaystyle\mathcal{K}_{\alpha_{0},\chi}(\Gamma_{0}(q_{1}q_{2}),T_{0},L_{0})=\mathcal{K}_{\alpha,\chi}(\Gamma_{2}(q_{1},q_{2}),T,L).

Note also that the conjugation by
\mathrm{a}[q_{1}]
maps the Hecke operator (
7.1
) for
\Gamma_{2}(q_{1},q_{2})
to the one defined in (
3.5
) for
\Gamma_{0}(q_{1}q_{2})
.

7.2.
Rescaling the smooth weight

The next step of the proof is to rescale the weight such that it suffices to consider the case
C=D
. This has as consequence that in the sequel we only need to consider
\ell
small, more precisely,
|\ell|\ll\delta^{-1}Z^{\varepsilon}
. Without this rescaling, losses from
L
as in Proposition
6.1
would be too costly for many applications.

Recall we are given

\displaystyle F(g)=f(a,c,d),\quad g=\matrixquantity(\lx@physics@matrix a & b \\ c& d\endlx@physics@matrix)

with
f(a,c,d)
supported for
(|a|,|c|,|d|)\in[A,2A]\times[C,2C]\times[D,2D]
, satisfying

\partial_{a}^{J_{1}}\partial_{c}^{J_{2}}\partial_{d}^{J_{3}}f\ll_{J_{1},J_{2},J_{3}}(\delta A)^{-J_{1}}(\delta C)^{-J_{2}}(\delta D)^{-J_{3}}

for any choice of
J_{1},J_{2},J_{3}\leq 4J+1
.
Let
L:=C/D
and define

\displaystyle F_{0}(g):=F(g\mathrm{a}[L]),

then

\displaystyle F_{0}(g)=f(a\sqrt{L},c\sqrt{L},d/\sqrt{L})

is supported on
[A_{0},2A_{0}]\times[C_{0},2C_{0}]\times[D_{0},2D_{0}]
with

\displaystyle A_{0}

\displaystyle=A/\sqrt{L}

\displaystyle C_{0}

\displaystyle=C/\sqrt{L}

\displaystyle D_{0}

\displaystyle=D\sqrt{L}.

In particular
C_{0}/D_{0}=1
and we still have

(7.7)

\displaystyle\partial_{a}^{J_{1}}\partial_{c}^{J_{2}}\partial_{d}^{J_{3}}F_{0}\ll_{J_{1},J_{2},J_{3}}(\delta A_{0})^{-J_{1}}(\delta C_{0})^{-J_{2}}(\delta D_{0})^{-J_{3}}.

Then

\displaystyle P_{f}(\tau)=P_{f_{0}}(\tau\mathrm{a}[L^{-1}]),

so that by replacing the set
T
with
T\mathrm{a}[L^{-1}]
, we see that
P_{f_{0}}
and
T\mathrm{a}[L^{-1}]
fulfill the conditions of Theorem
7.1
with
L=1
. Note that
AD
,
CD
, and
N=\frac{CD}{AD+1}
are all invariant under the above rescaling of
L
. Furthermore,
k_{L}(g)
is of the form

\displaystyle k_{L}(g)=k_{1}(\mathrm{a}[L]g\mathrm{a}[L^{-1}])

with

\displaystyle k_{1}(\smallmatrixquantity(\lx@physics@smallmatrix a & b \\ c& d\endlx@physics@smallmatrix))\ll 1_{|a|+|b|+|c|+|d|\leq 6}.

Thus, it suffices to prove Theorem
7.1
with
L=C/D=1
.

7.3.
Proof of Theorem
7.1
for
\Gamma=\Gamma_{0}(q)
and
L=C/D=1

Recall that the Iwasawa coordinates of
g=\smallmatrixquantity(\lx@physics@smallmatrix a & b \\ c & d\endlx@physics@smallmatrix)
are given by (
2.1
)

\displaystyle x=\frac{ac+bd}{c^{2}+d^{2}},\quad y=\frac{1}{c^{2}+d^{2}},\quad\theta=\arctan(-c/d).

We claim that
F(g)=F(\mathrm{n}[x]\mathrm{a}[y]\mathrm{k}[\theta])
satisfies the assumptions of Proposition
4.1
with

\displaystyle X\asymp\frac{AD+1}{CD}=\frac{1}{N}\quad\text{and}\quad Y\asymp\frac{1}{CD},\quad\delta\mapsto\delta_{1}:=\min\{\delta,\delta AD\}\geq\delta^{2}.

Indeed, denoting
(a,c,d)=(\phi_{1}(x,\theta,y),\phi_{2}(x,\theta,y),\phi_{3}(x,\theta,y))
, using (
2.2
) we compute for any
J_{1}+J_{2}+J_{3}\leq 2J+1

\displaystyle\partial_{x}^{J_{1}}\partial_{y}^{J_{2}}\partial_{\theta}^{J_{3}}F(\mathrm{n}[x]\mathrm{a}[y]\mathrm{k}[\theta])=

\displaystyle\bigg(\frac{\partial\phi_{1}}{\partial x}\partial_{a}+\frac{\partial\phi_{2}}{\partial x}\partial_{c}+\frac{\partial\phi_{3}}{\partial x}\partial_{d}\bigg)^{J_{1}}\bigg(\frac{\partial\phi_{1}}{\partial y}\partial_{a}+\frac{\partial\phi_{2}}{\partial y}\partial_{c}+\frac{\partial\phi_{3}}{\partial y}\partial_{d}\bigg)^{J_{2}}

\displaystyle\times\bigg(\frac{\partial\phi_{1}}{\partial\theta}\partial_{a}+\frac{\partial\phi_{2}}{\partial\theta}\partial_{c}+\frac{\partial\phi_{3}}{\partial\theta}\partial_{d}\bigg)^{J_{3}}f(a,c,d)

\displaystyle\ll_{J_{1},J_{2},J_{3}}

\displaystyle\delta^{-J_{1}}\bigg(\frac{1}{Y^{1/2}A}\bigg)^{J_{1}}\delta^{-J_{2}}\bigg(\frac{1}{Y^{1/2}A}+\frac{X}{Y^{3/2}A}+\frac{1}{Y^{3/2}C}+\frac{1}{Y^{3/2}D}\bigg)^{J_{2}}

\displaystyle\times\delta^{-J_{3}}\bigg(\frac{Y^{1/2}}{A}+\frac{X}{Y^{1/2}A}+\frac{1}{Y^{1/2}C}+\frac{1}{Y^{1/2}D}\bigg)^{J_{3}}

\displaystyle\ll_{J_{1},J_{2},J_{3}}

\displaystyle\delta^{-J_{1}}\bigg(\frac{C}{A}\bigg)^{J_{1}}\delta^{-J_{2}}\bigg(\frac{C}{A}+CD\bigg)^{J_{2}}\delta^{-J_{3}}\bigg(1+\frac{1}{AD}\bigg)^{J_{3}}

\displaystyle\ll_{J_{1},J_{2},J_{3}}

\displaystyle(\delta_{1}X)^{-J_{1}}(\delta_{1}Y)^{-J_{2}}(\delta_{1})^{-J_{3}},

making use of
C=D
and denoting
\delta_{1}:=\min\{\delta,\delta AD\}
. Note also
Y/X\leq 1
.

We now apply spectral expansion for
P_{f}
. By Proposition
3.1
we have

\displaystyle P_{f}(g)=

\displaystyle\mathbf{1}_{\chi\,\mathrm{principal}}\frac{1}{|\Gamma\backslash G|}\langle P_{f},1\rangle_{\Gamma\backslash G}+\sum_{V\in\mathcal{B}(q,\chi)}\sum_{\ell\equiv\kappa\,\,(\mathrm{mod}\,{2})}\langle P_{f},\varphi_{V}^{(\ell)}\rangle_{\Gamma\backslash G}\varphi_{V}^{(\ell)}(g)

\displaystyle+\sum_{\mathfrak{c}\in\mathfrak{C}(q,\chi)}\sum_{\ell\equiv\kappa\,\,(\mathrm{mod}\,{2})}\frac{1}{4\pi i}\int_{(0)}\langle P_{f},E^{(\ell)}_{\mathfrak{c}}(\cdot,\nu)\rangle_{\Gamma\backslash G}E^{(\ell)}_{\mathfrak{c}}(g,\nu)\mathrm{d}\nu.

Therefore, we get

(7.8)

\displaystyle\begin{split}S_{f,\alpha,\beta,\chi}(\Gamma,T)=&\sum_{\tau\in T}\sum_{\gcd(h,q)=1}\alpha(\tau)\frac{\beta_{h}\sigma_{1}(h)}{\sqrt{h}}\mathbf{1}_{\chi\,\mathrm{principal}}\frac{1}{|\Gamma\backslash G|}\langle P_{f},1\rangle_{\Gamma\backslash G}+\mathcal{C}_{f,\alpha,\beta,\chi}(\Gamma,T)\\
&+\mathcal{E}_{f,\alpha,\beta,\chi}(\Gamma,T),\end{split}

where by the action of the Hecke operators as in (
3.6
) the cuspidal spectrum contribution is

(7.9)

\displaystyle\mathcal{C}_{f,\alpha,\beta,\chi}(\Gamma,T)=\sum_{V\in\mathcal{B}(q,\chi)}\sum_{\gcd(h,q)=1}\beta_{h}\lambda_{V}(h)\sum_{\ell\equiv\kappa\,\,(\mathrm{mod}\,{2})}\langle P_{f},\varphi_{V}^{(\ell)}\rangle_{\Gamma\backslash G}\sum_{\tau\in T}\alpha(\tau)\varphi_{V}^{(\ell)}(\tau)

and similarly by (
3.7
) the continuous spectrum contribution is

(7.10)

\displaystyle\mathcal{E}_{f,\alpha,\beta,\chi}(\Gamma,T)=\frac{1}{4\pi i}\sum_{\mathfrak{c}\in\mathfrak{C}(q,\chi)}\int_{(0)}\sum_{\gcd(h,q)=1}\beta_{h}\lambda_{\mathfrak{c},\nu}(h)\sum_{\ell\equiv\kappa\,\,(\mathrm{mod}\,{2})}\langle P_{f},E^{(\ell)}_{\mathfrak{c}}(\cdot,\nu)\rangle_{\Gamma\backslash G}\sum_{\tau\in T}\alpha(\tau)E^{(\ell)}_{\mathfrak{c}}(\tau,\nu)\mathrm{d}\nu.

7.3.1.
Main term

The projection onto the constant function in (
7.8
) gives the main term in (
7.4
). Indeed, for
\chi
principal we have by unfolding

\displaystyle\langle P_{f},1\rangle_{\Gamma\backslash G}

\displaystyle=\sum_{\gamma\in\Gamma}\int_{\Gamma\backslash G}F(\gamma g)\mathrm{d}g=\int_{G}F(g)\mathrm{d}g.

Thus, using the fact that the Hecke operator runs over
\sum_{d|h}\sum_{b\,\,(\mathrm{mod}\,{d})}1=\sigma_{1}(h)
many points, we get

\displaystyle\sum_{\tau\in T}\sum_{\gcd(h,q)=1}\alpha(\tau)\frac{\beta_{h}\sigma_{1}(h)}{\sqrt{h}}\mathbf{1}_{\chi\,\mathrm{principal}}\frac{1}{|\Gamma\backslash G|}\langle P_{f}(\tau),1\rangle

\displaystyle=M_{f,\alpha,\beta,\chi}(\Gamma,T).

Consequently, the proof of Theorem
7.1
is reduced to estimating
\mathcal{C}_{f,\alpha,\beta}(\Gamma,T)
and
\mathcal{E}_{f,\alpha,\beta}(\Gamma,T)
.

7.3.2.
Cuspidal Spectrum

We now estimate
\mathcal{C}_{f,\alpha,\beta,\chi}(\Gamma,T)
as given by (
7.9
). Unfolding gives for the projections

\displaystyle\langle P_{f},\varphi_{V}^{(\ell)}\rangle_{\Gamma\backslash G}

\displaystyle=\sum_{\gamma\in\Gamma}\int_{\Gamma\backslash G}\overline{\chi}(\gamma)F(\gamma g)\overline{\varphi_{V}^{(\ell)}(g)}\mathrm{d}g=\int_{G}F(g)\overline{\varphi_{V}^{(\ell)}(g)}\mathrm{d}g.

We plug in the Fourier series expansion of Proposition
3.2
to get

\displaystyle\int_{G}F(g)\overline{\varphi_{V}^{(\ell)}(g)}\mathrm{d}g

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\left|\pi^{-2\nu_{V}}\frac{\Gamma(|\ell|+\nu_{V}+1/2)}{\Gamma(|\ell|-\nu_{V}+1/2)}\right|^{1/2}\sum_{n\neq 0}\frac{\overline{\varrho_{V}(n)}}{\sqrt{|n|}}\int_{G}F(g)\overline{\mathcal{A}^{\operatorname{sgn}(n)}\phi_{\ell}(\mathrm{a}[|n|]g,\nu_{V})}\mathrm{d}g.

We now apply Proposition
4.1
. More precisely, for
V
in the regular and exceptional spectrum (that is
\operatorname{Re}(\nu_{V})<1/2
), we observe that

\displaystyle\left|\pi^{-2\nu_{V}}\frac{\Gamma(|\ell|+\nu_{V}+1/2)}{\Gamma(|\ell|-\nu_{V}+1/2)}\right|^{1/2}\ll 1

and apply (
4.3
). For the remaining part of the cuspidal spectrum, that is for
V
in the discrete series, we apply (
4.4
). Hence, denoting

\displaystyle a_{V,\ell}(n):=\overline{\varrho_{V}(\sigma n)}\sum_{\gcd(h,q)=1}\beta_{h}\lambda_{V}(h)\sum_{\tau\in T}\alpha(\tau)\varphi_{V}^{(\ell)}(\tau),

we obtain (with
D_{J}(t,\nu_{V},\ell)
as in (
4.2
))

\displaystyle\mathcal{C}_{f,\alpha,\beta,\chi}(\Gamma,T)\ll_{\varepsilon,J}\max_{t>0}\frac{Z^{\varepsilon}X}{Y^{1/2}}\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}V\in\mathcal{B}(q,\chi)\\
\end{subarray}}\sum_{\ell\equiv\kappa\,\,(\mathrm{mod}\,{2})}

\displaystyle(1+\mathbf{1}_{\operatorname{Re}(\nu_{V})<1/2}(Yt)^{-\operatorname{Re}(\nu_{V})})D_{J}(t,\nu_{V},\ell)

\displaystyle\times\bigg|\sum_{\tau\in T}\alpha(\tau)\varphi_{V}^{(\ell)}(\tau)\bigg||R_{V}(t,\beta)|.

By using
Y=\frac{1}{CD}
,
X=1/N=\frac{AD+1}{CD}
we get

\displaystyle\mathcal{C}_{f,\alpha,\beta,\chi}(\Gamma,T)\ll_{\varepsilon,J}

\displaystyle\max_{t>0}\frac{Z^{\varepsilon}\delta^{1/2}\delta_{1}^{-1/2}(AD)^{1/2}}{N^{1/2}}

\displaystyle\times\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}V\in\mathcal{B}(q,\chi)\end{subarray}}\sum_{\ell\equiv\kappa\,\,(\mathrm{mod}\,{2})}(1+\mathbf{1}_{\operatorname{Re}(\nu_{V})<1/2}(\tfrac{CD}{t})^{\operatorname{Re}(\nu_{V})})D_{J}(t,\nu_{V},\ell)

\displaystyle\times\bigg|\sum_{\tau\in T}\alpha(\tau)\varphi_{V}^{(\ell)}(\tau)\bigg||R_{V}(t,\beta)|.

We apply Cauchy-Schwarz in the form

\displaystyle\sum_{V,\ell}\frac{D_{J}(t,\nu_{V},\ell)}{N^{1/2}}\bigg|\sum_{\tau}\sum_{h}\sum_{n}\bigg|

(7.11)

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle\bigg(\sum_{V,\ell}D_{J}(t,\nu_{V},\ell)(1+\log t)^{2}\bigg|\sum_{\tau}\bigg|^{2}\bigg)^{1/2}\bigg(\sum_{V,\ell}\frac{D_{J}(t,\nu_{V},\ell)}{N(1+\log t)^{2}}\bigg|\sum_{h}\sum_{n}\bigg|^{2}\bigg)^{1/2}.

Here
\sum_{\ell}\frac{D_{J}(t,\nu_{V},\ell)}{(1+\log t)^{2}}\ll\delta^{-O(1)}Z^{\varepsilon}E_{J}(t/N,\nu_{V})
so that the second factor is bounded by

\displaystyle\delta^{-O(1)}\mathcal{R}_{\Gamma,\beta}(X,N)^{1/2}.

Using Proposition
6.1
the first factor is bounded by

\displaystyle\ll_{\varepsilon,J}Z^{\varepsilon}\delta^{-O(1)}\bigg(\sum_{V,\ell}\min_{J_{0}\in\{0,1,\dots,J\}}\frac{1}{(1+|\delta\nu|+|\delta\ell|)^{2J_{0}-1}}\bigg|\sum_{\tau\in T}\alpha(\tau)\varphi_{V}^{(\ell)}(\tau)\bigg|^{2}\bigg)^{1/2}

\displaystyle\ll_{\varepsilon}Z^{\varepsilon}\delta^{-O(1)}\mathcal{K}_{f,\alpha}(\Gamma,T,1)^{1/2},

which converges for
J\geq 3
. Thus, we obtain

\displaystyle\mathcal{C}_{f,\alpha,\beta,\chi}(\Gamma,T)\ll_{\varepsilon,J}Z^{\varepsilon}\delta^{-O(1)}(AD)^{1/2}\,\mathcal{K}_{f,\alpha}(\Gamma,T,1)^{1/2}\mathcal{R}_{\Gamma,\beta}(X,N)^{1/2}.

(
7.5
).

7.3.3.
Eisenstein Series

Similarly as above, we compute
\langle P_{f},E^{(\ell)}_{\mathfrak{c}}(\cdot,\nu)\rangle_{\Gamma\backslash G}
by unfolding. The contribution from
n\neq 0
is bounded
mutatis mutandis
as in the previous section. The contribution from
n=0
is

\displaystyle\mathcal{E}_{f,\alpha,\beta,\chi,0}(\Gamma,T):=\frac{1}{4\pi i}\sum_{\mathfrak{c}\in\mathfrak{C}(q,\chi)}\int_{(0)}\sum_{\ell\equiv\kappa\,\,(\mathrm{mod}\,{2})}\sum_{\gcd(h,q)=1}\beta_{h}\lambda_{\mathfrak{c},\nu}(h)\langle F,E^{(\ell)}_{\mathfrak{c},0}(\cdot,\nu)\rangle_{G}\sum_{\tau\in T}\alpha(\tau)E^{(\ell)}_{\mathfrak{c}}(\tau,\nu)\mathrm{d}\nu

where

\displaystyle E^{(\ell)}_{\mathfrak{c},0}(g,\nu):=1_{\mathfrak{c}=\infty}\phi_{\ell}(g,\nu)+\varrho_{\mathfrak{c},\nu}^{(\ell)}(0)\phi_{\ell}(g,-\nu).

Using
|\lambda_{\mathfrak{c},\nu}(h)|\ll_{\varepsilon}h^{\varepsilon}
and Proposition
4.2
we get

\displaystyle\mathcal{E}_{f,\alpha,\beta,\chi,0}(\Gamma,T)\ll_{\varepsilon,J}\frac{Z^{\varepsilon}\|\beta\|_{1}\delta^{1/2}\delta_{1}^{-1/2}(AD)^{1/2}}{N^{1/2}}\sum_{\mathfrak{c}\in\mathfrak{C}(q,\chi)}\int_{(0)}\sum_{\ell\equiv\kappa\,\,(\mathrm{mod}\,{2})}D_{J}(1,\nu,\ell)

\displaystyle\times\left(\mathbf{1}_{\mathfrak{c}=\infty}+|\varrho_{\mathfrak{c},\nu}^{(\ell)}(0)|\right)\bigg|\sum_{\tau\in T}\alpha(\tau)E^{(\ell)}_{\mathfrak{c}}(\tau,\nu)\bigg||\mathrm{d}\nu|.

We have

\displaystyle|\varrho_{\mathfrak{c},\nu}^{(\ell)}(0)|\leq

\displaystyle\mathbf{1}_{\mathfrak{c}=\infty}+|\mathbf{1}_{\mathfrak{c}=\infty}+\varrho_{\mathfrak{c},\nu}^{(\ell)}(0)|

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\mathbf{1}_{\mathfrak{c}=\infty}+\bigg|\int_{0}^{1}E^{(\ell)}_{\mathfrak{c}}(\mathrm{n}[x],\nu)\mathrm{d}x\bigg|

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle\mathbf{1}_{\mathfrak{c}=\infty}+\int_{0}^{1}\bigg|E^{(\ell)}_{\mathfrak{c}}(\mathrm{n}[x],\nu)\bigg|\mathrm{d}x.

Thus, for some
x\in{\mathbb{R}}
we have

\displaystyle\mathcal{E}_{f,\alpha,\beta,\chi,0}(\Gamma,T)\ll_{\varepsilon,J}\frac{Z^{\varepsilon}\|\beta\|_{1}\delta^{1/2}\delta_{1}^{-1/2}(AD)^{1/2}}{N^{1/2}}\sum_{\mathfrak{c}\in\mathfrak{C}(q,\chi)}\int_{(0)}\sum_{\ell\equiv\kappa\,\,(\mathrm{mod}\,{2})}D_{J}(1,\nu,\ell)

\displaystyle\times\left(\mathbf{1}_{\mathfrak{c}=\infty}+\bigg|E^{(\ell)}_{\mathfrak{c}}(\mathrm{n}[x],\nu)\bigg|\right)\bigg|\sum_{\tau\in T}\alpha(\tau)E^{(\ell)}_{\mathfrak{c}}(\tau,\nu)\bigg||\mathrm{d}\nu|

The contribution from
\mathbf{1}_{\mathfrak{c}=\infty}
is bounded by Cauchy-Schwarz and Proposition
6.1
by

\displaystyle\ll_{\varepsilon,J}\frac{Z^{\varepsilon}\|\beta\|_{1}\delta^{-O(1)}(AD)^{1/2}}{N^{1/2}}\mathcal{K}_{f,\alpha}(\Gamma,T,1)^{1/2}.

The contribution from
\bigg|E^{(\ell)}_{\mathfrak{c}}(\mathrm{n}[x],\nu)\bigg|
is bounded by applying Cauchy-Schwarz and Proposition
6.1
twice

\displaystyle\ll_{\varepsilon,J}\frac{Z^{\varepsilon}\|\beta\|_{1}\delta^{-O(1)}(AD)^{1/2}}{N^{1/2}}\mathcal{K}(\Gamma,\{\mathrm{n}[x]\})^{1/2}\mathcal{K}_{f,\alpha}(\Gamma,T,1)^{1/2}+Z^{-\varepsilon J},

where

\displaystyle\mathcal{K}(\Gamma,\{\mathrm{n}[x]\})\ll\delta^{-O(1)}\sum_{\gamma\in\Gamma}k_{1}(\mathrm{n}[-x]\gamma\mathrm{n}[x])\ll\delta^{-O(1)}

by using
k_{1}(\smallmatrixquantity(\lx@physics@smallmatrix a& b \\ c& d\endlx@physics@smallmatrix))\leq\mathbf{1}_{|a|+|b|+|c|+|d|\leq 6}.
∎

8.
The spectral large sieve

In this section we bound the
\mathcal{R}_{\beta,\chi}(\Gamma,N,AD)
part of Theorem
7.1
with the help of the spectral large sieve. For the the unexceptional part of the spectrum, we have the following result
[
9
, Proposition 4.7]
.

Proposition 8.1
.

Let
K\geq 1
,
N\geq 1/2
,
\varepsilon>0
be real, and
a_{n}
be a sequence of complex numbers supported on
n\sim N
. Each of the expressions

(8.1)

\displaystyle\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}V\in\mathcal{B}(q,\chi)\\
|\nu_{V}|\leq K\end{subarray}}\left|\sum_{n}a_{n}\varrho_{V}(n)\right|^{2}

(8.2)

\displaystyle\sum_{c\in\mathfrak{C}(q,\chi)}\int_{-iK}^{iK}\left|\sum_{n}a_{n}\varrho_{\mathfrak{c},\nu}(n)\right|^{2}|\mathrm{d}\nu|

is bounded by

\displaystyle\ll_{\varepsilon}(K^{2}+N^{1+\varepsilon}\mathrm{cond}(\chi)^{1/2}/q)\|a_{n}\|_{2}^{2}.

Remark 3
.

It is possible that the term
\mathrm{cond}(\chi)^{1/2}
can be improved by a more careful argument, characterizing the set of bad cases in the Weil type bound of Knightly and Li
[
18
, Theorem 9.3]
.

Deshoulliers and Iwaniec
[
8
]
also proved a weighted version of the large sieve, useful for the exceptional spectrum, which was generalized by Drappeau
[
9
]
. Their result was recently improved by Duker Lichtman
1
1

1

After the completion of this manuscript, Pascadi
[
26
]
has shown an improved exceptional large sieve for certain coefficients. Incorporating it into our
\mathcal{R}
estimates will improve the
\theta
dependency at various places throughout this work.
, using the Kim-Sarnak bound on the largest possible exceptional eigenvalue (see
[
9
, Remark at the end of Section 4.2]
for a statement of the result with
a multiplier
\chi
). We use the following notation to state the results. Recall that
\theta_{q}=\max\{\operatorname{Re}(\nu_{V})<1/2\}
and
\theta=\max_{q}\theta_{q}
. By the work of Kim-Sarnak
[
17
]
we know that
\theta\leq 7/64.

Proposition 8.2
.

Let
a_{n}
be a sequence of complex numbers supported on
n\sim N
. We have

\displaystyle\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}V\in\mathcal{B}(q,\chi)\\
\operatorname{Re}(\nu_{V})\in(0,1/2)\end{subarray}}X^{2\nu_{V}}\left|\sum_{n}a_{n}\varrho_{V}(n)\right|^{2}\ll\left(1+\bigg(\frac{NX}{q}\bigg)^{2\theta_{q}}\right)\left(1+\mathrm{cond}(\chi)^{1/2}\bigg(\frac{N^{1+\varepsilon}}{q}\bigg)^{1-2\theta_{q}}\right)\|a_{n}\|_{2}^{2}

and for any
N<N_{1}\leq 2N

\displaystyle\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}q\sim Q\\
\mathrm{cond}(\chi)|q\end{subarray}}\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}V\in\mathcal{B}(q,\chi)\\
\operatorname{Re}(\nu_{V})\in(0,1/2)\end{subarray}}X^{2\nu_{V}}\left|\sum_{N<n\leq N_{1}}\varrho_{V}(n)\right|^{2}\ll_{\varepsilon}(QN)^{\varepsilon}\bigg(\frac{Q}{\mathrm{cond}(\chi)}+N+X^{2\theta}N^{2\theta}Q^{1-4\theta}\bigg)N

Here also it may be possible to improve on the dependency on
\mathrm{cond}(\chi)
.

8.1.
Main corollary

In this section we give bounds for the quantity
\mathcal{R}_{h,\chi}(q_{1},q_{2},N)
in Theorem
7.1
using the spectral large sieve bounds. Recall that
\vartheta_{q}
denotes the best exponent towards the Ramanujan-Peterson conjecture, that is, smallest exponent so that
|\lambda_{V}(h)|\ll_{\varepsilon}|h|^{\vartheta_{q}+\varepsilon}
.

Corollary 8.3
.

Suppose that the assumptions in Theorem
7.1
hold and let
\mathcal{R}_{\beta,\chi}(q,N)
be as defined there.
Assuming that
\beta_{h}
is supported for
|h|\leq H
, we have

\displaystyle\mathcal{R}_{\beta,\chi}(q_{1},q_{2},N)\ll_{\varepsilon}\,Z^{\varepsilon}\bigg(\|\beta\|_{1}|H|^{2\vartheta_{q}}\left(1+\bigg(\frac{CD}{q_{2}}\bigg)^{2\theta_{q}}\right)\left(1+\mathrm{cond}(\chi)^{1/2}\bigg(\frac{N}{q}\bigg)^{1-2\theta_{q}}\right)+\frac{\|\beta\|_{1}^{2}}{N}\bigg),

\displaystyle\mathcal{R}_{h,\chi}(q_{1},q_{2},N)\ll_{\varepsilon}\,Z^{\varepsilon}\bigg(\|\beta\|_{2}^{2}\left(1+\bigg(\frac{HCD}{q_{2}}\bigg)^{2\theta_{q}}\right)\left(1+\mathrm{cond}(\chi)^{1/2}\bigg(\frac{HN}{q}\bigg)^{1-2\theta_{q}}\right)+\frac{\|\beta\|_{1}^{2}}{N}\bigg),

\displaystyle\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}q=q_{1}q_{2}\sim Q\\
q_{1}\sim Q_{1}\\
\mathrm{cond}(\chi)|q\end{subarray}}\mathcal{R}_{h,\chi}(q_{1},q_{2},N)\ll_{\varepsilon}\,Z^{\varepsilon}\bigg(\|\beta\|_{1}|H|^{2\vartheta_{q}}\bigg(\frac{Q}{\mathrm{cond}(\chi)}+N+(CDQ_{1})^{2\theta}Q^{1-4\theta}\bigg)+\frac{\|\beta\|_{1}^{2}Q}{N\mathrm{cond}(\chi)}\bigg).

Proof.

First and third follow immediately from the point-wise bound
|\lambda_{V}(h)|\leq H^{\vartheta_{q}}
and Propositions
8.1
and
8.2
. The second bound also follows from these propositions, after we combine the coefficients by using the multiplicativity relation for
\gcd(h,q)=1

\displaystyle\lambda_{V}(h)\varrho_{V}(n)=\sum_{d|(h,n)}\chi(d)\varrho_{V}(nh/d^{2}),

so that we can apply the spectral large sieve with the weights

\displaystyle\alpha_{m}=\sum_{n\leq N,h\sim H}\beta_{h}\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}d|(h,n)\\
m=nh/d^{2}\end{subarray}}\chi(d)=\sum_{d^{2}\leq NH/m}\chi(d)\sum_{h\sim H/d}\beta_{hd}\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}n\leq N/d\\
nh=m\end{subarray}}1.

By a divisor bound

\displaystyle\sum_{m}|\alpha_{m}|^{2}

\displaystyle\leq\sum_{m}\left|\sum_{h}\sum_{d}|\beta_{hd}|\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}n\leq N/d\\
nh=m\end{subarray}}1\right|^{2}

\displaystyle=\sum_{m}\sum_{d_{1},d_{2}}\sum_{h_{1},h_{2}|m}|\beta_{d_{1}h_{1}}||\beta_{d_{2}h_{2}}|\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}n_{j}\leq N/d_{j}\\
n_{j}h_{j}=m\end{subarray}}1

\displaystyle\leq\sum_{m}\sum_{d_{1},d_{2}}\sum_{h_{1},h_{2}|m}(|\beta_{d_{1}h_{1}}|^{2}+|\beta_{d_{2}h_{2}}|^{2})\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}n_{j}\leq N/d_{j}\\
n_{j}h_{j}=m\end{subarray}}1

\displaystyle\ll_{\varepsilon}Z^{\varepsilon}\sum_{d_{1}}\sum_{h_{1}}|\beta_{d_{1}h_{1}}|^{2}\sum_{d_{2}}\sum_{n\leq N/d_{2}}1\ll_{\varepsilon}Z^{\varepsilon}N\|\beta\|_{2}^{2}.

∎

9.
Proof of Theorem
1.1

Recall that we want to evaluate

\displaystyle S_{f,\alpha}(\mathcal{M})=\sum_{g=\smallmatrixquantity(\lx@physics@smallmatrix a & b \\c& d\endlx@physics@smallmatrix)\in\mathcal{M}}\alpha(g)f(a,c,d),

where
\mathcal{M}\subseteq\operatorname{SL}_{2}({\mathbb{R}})
,
f
satisfies the assumptions of Theorem
7.1
, and

\displaystyle L=C/D\quad\text{and}\quad N\frac{CDq_{1}}{AD+1}=\delta^{-O(1)}\frac{q_{1}C}{A}.

The claim then follows from Theorem
7.1
(
h=1,\chi
principal) and Corollary
8.3
since by denoting
g=\tau_{2}^{-1}\gamma\tau_{1}

\displaystyle\mathcal{K}_{\alpha,\chi}(\Gamma,T,L)

\displaystyle=\sum_{\tau_{1},\tau_{2}\in T}\alpha(\tau_{1})\overline{\alpha(\tau_{2})}\sum_{\gamma\in\Gamma}k_{L}(\tau_{2}^{-1}\gamma\tau_{1})

\displaystyle=\sum_{g\in\mathcal{M}^{-1}\mathcal{M}}k_{L}(\tau_{2}^{-1}\gamma\tau_{1})\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}\tau_{1},\tau_{2}\in T\\
\tau_{2}g\tau_{1}^{-1}\in\Gamma\end{subarray}}\alpha(\tau_{1})\overline{\alpha(\tau_{2})}

\displaystyle=\sum_{g\in\mathcal{M}^{-1}\mathcal{M}}k_{L}(\tau_{2}^{-1}\gamma\tau_{1})\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}\tau\in\Gamma\backslash\mathcal{M}\\
\tau g\in\mathcal{M}\end{subarray}}\alpha(\tau)\overline{\alpha(\tau g)}

\displaystyle\leq\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}g=\smallmatrixquantity(\lx@physics@smallmatrix a & b \\c & d\endlx@physics@smallmatrix)\in\mathcal{M}^{-1}\mathcal{M}\\
|a|+|b|L+|c|/L+|d|\leq 6\end{subarray}}\Bigl|\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}\tau\in\Gamma\backslash\mathcal{M}\\
\tau g\in\mathcal{M}\end{subarray}}\alpha(\tau)\overline{\alpha(\tau g)}\Bigr|.

∎

10.
Determinant equations with an automorphic twist

In this section we state and prove a more flexible technical version of Theorem
1.1
and provide some helping information that make it easier to apply. To be more precise, the main theorem of this section allows us to count solutions to a determinant equation
ad-bc=hk
twisted by a function

\displaystyle\alpha(\smallmatrixquantity(\lx@physics@smallmatrix a & b \\ c& d\endlx@physics@smallmatrix)):=\alpha_{0}(\smallmatrixquantity(\lx@physics@smallmatrix a & b \\ c& d\endlx@physics@smallmatrix))\chi_{1}(a)\psi_{1}(b)\chi_{2}(c)\psi_{2}(d)

where
\chi_{1},\psi_{1}
(resp.
\chi_{2},\psi_{2}
) are Dirichlet-characters to the modulus
q_{1}
(resp.
q_{2}
) and for any matrix
\smallmatrixquantity(\lx@physics@smallmatrix a & b \\ c & d\endlx@physics@smallmatrix)\in\operatorname{M}_{2}({\mathbb{Z}})
with
q_{1}|b,q_{2}|c
we have
\alpha_{0}(\smallmatrixquantity(\lx@physics@smallmatrix a & b \\ c& d\endlx@physics@smallmatrix)g)=\alpha_{0}(g)
. Note that then for any such
\smallmatrixquantity(\lx@physics@smallmatrix a & b \\ c & d\endlx@physics@smallmatrix)
we have for
\chi=\chi_{1}\psi_{1}\overline{\chi_{2}}\overline{\psi_{2}}

\displaystyle\alpha(\smallmatrixquantity(\lx@physics@smallmatrix a & b \\ c& d\endlx@physics@smallmatrix)g)=\chi(a)\chi_{2}\psi_{2}(ad-bc)\alpha(g)=\chi(a)\chi_{2}\psi_{2}(ad)\alpha(g).

Here we require that
(h,q_{1}q_{2})=1
. This motivates the following generalisation of Definition
1
.

Definition 3

(Automorphic function with character and determinant twist)
.

Let
q_{1},q_{2}\in{\mathbb{Z}}_{>0}
and let
\chi
be a Dirichlet character to a modulus dividing
q_{1}q_{2}
. For multiplicative coefficients
\xi_{h}
we define

\displaystyle\mathcal{A}(q_{1},q_{2},\chi,\xi):=\{\alpha:\operatorname{M}_{2}({\mathbb{Z}})\to{\mathbb{C}}:\,l_{g}\alpha=\chi(a)\xi_{\det g}\alpha\,\,\text{for all}\,\,g=\smallmatrixquantity(\lx@physics@smallmatrix a & b \\ c& d\endlx@physics@smallmatrix)

\displaystyle\in\operatorname{M}_{2}({\mathbb{Z}}),\,q_{1}|b,\,q_{2}|c,

\displaystyle\gcd(\det g,q)=1\}.

Theorem 10.1
.

(Determinant equation twisted by an automorphic function).

Let
q_{1},q_{2}\in{\mathbb{Z}}_{>0}
. For non-zero integers
h,k
denote

\displaystyle\operatorname{M}_{2,h,k}({\mathbb{Z}}):=\bigg\{\matrixquantity(\lx@physics@matrix a &b \\ c &d\endlx@physics@matrix)\in\operatorname{M}_{2,hk}({\mathbb{Z}}):\,\gcd(a,c,k)=\gcd(b,d,k)=1\bigg\},

Denote

\displaystyle q=q_{1}q_{2},\quad\Gamma=\Gamma_{2}(q_{1},q_{2}),\quad T:=\Gamma\backslash\operatorname{SL}_{2}({\mathbb{Z}}),\quad T_{1,k}:=\operatorname{SL}_{2}({\mathbb{Z}})\backslash\operatorname{M}_{2,1,k}({\mathbb{Z}}).

Let
\chi
be a Dirichlet character to a modulus dividing
q_{1}q_{2}
and let
\xi_{h}
be multiplicative complex coefficients. Let

\displaystyle\alpha\in\mathcal{A}(q_{1},q_{2},\chi,\xi).

Let
A,C,D,\delta,\eta>0
with
AD>\delta
and denote
Z:=\max\{A^{\pm 1},C^{\pm 1},D^{\pm 1},\delta^{-1}\}
. Let
H,K\geq 1
and assume that
HK\leq(AD)^{1+\eta}
. Let

\displaystyle f\in C^{7}_{\delta}\bigg(\frac{A}{\sqrt{HK}},\frac{C}{\sqrt{HK}},\frac{D}{\sqrt{HK}}\bigg).

Let
\beta_{h}
be supported on
|h|\in[H,2H]
and
\gamma_{k}
supported on
|k|\in[K,2K]
. Denote

(10.1)

\displaystyle w(\sigma,\sigma_{1},\sigma_{2})=\sum_{\tau\in T}\alpha(\tau\sigma\sigma_{1})\overline{\alpha(\tau\sigma_{2})}

and assume that for some
\mathcal{K}_{+}>0

(10.2)

\displaystyle\frac{1}{K}\sum_{k_{1},k_{2}}|\gamma_{k_{1}}\gamma_{k_{2}}|\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}g=\smallmatrixquantity(\lx@physics@smallmatrix a&b \\ c & d\endlx@physics@smallmatrix)\in(k_{1}/k_{2})^{1/2}\operatorname{SL}_{2}({\mathbb{R}})\\
|a|+|b|C/D+|c|D/C+|d|\leq 10\end{subarray}}\bigg|\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}\sigma_{j}\in T_{1,k_{j}}\\
\sigma_{2}g\sigma_{1}^{-1}=\sigma\in\operatorname{SL}_{2}({\mathbb{Z}})\end{subarray}}w(\sigma,\sigma_{1},\sigma_{2})\bigg|\ll Z^{O(\eta)}\mathcal{K}_{+}.

Then

\displaystyle\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}h,k\\
\gcd(h,kq)=1\end{subarray}}\beta_{h}\gamma_{k}\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}\smallmatrixquantity(\lx@physics@smallmatrix a& b \\ c& d\endlx@physics@smallmatrix)\in\operatorname{M}_{2,h,k}({\mathbb{Z}})\end{subarray}}\alpha(\smallmatrixquantity(\lx@physics@smallmatrix a & b \\ c & d\endlx@physics@smallmatrix))f\bigg(\frac{a}{\sqrt{|hk|}},\frac{c}{\sqrt{|hk|}},\frac{d}{\sqrt{|hk|}}\bigg)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\frac{\mathbf{1}_{\chi\,\mathrm{principal}}}{\zeta(2)q\,\prod_{p|q}(1+p^{-1})}\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}h,k\\
\gcd(h,kq)=1\end{subarray}}\beta_{h}\xi_{h}\sigma_{1}(|h|)\gamma_{k}\sum_{\tau\in\Gamma\backslash\operatorname{M}_{2,1,k}({\mathbb{Z}})}\alpha(\tau)\int_{{\mathbb{R}}^{3}}f(a,c,d)\frac{\mathrm{d}a\mathrm{d}c\mathrm{d}d}{c}

\displaystyle\hskip 80.0pt+O\left(Z^{O(\eta)}\delta^{-O(1)}(AD)^{1/2}\|\beta\xi\|_{2}\,\mathcal{K}_{+}^{1/2}\left(\mathcal{R}_{0}+\min_{j\in\{1,2\}}\mathcal{R}_{j}\right)\right),

where

\displaystyle\mathcal{R}_{0}

\displaystyle:=\frac{\|\beta\xi\|_{1}A^{1/2}}{\|\beta\xi\|_{2}q_{1}^{1/2}C^{1/2}},

\displaystyle\mathcal{R}_{1}

\displaystyle:=\frac{\|\beta\xi\|_{1}}{\|\beta\xi\|_{2}}H^{\vartheta_{q}}\left(1+\bigg(\frac{CD}{HKq_{2}}\bigg)^{\theta_{q}}\right)\left(1+\mathrm{cond}(\chi)^{1/4}\bigg(\frac{C}{Aq_{2}}\bigg)^{1/2-\theta_{q}}\right),

\displaystyle\mathcal{R}_{2}

\displaystyle:=\left(1+\bigg(\frac{CD}{Kq_{2}}\bigg)^{\theta_{q}}\right)\left(1+\mathrm{cond}(\chi)^{1/4}\bigg(\frac{HC}{Aq_{2}}\bigg)^{1/2-\theta_{q}}\right).

Note that the smooth weight is supported on
(|a|,|c|,|d|)
of size
\asymp(A,C,D)
. In our applications we only consider
\mathcal{A}(q_{1},q_{2},\chi,\xi)
with a principal character
\chi
and with
k
fixed, but the generality will be useful for future work.

We have the following observation that will be useful in the proof of Theorem
1.2
.

Remark 4
.

In the case that
\gamma_{k}
is supported on
k=1
we can denote
w(\sigma)=w(\sigma,I,I)
and the assumption
(
10.2
) may be simplified to

(10.3)

\displaystyle\begin{split}\sum_{0\leq|c|\leq 6C/D}|w(\smallmatrixquantity(\lx@physics@smallmatrix\pm 1 & \\ c & \pm 1\endlx@physics@smallmatrix))|&\ll Z^{O(\eta)}\mathcal{K}_{+}\\
\sum_{0\leq|b|\leq 6D/C}|w(\smallmatrixquantity(\lx@physics@smallmatrix\pm 1 & b \\ & \pm 1\endlx@physics@smallmatrix))|&\ll Z^{O(\eta)}\mathcal{K}_{+}.\end{split}

To make the application of Theorem
10.1
and in particular the calculation of
w(\sigma,\sigma_{1},\sigma_{2})
easier, we now give a parametrization for
T=\Gamma\backslash\operatorname{SL}_{2}({\mathbb{Z}})
. Define the projective line over
{\mathbb{Z}}/p^{k}{\mathbb{Z}}

\displaystyle\mathbb{P}_{p^{k}}^{1}:=\{(x,y)\in({\mathbb{Z}}/p^{k}{\mathbb{Z}})^{2}:x\,\text{or}\,y\in({\mathbb{Z}}/p^{k}{\mathbb{Z}})^{\times}\}/\sim,

where we define an equivalence relation by
(x_{1},y_{1})\sim(x_{2},y_{2})
if there exists
\lambda\in({\mathbb{Z}}/p^{k}{\mathbb{Z}})^{\times}
such that
(x_{1},y_{1})=(\lambda x_{2},\lambda y_{2})
. This can be identified with the set of size
p^{k}+p^{k-1}

\displaystyle\{(x,1):x\in{\mathbb{Z}}/p^{k}{\mathbb{Z}}\}\cup\{(1,y):y\in{\mathbb{Z}}/p^{k}{\mathbb{Z}},\,\,p|y\}.

For
q\in{\mathbb{Z}}_{>0}
we define

\displaystyle\mathbb{P}_{q}^{1}:=\prod_{p^{k}||q}\mathbb{P}_{p^{k}}^{1}.

By the Chinese remainder theorem we may identify this with the set

\displaystyle\{(x,y)\in({\mathbb{Z}}/q{\mathbb{Z}})^{2}:\gcd(x,y,q)=1\}/\sim,

where the equivalence relation is as before with
\lambda\in({\mathbb{Z}}/q{\mathbb{Z}})^{\times}
.
Note that the normalization in
q
in the expected main term in Theorem
10.1
satisfies

\displaystyle q\prod_{p|q}(1+p^{-1})=|\mathbb{P}_{q}^{1}|.

Let
{\mathbb{Z}}^{2}(q):=\{(a,b)\in{\mathbb{Z}}:\gcd(a,b,q)=1\}
and define projections
for
q=p_{1}^{k_{1}}\cdots p_{m}^{k_{m}}

\displaystyle\pi_{p^{k}}:

\displaystyle{\mathbb{Z}}^{2}(q)\to\mathbb{P}_{p^{k}}^{1},\quad(a,b)\mapsto(a,b)\,\,(\mathrm{mod}\,{p^{k}})

\displaystyle\pi_{q}:

\displaystyle{\mathbb{Z}}^{2}(q)\to\mathbb{P}_{q}^{1},\quad\pi_{q}:=\pi_{p^{k_{1}}}\times\cdots\times\pi_{p^{k_{m}}}.

By Chinese remainder theorem we may continue to denote elements of
\mathbb{P}_{q}^{1}
as pairs
(a,b)\in\mathbb{P}_{q}^{1}
,
a,b\in{\mathbb{Z}}/q{\mathbb{Z}}
.
Given
q_{1},q_{2}
we define then the projection

\displaystyle\pi_{q_{1},q_{2}}:\operatorname{SL}_{2}({\mathbb{Z}})\to\mathbb{P}_{q_{1}}^{1}\times\mathbb{P}_{q_{2}}^{1}:\matrixquantity(\lx@physics@matrix a & b \\ c &d\endlx@physics@matrix)\mapsto(\pi_{q_{1}}(a,b),\pi_{q_{2}}(c,d)).

This map is surjective iff
q_{0}=\gcd(q_{1},q_{2})=1
. The image is characterized by the condition
\gcd(ad-bc,q_{0})=1
. The map
\pi_{q_{1},q_{2}}
is invariant under the action of
\Gamma_{2}(q_{1},q_{2})
and we get a bijection

(10.4)

\displaystyle\varpi_{q_{1},q_{2}}:T=\Gamma_{2}(q_{1},q_{2})\backslash\operatorname{SL}_{2}({\mathbb{Z}})\to\mathrm{Im}(\pi_{q_{1},q_{2}}).

This is well-defined since
\pi_{q_{1},q_{2}}
is invariant under the action of
\Gamma_{2}(q_{1},q_{2})
from the left. Hence, we have the following lemma for
\xi\equiv 1
, which we require for the proof of Theorems
1.6
and
1.7
.

Lemma 10.2
.

Let
q,q_{1},q_{2}
,
\alpha_{0}
,
\chi_{j},\psi_{j}
, and
\alpha(\smallmatrixquantity(\lx@physics@smallmatrix a & b \\ c& d\endlx@physics@smallmatrix))=\alpha_{0}(\smallmatrixquantity(\lx@physics@smallmatrix a & b \\ c& d\endlx@physics@smallmatrix))\chi_{1}(a)\psi_{1}(b)\chi_{2}(c)\psi_{2}(d)
with
\alpha_{0}
being
\Gamma_{2}(q_{1},q_{2})
invariant and assume that
\alpha\in\mathcal{A}(q_{1},q_{1},\chi,1)
. Denote
q_{0}=\gcd(q_{1},q_{2})
.
Then for
w
given by (
10.1
) we have under the identification (
10.4
)

\displaystyle w(\sigma,I,I)=\sum_{(a_{2},b_{2})\in\mathbb{P}^{1}_{q_{1}}}\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}(c_{2},d_{2})\in\mathbb{P}^{1}_{q_{2}}\\
\gcd(a_{2}d_{2}-b_{2}c_{2},q_{0})=1\end{subarray}}\alpha_{0}(\tau_{2}\sigma)\overline{\alpha_{0}(\tau_{2})}

\displaystyle\,\chi_{1}(aa_{2}+cb_{2})\psi_{1}(ba_{2}+db_{2})\overline{\chi_{1}}(a_{2})\overline{\psi_{1}}(b_{2})

\displaystyle\times

\displaystyle\,\chi_{2}(ac_{2}+cd_{2})\psi_{2}(bc_{2}+dd_{2})\overline{\chi_{2}}(c_{2})\overline{\psi_{2}}(d_{2}).

Furthermore, for any
\sigma\in\operatorname{SL}_{2}({\mathbb{Z}})
we have
|w(\sigma,I,I)|\leq\sum_{\tau\in T}|\alpha_{0}(\tau)|^{2}
.

10.1.
Proof of Theorem
10.1

By symmetry we may restrict to the part
h,k\geq 1
(otherwise we can multiply the determinant equation throughout by
-1
and let
(c,d)\mapsto(-c,-d)
). Recall that

\displaystyle\operatorname{M}_{2,h,k}({\mathbb{Z}})=\bigg\{\matrixquantity(\lx@physics@matrix a &b \\ c &d\endlx@physics@matrix)\in\operatorname{M}_{2,hk}({\mathbb{Z}}):\,\gcd(a,c,k)=\gcd(b,d,k)=1\bigg\}.

Note that the action from left by
\operatorname{SL}_{2}({\mathbb{Z}})
preserves the conditions
\gcd(a,c,k)=\gcd(b,d,k)=1
so that we may set

\displaystyle T_{h,k}:=\operatorname{SL}_{2}({\mathbb{Z}})\backslash\operatorname{M}_{2,h,k}({\mathbb{Z}}).

Recall that in the statement of Theorem
10.1
we denote
T=\Gamma\backslash\operatorname{SL}_{2}({\mathbb{Z}})
,
\Gamma=\Gamma_{2}(q_{1},q_{2})
,
q=q_{1}q_{2}
. By definition

\displaystyle\operatorname{M}_{2,h,k}({\mathbb{Z}})=\operatorname{SL}_{2}({\mathbb{Z}})T_{h,k}=\Gamma\,T\,T_{h,k}.

Denote further

\displaystyle T_{h}^{(q_{1})}=\bigg\{\matrixquantity(\lx@physics@matrix a & bq_1 \\ & d\endlx@physics@matrix):a,d\in{\mathbb{Z}}_{>0},\quad ad=h,\quad b\,\,(\mathrm{mod}\,{d})\bigg\}.

By
(h,kq)=1
we have

(10.5)

\displaystyle\operatorname{M}_{2,h,k}({\mathbb{Z}})=\Gamma\,T\,T_{h}^{(q_{1})}T_{1,k}=\Gamma\,T_{h}^{(q_{1})}\,T\,T_{1,k}.

Indeed, to show that
\Gamma\,T\,T_{h}^{(q_{1})}=\Gamma\,T_{h}^{(q_{1})}\,T
we note that for
\sigma_{j}\in T_{h}^{(q_{1})},\tau_{j}\in T

\displaystyle\Gamma\sigma_{1}\tau_{1}=\Gamma\sigma_{2}\tau_{2}\Rightarrow\sigma_{1}\tau_{1}\tau_{2}^{-1}\sigma_{2}^{-1}\in\Gamma\Rightarrow\tau_{1}=\tau_{2}\Rightarrow\sigma_{1}\sigma_{2}^{-1}\in SL_{2}({\mathbb{Z}})\Rightarrow\sigma_{1}=\sigma_{2}.

Here in the second implication we rely on the fact that
q_{1},q_{2}
respectively divide the top right, bottom left entries of
\sigma_{j}
. Therefore, the orbits in
T_{h}^{(q_{1})}\,T
are distinct and has the same cardinality as
T\,T_{h}^{(q_{1})}
, so the two sets of orbits must be equal.

Since
\alpha\in\mathcal{A}(q_{1},q_{2},\chi,\xi)
, for
\smallmatrixquantity(\lx@physics@smallmatrix a & bq_1 \\ & d\endlx@physics@smallmatrix)\in T_{h}^{(q_{1})}
we have for any
\sigma\in\operatorname{M}_{2}({\mathbb{Z}})

\displaystyle\alpha(\smallmatrixquantity(\lx@physics@smallmatrix a & bq_1 \\ & d\endlx@physics@smallmatrix)\sigma)=\xi_{h}\chi(a)\alpha(\sigma)

and for any
\gamma\in\Gamma
we have
\alpha(\gamma\sigma)=\overline{\chi}(\gamma)\alpha(\sigma)
. Therefore, denoting

\displaystyle P_{f}(\tau):=\sum_{\gamma\in\Gamma}\overline{\chi}(\gamma)f(\gamma\tau),

we have

\displaystyle\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}\smallmatrixquantity(\lx@physics@smallmatrix a& b \\ c& d\endlx@physics@smallmatrix)\in\operatorname{M}_{2,h,k}\end{subarray}}\alpha(\smallmatrixquantity(\lx@physics@smallmatrix a & b \\ c & d\endlx@physics@smallmatrix))f\bigg(\frac{a}{\sqrt{|hk|}},\frac{c}{\sqrt{|hk|}},\frac{d}{\sqrt{|hk|}}\bigg)

\displaystyle=\sum_{\tau\in\frac{1}{\sqrt{hk}}\Gamma\backslash\operatorname{M}_{2,h,k}({\mathbb{Z}})}\alpha(\tau)P_{f}(\tau)

\displaystyle=\xi_{h}\sqrt{h}\sum_{\tau\in T}\sum_{\sigma\in\frac{1}{\sqrt{k}}T_{1,k}}\alpha(\tau\sigma)\mathcal{T}_{h}P(\tau\sigma),

where the Hecke operator
\mathcal{T}_{h}
(with character
\chi
) is defined in (
7.1
). By expanding
\gcd(h,k)=1
with the Möbius function we get

\displaystyle\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}h,k\\
\gcd(h,kq)=1\end{subarray}}\beta_{h}\gamma_{k}\sum_{ad-bc=hk}\alpha(\smallmatrixquantity(\lx@physics@smallmatrix a & b \\ c & d\endlx@physics@smallmatrix))f\bigg(\frac{a}{\sqrt{|hk|}},\frac{c}{\sqrt{|hk|}},\frac{d}{\sqrt{|hk|}}\bigg)

\displaystyle=\sum_{\gcd(m,q)=1}\mu(m)\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}h,k\\
\gcd(h,q)=1\\
m|h\\
m|k\end{subarray}}\beta_{h}\xi_{h}\gamma_{k}\sqrt{h}\sum_{\tau\in T}\sum_{\sigma\in k^{-1/2}T_{1,k}}\alpha(\tau\sigma)\mathcal{T}_{h}P(\tau\sigma).

Thus, denoting
\beta^{(m)}_{h}=\beta_{h}\xi_{h}\sqrt{h/H}\mathbf{1}_{m|h},
and
\gamma^{(m)}_{k}=\gamma_{k}\mathbf{1}_{m|k}
, in the notation of Theorem
7.1
we have

\displaystyle\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}h,k\\
\gcd(h,kq)=1\end{subarray}}\beta_{h}\gamma_{k}\sum_{ad-bc=hk}\alpha(\smallmatrixquantity(\lx@physics@smallmatrix a & b \\ c & d\endlx@physics@smallmatrix))

\displaystyle f\bigg(\frac{a}{\sqrt{|hk|}},\frac{c}{\sqrt{|hk|}},\frac{d}{\sqrt{|hk|}}\bigg)

\displaystyle=\sum_{\gcd(m,q)=1}\mu(m)H^{1/2}S_{f,\alpha\star\gamma^{(m)},\beta^{(m)},\chi}(\Gamma,T_{0}),

where

\displaystyle T_{0}=\bigcup_{k}k^{-1/2}TT_{1,k}

and

\displaystyle(\alpha\star\gamma^{(m)})(\tau_{0})=\sum_{k}\gamma^{(m)}_{k}\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}\tau_{0}=\tau\sigma k^{-1/2}\\
\tau\in T\\
\sigma\in T_{1,k}\end{subarray}}\alpha(\tau\sigma).

We apply Theorem
7.1
with

\displaystyle f\in C^{7}_{\delta}\bigg(\frac{A}{\sqrt{HK}},\frac{C}{\sqrt{HK}},\frac{D}{\sqrt{HK}}\bigg).

After substituting in the integral and going back from
\beta^{(m)},\gamma^{(m)}
to
\beta,\gamma
, the main term in Theorem
7.1
matches the one in Theorem
10.1
. We get an error of size (after applying Cauchy-Schwarz on
m
)

\displaystyle\ll_{\varepsilon}Z^{O(\eta)}(AD)^{1/2}K^{-1/2}\bigg(\sum_{m}\mathcal{K}_{\alpha\star\gamma^{(m)},\chi}(\Gamma,T_{0},L)\bigg)^{1/2}\bigg(\sum_{m}\mathcal{R}_{\beta^{(m)},\chi}(q_{1},q_{2},N)\bigg)^{1/2},

It remains to estimate
\mathcal{K}
and
\mathcal{R}
.

We first consider
\mathcal{K}
. We have

\displaystyle\frac{1}{K}\sum_{m}\mathcal{K}_{\alpha\star\gamma^{(m)},\chi}(\Gamma,T,L)=

\displaystyle\frac{1}{K}\sum_{m}\sum_{\tau_{1},\tau_{2}\in T_{0}}(\alpha\star\gamma^{(m)})(\tau_{1})\overline{(\alpha\star\gamma^{(m)})(\tau_{2})}\sum_{\gamma\in\Gamma}\overline{\chi}(\gamma)k_{L}(\tau_{2}^{-1}\gamma\tau_{1})

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\frac{1}{K}\sum_{k_{1},k_{2}}\sum_{m|\gcd(k_{1},k_{2})}\gamma_{k_{1}}\overline{\gamma_{k_{2}}}\sum_{\sigma_{j}\in T_{1,k_{j}}}\sum_{\tau_{1},\tau_{2}\in T}\alpha(\tau_{1}\sigma_{1})\overline{\alpha(\tau_{2}\sigma_{2})}

\displaystyle\times\sum_{\gamma\in\Gamma}\overline{\chi}(\gamma)k_{L}(k_{2}^{1/2}\sigma_{2}^{-1}\tau_{2}^{-1}\gamma\tau_{1}\sigma_{1}k_{1}^{-1/2})

for some function
k_{L}:G\to[0,1]
with

\displaystyle k_{L}(\smallmatrixquantity(\lx@physics@smallmatrix a & b \\ c& d\endlx@physics@smallmatrix))\leq\mathbf{1}_{|a|+|b|L+|c|/L+|d|\leq 6}.

By denoting
\sigma=\tau_{2}^{-1}\gamma\tau_{1}
and
g=\sigma_{2}^{-1}\tau_{2}^{-1}\gamma\tau_{1}\sigma_{1}
we have by the triangle inequality and a divisor bound to handle
\sum_{m}

\displaystyle\frac{1}{K}\sum_{m}\mathcal{K}_{\alpha\star\gamma^{(m)},\chi}(\Gamma,T,L)\ll_{\varepsilon}

\displaystyle\frac{Z^{\varepsilon}}{K}\sum_{k_{1},k_{2}}|\gamma_{k_{1}}\gamma_{k_{2}}|\sum_{g\in(k_{1}/k_{2})^{1/2}\operatorname{SL}_{2}({\mathbb{R}})}|k_{L}((k_{2}/k_{1})^{1/2}g)|

\displaystyle\bigg|\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}\sigma_{j}\in T_{1,k_{j}}\\
\sigma_{2}g\sigma_{1}^{-1}=\sigma\in\operatorname{SL}_{2}({\mathbb{Z}})\end{subarray}}\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}\tau_{j}\in T\\
\tau_{2}\sigma\tau_{1}^{-1}\in\Gamma\end{subarray}}\overline{\chi}(\tau_{2}\sigma\tau_{1}^{-1})\alpha(\tau_{1}\sigma_{1})\overline{\alpha(\tau_{2}\sigma_{2})}\bigg|

\displaystyle\ll_{\varepsilon}

\displaystyle\frac{Z^{\varepsilon}}{K}\sum_{k_{1},k_{2}}|\gamma_{k_{1}}\gamma_{k_{2}}|\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}g=\smallmatrixquantity(\lx@physics@smallmatrix a &b \\c& d\endlx@physics@smallmatrix)\in(k_{1}/k_{2})^{1/2}\operatorname{SL}_{2}({\mathbb{R}})\\
|a|+|b|L+|c|/L+|d|\leq 10\end{subarray}}

\displaystyle\bigg|\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}\sigma_{j}\in T_{1,k_{j}}\\
\sigma_{2}g\sigma_{1}^{-1}=\sigma\in\operatorname{SL}_{2}({\mathbb{Z}})\end{subarray}}\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}\tau_{j}\in T\\
\tau_{2}\sigma\tau_{1}^{-1}\in\Gamma\end{subarray}}\overline{\chi}(\tau_{2}\sigma\tau_{1}^{-1})\alpha(\tau_{1}\sigma_{1})\overline{\alpha(\tau_{2}\sigma_{2})}\bigg|.

The inner sum is
w(\sigma,\sigma_{1},\sigma_{2})
. Indeed, in the range of summation
\tau_{1}=\gamma\tau_{2}\sigma
for some
\gamma\in\Gamma
since
\Gamma\tau_{1}=\Gamma\tau_{2}\sigma
. Thus, by using
\alpha\in\mathcal{A}(q_{1},q_{2},\chi,\xi)
we get

\displaystyle\overline{\chi}(\tau_{2}\sigma\tau_{1}^{-1})\alpha(\tau_{1}\sigma_{1})\overline{\alpha(\tau_{2}\sigma_{2})}=\chi(\gamma)\alpha(\gamma\tau_{2}\sigma\sigma_{1})\overline{\alpha(\tau_{2}\sigma_{2})}=\alpha(\tau_{2}\sigma\sigma_{1})\overline{\alpha(\tau_{2}\sigma_{2})}.

To complete the proof of Theorem
10.1
we now estimate
\mathcal{R}
with the help of Corollary
8.3
. Applying it gives us that (ignoring a factor of
Z^{O(\eta)}
)

\displaystyle\sum_{m}\mathcal{R}_{\beta^{(m)},\chi}(q_{1},q_{2},N)\ll

\displaystyle\,\|\beta\xi\|_{1}|H|^{2\vartheta_{q}}\left(1+\bigg(\frac{CD}{HKq_{2}}\bigg)^{2\theta_{q}}\right)\left(1+\mathrm{cond}(\chi)^{1/2}\bigg(\frac{N}{q}\bigg)^{1-2\theta_{q}}\right)+\frac{\|\beta\xi\|_{1}^{2}}{N},

\displaystyle\sum_{m}\mathcal{R}_{\beta^{(m)},\chi}(q_{1},q_{2},N)\ll

\displaystyle\,\|\beta\xi\|_{2}^{2}\left(1+\bigg(\frac{CD}{Kq_{2}}\bigg)^{2\theta_{q}}\right)\left(1+\mathrm{cond}(\chi)^{1/2}\bigg(\frac{HN}{q}\bigg)^{1-2\theta_{q}}\right)+\frac{\|\beta\xi\|_{1}^{2}}{N}.

Plugging in
q_{1}C/A\ll N\ll Z^{O(\eta)}q_{1}C/A
, we obtain that up to a factor of
Z^{O(\eta)}

\displaystyle\sum_{m}\mathcal{R}_{\beta^{(m)},\chi}(q_{1},q_{2},N)\ll

\displaystyle\,\|\beta\xi\|_{1}^{2}|H|^{2\vartheta_{q}}\left(1+\bigg(\frac{CD}{HKq_{2}}\bigg)^{2\theta_{q}}\right)\left(1+\mathrm{cond}(\chi)^{1/2}\bigg(\frac{C}{Aq_{2}}\bigg)^{1-2\theta_{q}}\right)+\frac{\|\beta\xi\|_{1}^{2}A}{q_{1}C},

\displaystyle\sum_{m}\mathcal{R}_{\beta^{(m)},\chi}(q_{1},q_{2},N)\ll

\displaystyle\,\|\beta\xi\|_{2}^{2}\left(1+\bigg(\frac{CD}{Kq_{2}}\bigg)^{2\theta_{q}}\right)\left(1+\mathrm{cond}(\chi)^{1/2}\bigg(\frac{HC}{Aq_{2}}\bigg)^{1-2\theta_{q}}\right)+\frac{\|\beta\xi\|_{1}^{2}A}{q_{1}C},

as required.
∎

11.
Proof of Theorem
1.2

We first prove the following proposition, which is Theorem
1.2
with a weaker error term.

Proposition 11.1
.

Let

\displaystyle U_{h}(r_{1},r_{2};q)

\displaystyle:=\prod_{p|q}\bigg(\mathbf{1}_{r_{1}\equiv 0\,\,(\mathrm{mod}\,{p})}+\mathbf{1}_{r_{2}\equiv 0\,\,(\mathrm{mod}\,{p})}+\mathbf{1}_{r_{2}\equiv-h\,\,(\mathrm{mod}\,{p})}+\mathbf{1}_{r_{1}\equiv r_{2}\,\,(\mathrm{mod}\,{p})}+p^{-1}\bigg)

(11.1)

\displaystyle\mathcal{N}_{h}(t)

\displaystyle:=\sum_{r_{1},r_{2}\,\,(\mathrm{mod}\,{q})}|t(r_{1})t(r_{2})|U_{h}(r_{1},r_{2};q).

Then the asymptotic in Theorem
1.2
holds with the error term

\displaystyle O\bigg(X^{1/2+O(\eta)}q^{1/2}\mathcal{N}_{h}(t)^{1/2}(|h|^{\theta}+(X/q)^{\theta})\bigg).

For the proof, we begin by expressing
t(n)
as a sum of functions which are
balanced
in a certain sense. Defining

(11.2)

\displaystyle u(r;p):=\mathbf{1}_{r\equiv 0\,\,(\mathrm{mod}\,{p})}-p^{-1},\quad u(r;q_{0}):=\prod_{p|q_{0}}u(r;p),

we have for square-free
q

\displaystyle\mathbf{1}_{n\equiv r\,\,(\mathrm{mod}\,{p})}=\prod_{p|q}\mathbf{1}_{n\equiv r\,\,(\mathrm{mod}\,{p})}=\prod_{p|q}(u(n-r;p)+p^{-1})=\sum_{q_{0}|q}\frac{1}{q/q_{0}}u(n-r;q_{0}).

Therefore,

(11.3)

\displaystyle\begin{split}t(n)&=\sum_{r\,\,(\mathrm{mod}\,{q})}t(r)\mathbf{1}_{n\equiv r\,\,(\mathrm{mod}\,{p})}=\sum_{q_{0}|q}\frac{1}{q/q_{0}}\sum_{r\,\,(\mathrm{mod}\,{q})}t(r)u(n-r;q_{0})\\
&=\sum_{q_{0}|q}t^{\flat}(n;q_{0}),\\
t^{\flat}(n;q_{0})&:=\sum_{r_{0}\,\,(\mathrm{mod}\,{q_{0}})}t(r_{0};q_{0})u(n-r_{0};q_{0}),\\
t(r_{0};q_{0})&:=\frac{1}{q/q_{0}}\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}r\,\,(\mathrm{mod}\,{q})\\
r\equiv r_{0}\,\,(\mathrm{mod}\,{q_{0}})\end{subarray}}t(r).\end{split}

Denote

\displaystyle\alpha(\smallmatrixquantity(\lx@physics@smallmatrix a & b \\c & d\endlx@physics@smallmatrix);q_{0}):=t^{\flat}(ad;q_{0}).

The parts where some
p|q_{0}
divides one of
a,b,c,d
also need to be separated. To this end, we write for
ad-bc=-h
with
\gcd(h,q)=1

\displaystyle\alpha(\smallmatrixquantity(\lx@physics@smallmatrix a & b \\c & d\endlx@physics@smallmatrix);q_{0})

\displaystyle=\alpha(\smallmatrixquantity(\lx@physics@smallmatrix a & b \\c & d\endlx@physics@smallmatrix);q_{0})\prod_{p|q_{0}}\bigg(\mathbf{1}_{p|a}+\mathbf{1}_{p|b}+\mathbf{1}_{\begin{subarray}{c}p|c\end{subarray}}+\mathbf{1}_{\begin{subarray}{c}p|d\end{subarray}}-\mathbf{1}_{p|\gcd(a,d)}-\mathbf{1}_{p|\gcd(b,c)}+\mathbf{1}_{\gcd(abcd,p)=1}\bigg)

(11.4)

\displaystyle=\sum_{q_{0}=q_{1}q_{2}q_{3}q_{4}q_{5}q_{6}q_{7}}\alpha(\smallmatrixquantity(\lx@physics@smallmatrix a & b \\c & d\endlx@physics@smallmatrix);q_{0})\prod_{i=1}^{7}v_{i}(\smallmatrixquantity(\lx@physics@smallmatrix a & b \\c & d\endlx@physics@smallmatrix);q_{i})=:\sum_{q_{0}=q_{1}q_{2}q_{3}q_{4}q_{5}q_{6}q_{7}}\alpha(\smallmatrixquantity(\lx@physics@smallmatrix a & b \\c & d\endlx@physics@smallmatrix);\mathbf{q}),

where

\displaystyle v_{1}(\smallmatrixquantity(\lx@physics@smallmatrix a & b \\c & d\endlx@physics@smallmatrix);q_{1})=\mathbf{1}_{q_{1}|a},\quad v_{2}(\smallmatrixquantity(\lx@physics@smallmatrix a & b \\c & d\endlx@physics@smallmatrix);q_{2})=\mathbf{1}_{q_{2}|b},\quad v_{3}(\smallmatrixquantity(\lx@physics@smallmatrix a & b \\c & d\endlx@physics@smallmatrix);q_{3})=\mathbf{1}_{q_{3}|c},\quad v_{4}(\smallmatrixquantity(\lx@physics@smallmatrix a & b \\c & d\endlx@physics@smallmatrix);q_{4})=\mathbf{1}_{q_{4}|d},

\displaystyle v_{5}(\smallmatrixquantity(\lx@physics@smallmatrix a & b \\c & d\endlx@physics@smallmatrix);q_{1})=\mathbf{1}_{q_{5}|\gcd(a,d)},\quad v_{1}(\smallmatrixquantity(\lx@physics@smallmatrix a & b \\c & d\endlx@physics@smallmatrix);q_{1})=\mathbf{1}_{q_{6}|\gcd(b,c)},\quad v_{1}(\smallmatrixquantity(\lx@physics@smallmatrix a & b \\c & d\endlx@physics@smallmatrix);q_{1})=\mathbf{1}_{\gcd(abcd,q_{7})=1}.

Denote also

\displaystyle V(\smallmatrixquantity(\lx@physics@smallmatrix a & b \\c & d\endlx@physics@smallmatrix);\mathbf{q}):=\prod_{i=1}^{7}v_{i}(\smallmatrixquantity(\lx@physics@smallmatrix a & b \\c & d\endlx@physics@smallmatrix);q_{i}).

We will apply Theorem
10.1
with the group

(11.5)

\displaystyle\Gamma=\Gamma_{2}(q_{1}q_{2}q_{5}q_{6}q_{7},q_{3}q_{4}q_{5}q_{6}q_{7})=:\Gamma_{2}(Q_{1},Q_{2}).

To prepare we need some lemmas. For any prime number
p
we write
p_{j}=\gcd(p,q_{j})
,
\mathbf{p}=(p_{1},\dots,p_{7})
, and
P_{j}:=\gcd(p,Q_{j})
.

Lemma 11.2
.

Let
b\in{\mathbb{Z}}
and let
p=p_{j}
for some
j\in\{1,2,3,4,5,6,7\}
. Define

\displaystyle\Pi:\,

\displaystyle\Gamma_{2}(P_{1},P_{2})\backslash\operatorname{SL}_{2}({\mathbb{Z}})\to{\mathbb{Z}}/P_{1}{\mathbb{Z}}\times{\mathbb{Z}}/P_{2}{\mathbb{Z}},

\displaystyle\Pi(\smallmatrixquantity(\lx@physics@smallmatrix a_0 & b_0 \\ c_0 & d_0\endlx@physics@smallmatrix))

\displaystyle:=\bigg(a_{0}d_{0}\frac{-h}{a_{0}d_{0}-b_{0}c_{0}},a_{0}c_{0}\frac{-h}{a_{0}d_{0}-b_{0}c_{0}}\bigg).

Let

\displaystyle R_{p}(r,s,b;\mathbf{p})

\displaystyle:=\sum_{\tau=\smallmatrixquantity(\lx@physics@smallmatrix a_0 & b_0 \\ c_0 & d_0\endlx@physics@smallmatrix)\in\Gamma_{2}(P_{1},P_{2})\backslash\operatorname{SL}_{2}({\mathbb{Z}})}\mathbf{1}_{\Pi(\smallmatrixquantity(\lx@physics@smallmatrix a_0 & b_0 \\ c_0 & d_0\endlx@physics@smallmatrix))=(r,s)}V(\smallmatrixquantity(\lx@physics@smallmatrix a_0 & b_0 \\c_0 & d_0\endlx@physics@smallmatrix);\mathbf{p})V(\smallmatrixquantity(\lx@physics@smallmatrix a_0 & b_0 \\c_0 & d_0\endlx@physics@smallmatrix)\smallmatrixquantity(\lx@physics@smallmatrix\pm 1 & b \\ & \pm 1\endlx@physics@smallmatrix);\mathbf{p}).

Then for
\gcd(r(r+h)s,p)=1
we have

\displaystyle R_{p}(r,s,b;\mathbf{p})\equiv\mathbf{1}_{p=p_{7}}

and for
p|r(r+h)s
we have

\displaystyle R_{p}(r,s,b;\mathbf{p})\leq 1.

Proof.

Note that

\displaystyle\matrixquantity(\lx@physics@matrix a_0 & b_0 \\c_0 & d_0\endlx@physics@matrix)\matrixquantity(\lx@physics@matrix\pm 1 & b \\ & \pm 1\endlx@physics@matrix)=\matrixquantity(\lx@physics@matrix a_0 & b_0 +b a_0 \\c_0 & d_0 + b c_0\endlx@physics@matrix).

Therefore, for
\gcd(r(r+h)s,p)=1
we have
\gcd(a_{0}b_{0}c_{0}d_{0},p)=1
and the claim follows from the fact that the map

\displaystyle\{\smallmatrixquantity(\lx@physics@smallmatrix a_0 & b_0 \\ c_0 & d_0\endlx@physics@smallmatrix)\in\Gamma_{2}(P_{1},P_{2})\backslash\operatorname{SL}_{2}({\mathbb{Z}}):\gcd(a_{0}b_{0}c_{0}d_{0},p)=1\}

\displaystyle\to\{(r,s)\in{\mathbb{Z}}/P_{1}{\mathbb{Z}}\times{\mathbb{Z}}/P_{2}{\mathbb{Z}}:\,(r(r+h)s,p)=1\}

\displaystyle\smallmatrixquantity(\lx@physics@smallmatrix a_0 & b_0 \\ c_0 & d_0\endlx@physics@smallmatrix)

\displaystyle\mapsto\bigg(a_{0}d_{0}\frac{-h}{ad-bc},a_{0}c_{0}\frac{-h}{ad-bc}\bigg)

is a bijection. Indeed, clearly this map is surjective (consider
